\Gamma(e^{i\mathcal{O}})=e^{i\lambda(\mathcal{O})}=\sum_{N\geq 0}e^{i\lambda_{N}(\mathcal{O})}=\sum_{N\geq 0}\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{i^{n}}{n!}\lambda_{N}(\mathcal{O})^{n}=\sum_{N\geq 0}(e^{i\mathcal{O}})^{\otimes N}

(7.9)

From the integrated Poisson quantized operators, we can recover the noncommutative Poisson moment generating formula:

\varphi_{\omega}(\Gamma(e^{it\mathcal{O}}))=\sum_{N\geq 0}\frac{e^{-\omega(1)}}{N!}\omega^{\otimes N}((e^{it\mathcal{O}})^{\otimes N})=\sum_{N\geq 0}\frac{e^{-\omega(1)\omega(e^{it\mathcal{O}})^{N}}}{N!}=\exp(\omega(e^{it\mathcal{O}} - 1))

(7.10)

where
\mathcal{O}^{\dagger}=\mathcal{O}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
is a parameter. Recall that for a classical Poisson random variable
X
with intensity
\mu>0
, the classical Poisson moment generating function is given by:

\mathbb{E}(e^{itX})=\sum_{N\geq 0}\frac{e^{-\mu}\mu^{N}e^{itN}}{N!}=\exp(\mu(e^{it} - 1))

(7.11)

The domain of the Poisson state can be extended to include certain affiliated operators to the Poisson algebra. In particular, the following expressions are well-defined:

\varphi_{\omega}\left(\lambda(x_{1})...\lambda(x_{n})\right)

(7.12)

We will provide formulae to calculate these expressions shortly. These expressions are the noncommutative generalizations of the classical moments. Hence we will call these expressions the
Poisson moments
. When
\omega
is finite (as is the case we focus on in this section), we can define Poisson moments for any product of Poisson quantized operators. When
\omega
is not finite, the Poisson moments are only defined for products of Poisson quantized operators
\lambda(\mathcal{O})
’s where the operators

\mathcal{O}
’s are in the Tomita algebra of
(\mathcal{N},\omega)

T
.

Theorem 1
.

The Poisson moment formula calculates the
n
-point correlation function of Poisson quantized operators
\{\lambda(\mathcal{O}_{i})\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
in the Poisson state
\varphi_{\omega}
. The formula is given by a summation over all possible partitions of the set
[n]:=\{1,\cdots,n\}

\varphi_{\omega}(\lambda(\mathcal{O}_{1})\cdots\lambda(\mathcal{O}_{n})):=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\omega(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{i\in A}\mathcal{O}_{i})

(7.13)

where
\mathcal{P}_{n}
is the set of partition on
[n]
,
\sigma
is a particular partition,
A\in\sigma
is a subset which is a part of the partition
\sigma
, and the final product is an ordered product because the elements
\{\mathcal{O}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
does not generally commute.

Before presenting the derivation of the Poisson moment formula, we first write down some examples of the Poisson moment formula.

1.

For the simplest case
n=1
, the first moment is preserved:

\varphi_{\omega}(\lambda(\mathcal{O}))=\sum_{N\geq 0}\frac{e^{-\omega(1)}}{N!}\omega^{\otimes N}(\lambda_{N}(\mathcal{O}))=\sum_{N\geq 0}\frac{e^{-\omega(1)}}{N!}N\omega(1)^{N-1}\omega(\mathcal{O})=\omega(\mathcal{O})

(7.14)

2.

For
n=2
, the two-point Poisson moment formula contains two terms, because the set of partitions
\mathcal{P}(2)
contains two partitions:

\mathcal{P}(2)=\{\{1\}\{2\},\{1,2\}\}

(7.15)

Therefore, we have:

\varphi_{\omega}(\lambda(\mathcal{O}_{1})\lambda(\mathcal{O}_{2}))=\omega(\mathcal{O}_{1})\omega(\mathcal{O}_{2})+\omega(\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2})

(7.16)

3.

For
n=3
, the three-point Poisson moment formula contains 5 terms, because the set of partitions
\mathcal{P}(3)
contains 5 partitions:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{P}(3)&=\{\{1\}\{2\}\{3\},\{1,2\}\{3\},\{1,3\}\{2\},\{2,3\}\{1\},\{1,2,3\}\}\end{split}

(7.17)

Notice that the set partition is unordered. Therefore, we have:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\varphi_{\omega}(\lambda(\mathcal{O}_{1})\lambda(\mathcal{O}_{2})\lambda(\mathcal{O}_{3}))&=\omega(\mathcal{O}_{1})\omega(\mathcal{O}_{2})\omega(\mathcal{O}_{3})+\omega(\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2})\omega(\mathcal{O}_{3})+\omega(\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{3})\omega(\mathcal{O}_{2})\\
&+\omega(\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{3})\omega(\mathcal{O}_{1})+\omega(\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{3})\end{split}

(7.18)

Now we are ready to present the derivation of the Poisson moment formula. The derivation is simply done by a brute-force calculation.

Proof.

First we calculate the product
\lambda(\mathcal{O}_{1})\cdots\lambda(\mathcal{O}_{n})
. Recall
\lambda(\mathcal{O})=\sum_{N\geq 0}\lambda_{N}(\mathcal{O})
. To calculate this product, we only need to calculate the
N
-th degree component of this product:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lambda_{m}(\mathcal{O}_{1})\cdots\lambda_{m}(\mathcal{O}_{n})&=\sum_{1\leq i_{1},\cdots,i_{n}\leq m}\bigotimes_{1\leq k\leq m}\overrightarrow{\prod_{j\in[n]:i_{j}=k}}\mathcal{O}_{j}\\
&=\sum_{\vec{i}:[n]\rightarrow[m]}\bigotimes_{1\leq k\leq m}\overrightarrow{\prod_{j\in i^{-1}(k)}}\mathcal{O}_{j}\end{split}

(7.19)

where
\vec{i}(j)=i_{j}
is a set map from
[n]
to
[m]
. The product is an ordered product because the operators
\{\mathcal{O}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
do not generally commute. The set
i^{-1}(k)
is the preimage of
\{k\}
under the map
\vec{i}
. If this set is empty, the product is interpreted to be
1
. Using this formula, we can calculate the
m
-th degree component of the Poisson moment formula:

\omega^{\otimes m}(\lambda_{m}(\mathcal{O}_{1})\cdots\lambda_{m}(\mathcal{O}_{n}))=\sum_{\vec{i}:[n]\rightarrow[m]}\prod_{1\leq k\leq m}\omega(\overrightarrow{\prod_{j\in i^{-1}(k)}}\mathcal{O}_{j})

(7.20)

For each set map
\vec{i}
, the collection of non-empty subset
\{i^{-1}(k)\neq\emptyset:k\in[m]\}
forms a partition of
[n]

62
62

62

This is the induced partition induced by the map
\vec{i}
and the singleton partition of
[m]
:
\{\{1\}\{2\}\cdots\{m\}\}
.
. Different set maps can induce the same partition of
[n]
. For any given partition
\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
, the number of set maps that induce
\sigma
is given by:

|\sigma|!\binom{m}{|\sigma|}

(7.21)

where
|\sigma|
is the number of subsets in the partition
\sigma
and
|\sigma|\leq m

63
63

63

This combinatorial factor arises by simply counting the number of ways to assign a distinct number between
1
and
m
to each subset
A\in\sigma
. Different assignments define different set maps.
. Using this combinatorial observation, we can rewrite the
m
-th degree component of the Poisson moment formula:

\omega^{\otimes m}(\lambda_{m}(\mathcal{O}_{1})\cdots\lambda_{m}(\mathcal{O}_{n}))=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n},|\sigma|\leq m}|\sigma|!\binom{m}{|\sigma|}\omega(1)^{m-|\sigma|}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\omega(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{j\in A}\mathcal{O}_{j})

(7.22)

where the factor
\omega(1)^{m-|\sigma|}
comes from the empty sets
\{k\in[m]:i^{-1}(k)=\emptyset\}
. These empty sets exist because
|\sigma|\leq m
.

Now, applying the definition of Poisson state, we have:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\varphi_{\omega}(\lambda(\mathcal{O}_{1})\cdots\lambda(\mathcal{O}_{n}))&=\sum_{m\geq 0}\frac{e^{-\omega(1)}}{m!}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n},|\sigma|\leq m}|\sigma|!\binom{m}{|\sigma|}\omega(1)^{m-|\sigma|}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\omega(\overrightarrow{\prod_{j\in A}}\mathcal{O}_{j})\\
&=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\sum_{m\geq|\sigma|}\frac{e^{-\omega(1)}\omega(1)^{m-|\sigma|}}{(m-|\sigma|)!}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\omega(\overrightarrow{\prod_{j\in A}}\mathcal{O}_{j})\\
&=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\omega(\overrightarrow{\prod_{j\in A}}\mathcal{O}_{j})\qed\end{split}

(7.23)

Finally, we discuss the connected component of the Poisson moment formula, which is defined as the connected correlation function of Poisson quantized operators:

\varphi_{\omega}(\lambda(\mathcal{O}_{1})\cdots\lambda(\mathcal{O}_{n}))_{\text{conn}}:=(-i)^{n}\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial s_{1}\cdots\partial s_{n}}|_{s_{1},\cdots,s_{n}=0}\log\varphi_{\omega}(e^{is_{1}\lambda(\mathcal{O}_{1})}\cdots e^{is_{n}\lambda(\mathcal{O}_{n})})

(7.24)

To calculate the connected correlation function, we make a simple observation:

\displaystyle\begin{split}e^{is\lambda(\mathcal{O})}e^{it\lambda(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}})}&=\Gamma(e^{is\mathcal{O}})\Gamma(e^{it\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}})=\sum_{N\geq 0}(e^{is\mathcal{O}})^{\otimes N}\sum_{M\geq 0}(e^{it\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}})^{\otimes M}\\
&=\sum_{N\geq 0}(e^{is\mathcal{O}}e^{it\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}})^{\otimes N}=\Gamma(e^{it\mathcal{O}}e^{is\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}})\end{split}

(7.25)

where
\mathcal{O}^{\dagger}=\mathcal{O}\text{ , }\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{\dagger}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}
and
s,t\in\mathbb{R}

64
64

64

Recall that in this section
\omega
is finite. For
\omega
infinite, this formula needs to be used with care because the domains of the Poisson quantization map and the integrated Poisson quantization map are no longer the entire input von Neumann algebra.
.
Using these observations, we can calculate the connected component of the Poisson moment formula:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\varphi_{\omega}(\lambda(\mathcal{O}_{1})\cdots\lambda(\mathcal{O}_{n}))_{\text{conn}}&=(-i)^{n}\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial s_{1}\cdots\partial s_{n}}|_{s_{1},\cdots,s_{n}=0}\log\varphi_{\omega}(e^{is_{1}\lambda(\mathcal{O}_{1})}\cdots e^{is_{n}\lambda(\mathcal{O}_{n})})\\
&=(-i)^{n}\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial s_{1}\cdots\partial s_{n}}|_{s_{1},\cdots,s_{n}=0}\log\varphi_{\omega}(\Gamma(e^{is_{1}\mathcal{O}_{1}}\cdots e^{is_{n}\mathcal{O}_{n}}))\\
&=(-i)^{n}\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial s_{1}\cdots\partial s_{n}}|_{s_{1},\cdots,s_{n}=0}\log\exp(\omega(e^{is_1\mathcal{O}_1}\cdots e^{is_n\mathcal{O}_n} - 1))\\
&=(-i)^{n}\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial s_{1}\cdots\partial s_{n}}|_{s_{1},\cdots,s_{n}=0}\omega(e^{is_{1}\mathcal{O}_{1}}\cdots e^{is_{n}\mathcal{O}_{n}}-1)\\
&=\omega(\mathcal{O}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{O}_{n})\end{split}

(7.26)

This shows that
Poissonization preserves the connected correlation functions
.

7.2
Poissonization as noncommutative Bernoulli approximation

In this section, we present an alternative construction of Poissonization from the perspective of noncommutative probability theory. As the name suggests, Poissonization can be understood as a noncommutative generalization of the classical Poisson random measure. The simplest case of a Poisson random measure is a Poisson random variable (where the underlying space is a singleton). A classical Poisson random variable can be constructed as the limit of a sequence of sums of Bernoulli random variables. This is the well-known
law of rare events
.

To generalize this approximation to the noncommutative setting, two ingredients need to be modified:

1.

The classical Bernoulli random variables need to be generalized to the noncommutative setting. Poissonization replaces these random variables with Poisson quantized operators.

2.

The notion of limit needs to be modified so as to construct a well-defined Poisson algebra and Poisson state. Poissonization replaces the usual limit with the notion of an ultraproduct.

Although the notion of an ultraproduct seems like a formidable mathematical tool, physicists have long been using this notion implicitly. More precisely, the algebra generated by master fields in the large
N
limit of a matrix theory is an ultraproduct of matrix algebras
statmech
;
singer
;
voiculescu1
;
VND
;
accardi1
;
Speicher:1993kt
. For example, for
SU(N)
-gauge theory on 2D Riemann surfaces, the algebra generated by the master fields in the large
N
limit is a (non-hyperfinite) type
II_{1}
factor, which is an ultraproduct of finite-dimensional matrix algebras
singer
.

Poissonization is
not
free probability. But the same framework of noncommutative probability theory provides the right tools to construct Poissonization. The key observation of large
N
theory is that the large
N
limits of the correlation functions completely specify the theory. Mathematically, this observation can be made precise by a noncommutative Hamburger moment theorem
MAW
. For Poissonization, the key is the Poisson moment formula. In this section, we use noncommutative Bernoulli approximation to construct a sequence of algebras, weights and correlation functions whose limit is the Poisson moment formula. The limiting operators will be analogs of large
N
master fields (these are the Poisson quantized operators), the limiting algebra will be the Poisson algebra, and the limiting weight will be the Poisson weight.

Certain parts of this section can be technical, but we have tried to make the material accessible. We first recall the classical Bernoulli approximation and the law of rare events. For completeness, we have included a discussion of the algebraic formulation of classical probability theory (i.e., the commutative von Neumann algebras) in Appendix
F.1
. We then present the large
N
limit as an ultraproduct, and apply our discussion to the classical Bernoulli approximations and construct the classical Poisson random variable as an ultraproduct of sums of Bernoulli random variables. Finally, we generalize the construction to the noncommutative case and observe that Poissonization can be understood as the large
N
limit of particular sparse Bernoulli random matrices.

7.2.1
Classical Bernoulli Approximation to Poisson Distribution

First, recall the classical Bernoulli approximation to the Poisson distribution. We already discussed this in Subsection
2.1
with a physics example; however, we repeat it here using mathematical terminology to motivate ultralimits. Suppose there exists a rare event that happens with probability
\mu\Delta T
where
\Delta T
is the length of the time interval. In other words, in each unit interval of time, this rare event happens with probability
\mu
. We would like to know what the probability is of
k
such events happening during a unit time interval. One way to approach this problem is to coarse-grain the time interval into
M
equi-length subintervals:

[0,1]=[0,\frac{1}{M}]\sqcup[\frac{1}{M},\frac{2}{M}]\sqcup\cdots\sqcup[\frac{M-1}{M},1]

(7.27)

For large enough
M
, each time interval has negligible length. Therefore, the probability of a rare event happening more than once within a subinterval is dampened by a
\frac{1}{M}
. Hence, we can assume that the number of events within each subinterval is at most once. Then the total number of events within the unit time interval can be approximated by a summation:

X_{M}=\sum_{1\leq i\leq M}X^{(i)}

(7.28)

where
X^{(i)}
is the number of events within the
i
-th subinterval. By construction
X^{(i)}
is a Bernoulli random variable:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&X^{(i)}=0\text{ with probability }(1-\frac{\mu}{M})\\
&X^{(i)}=1\text{ with probability }\frac{\mu}{M}\end{split}

(7.29)

Then the probability distribution of the random variable
X_{M}
is a convolution of
M
Bernoulli random variables:

X_{M}\sim(\frac{\mu}{M}\delta_{1}+(1-\frac{\mu}{M})\delta_{0})^{*M}

(7.30)

where
*M
denotes the
M
-fold convolution of Bernoulli random variables. Concretely, we have:

\text{Prob}(X_{M}=k)=\binom{M}{k}(\frac{\mu}{M})^{k}(1-\frac{\mu}{M})^{M-k}

When the coarse graining becomes finer and finer (i.e.
M\rightarrow\infty
), we have the limiting distribution (the
law of rare events
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\text{Prob}(X_{M}=k)&=\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\frac{(1-\frac{\mu}{M})^{M}\mu^{k}}{k!}\frac{M!}{(M-k)!M^{k}(1-\frac{mu}{M})^{k}}\\
&=\frac{e^{-\mu}\mu^{k}}{k!}\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\prod_{0\leq i\leq k-1}\frac{M-i}{M-\mu}=\frac{e^{-\mu}\mu^{k}}{k!}\end{split}

(7.31)

The resulting probability distribution is the classical Poisson distribution with intensity
\mu
.

7.2.2
Large
N
Limit as ultraproduct

In this subsection, we introduce the mathematical tool of ultraproduct. Physicists have been using this tool implicitly when constructing master fields to study the large
N
limits of matrix models
Gopakumar-Gross
.

In matrix models, we are given a sequence of finite
N
matrix algebras and finite
N
correlation functions. The large
N
limit of the correlation functions can often be calculated. From these limiting correlation functions, we would like to construct master fields and a state on the algebra generated by the master fields so that limiting correlation functions can be reproduced.

For example consider the non-interacting matrix model with the partition function:

Z_{N}:=\int_{\text{Herm}_{N}}\prod_{1\leq i\leq k}dX_{i}e^{-\frac{N}{2}\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}\tr(X_i^2)}

(7.32)

where
X_{i}^{\dagger}=X_{i}
are Hermitian
65
65

65

In other words,
X_{i}
’s are mutually independent Hermitian Gaussian random matrices.
. The finite
N
correlation functions are:

\langle\frac{\tr}{N}(f(X_{1},\cdots,X_{k}))\rangle:=\frac{1}{Z_{N}}\int_{\text{Herm}_{N}}\prod_{1\leq i\leq k}dX_{i}e^{-\frac{N}{2}\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}\tr(X_i^2)}\frac{\tr}{N}(f(X_{1},\cdots,X_{k}))

(7.33)

where
\tr
is the un-normalized trace on the
N
-by-
N
matrices and
f(X_{1},\cdots,X_{k})
is a noncommutative monomial (or simply a word) formed from products of the matrices
X_{i}
.

When
k=1
(one-matrix model), the large
N
limit of the correlation functions can be calculated from the Wigner semi-circular law
mehta2004random
. When
k>1
, the key observation is the following factorization (free independence)
voiculescu1
:

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\langle\frac{\tr}{N}(X_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}X_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}\cdots X_{k}^{\alpha_{k}})\rangle=\prod_{1\leq i\leq k}\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\langle\frac{\tr}{N}(X_{i}^{\alpha_{i}})\rangle

(7.34)

where
\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
and the product is ordered so that
X_{i}\neq X_{j}
. More general correlation functions can be calculated from this factorization rule.

In this case, the master fields
\widehat{X_{i}}
corresponding to
X_{i}
’s are given by semi-circular random variables that are mutually freely independent
voiculescu1
. The algebra generated by these master fields is isomorphic to the free group factor with
k
generators. And using the canonical trace on the free group factor, we have:

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\langle\frac{\tr}{N}f(X_{1},\cdots,X_{k})\rangle:=\tau(f(\widehat{X}_{1},\cdots,\widehat{X}_{k}))

(7.35)

where
\widehat{X}_{i}
is the master field corresponding to
X_{i}
and
\tau
is the canonical trace on the free group factor. Here
f
is a noncommutative monomial.

Mathematically the free group factor can be constructed using an ultraproduct of matrix algebras
Speicher1992
;
Speicher:1993kt
. The key idea is to represent master fields as sequences of finite
N
matrices and to construct the limiting algebra via a GNS-type construction. The limiting state is well-defined, and it relies on a well-defined limit of finite
N
moment formulae. In the next section, we apply this procedure to recast the classical Bernoulli approximation as an ultraproduct.

Bernoulli approximation as ultraproduct

In this section, we recast the classical Bernoulli approximation as a particular example of an ultraproduct. The commutative von Neumann algebra generated by the limiting Poisson random variable is the ultraproduct algebra. Recall that the Poisson random variable can be approximated by a sequence of sums of i.i.d. Bernoulli random variables. Based on the algebraic characterization of random variables
F.1
, a Bernoulli distribution defines a state
\omega_{p}
on the commutative von Neumann algebra
\ell_{\infty}^{2}
where
\omega_{p}(\alpha_{0}\delta_{0}+\alpha_{1}\delta_{1})=\alpha_{0}(1-p)+\alpha_{1}p
(
\alpha_{i}
are constants for
i=0,1
). In addition, a sum of
M
i.i.d. Bernoulli random variables
X_{M}=\sum_{1\leq i\leq M}X^{(i)}
defines a tensor product state
\omega_{p}^{\otimes k}
on the symmetric tensor algebra
\otimes_{\text{sym}}^{M}\ell_{\infty}^{2}
. Moreover, the distribution of
X_{M}
is reproduced by the tensor product state and the spectral projections of the operator:

\widetilde{\lambda}_{M}(\delta_{1})=\sum_{1\leq i\leq M}1\otimes\cdots\underbrace{\otimes\delta_{1}\otimes}_{\text{i-th position}}\cdots\otimes 1

(7.36)

The discussion above indicates that we should consider the following sequence of algebras and states:

\{(\otimes_{\text{sym}}^{M}\ell_{\infty}^{2},\omega_{p_{M}}^{\otimes M})\}_{M\geq[\lambda]+1}

(7.37)

where for each
M
the success probability
p_{M}=\frac{\mu}{M}
. The indices
M
are required to be larger than
[\mu]+1
so that
p_{M}
is less than 1.

As mentioned in our discussion of large
N
limits, the ultraproduct construction represents the master field as a sequence of (uniformly bounded) operators. In our current application, we can be more explicit and consider the von Neumann algebra of (uniformly bounded) sequences:

\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{N},\otimes_{\text{sym}}^{M}\ell_{\infty}^{2}):=\{(\mathcal{O}_{M}):\mathcal{O}_{M}\in\otimes_{\text{sym}}^{M}\ell_{\infty}^{2}\text{ for }M\geq[\mu]+1\text{, and }0\text{ otherwise}\}

(7.38)

Heuristically, the limiting state
\omega
is defined by the limit “
\omega((\mathcal{O}_{M})):=\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\omega_{p_{M}}^{\otimes M}(\mathcal{O}_{M})
” where
\mathcal{O}_{M}\in\otimes_{\text{sym}}^{M}\ell_{\infty}^{2}
. The quotation mark emphasizes the fact that
not all
bounded sequences have a well-defined limit in the standard sense
66
66

66

This is where an ultrafilter/ultraproduct comes to the rescue. The crucial fact is that given any (non-principal) ultrafilter, any bounded sequence has a well-defined limit. And for convergent sequences, this limit reduces to the standard notion of limit.
. Then, following the GNS construction, we quotient out the null ideal:

\mathcal{I}_{w}:=\{(\mathcal{O}_{M})\in\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{N},\otimes_{\text{sym}}^{M}\ell_{\infty}^{2}):\lim_{M\rightarrow w}\omega_{p_{M}}^{\otimes M}(\mathcal{O}_{M}^{\dagger}\mathcal{O}_{M})=0\}

(7.39)

where
w
is a non-principal ultrafilter (see also Appendix
F.2
).
The resulting quotient algebra is
the ultraproduct algebra
:

\prod^{w}(\otimes_{\text{sym}}^{M}\ell_{\infty}^{2}):=\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{N},\otimes_{\text{sym}}^{M}\ell_{\infty}^{2})/I_{w}

(7.40)

We do not need the entire ultraproduct algebra to reproduce the Poisson random variable. We only need the sub-algebra generated by a particular sequence:

\widetilde{\lambda}(\delta_{1}):=[(\widetilde{\lambda}_{M}(\delta_{1}))]

(7.41)

where the square bracket emphasizes that the operator
\widetilde{\lambda}(\delta_{1})
exists in the quotient algebra. Notice that the expectation of
\widetilde{\lambda}(\delta_{1})
can be readily calculated:

\omega(\widetilde{\lambda}(\delta_{1}))=\lim_{M}\omega_{p_{M}}^{\otimes M}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{M}(\delta_{1}))=\lim_{M}M\omega_{p_{M}}(1)^{M-1}\omega_{p_{M}}(\delta_{1})=\lim_{M}Mp_{M}=\mu

(7.42)

To study the spectrum of
\widetilde{\lambda}(\delta_{1})
, we decompose each component
\lambda_{M}(\delta_{1})
:

(\widetilde{\lambda}_{M}(\delta_{1}))_{M\geq[\mu]+1}=(\sum_{0\leq k\leq M}kp^{M}_{k})_{M\geq[\mu]+1}

(7.43)

Hence for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have a sequence of projections
(p^{M}_{k})_{M\geq[\mu]+1}
where for
M<k
define
p^{M}_{k}=0
. We can calculate the ultraproduct of this sequence of projections:

\lim_{M}\omega_{p_{M}}^{\otimes M}(p^{M}_{k})=\lim_{M}\binom{M}{k}(\frac{\mu}{M})^{k}(1-\frac{\mu}{M})^{M-k}=\frac{e^{-\mu}\mu^{k}}{k!}

(7.44)

where the ultraproduct in the second equation reduces to the usual limit because the sequence converges. This is nothing but the classical Bernoulli approximation formula. The sequence of projections
(p^{M}_{k})_{M\geq[\mu]+1}
gives a well-defined projection
\widetilde{\lambda}(p_{k})
in the ultraproduct algebra such that:

\omega(\widetilde{\lambda}(p_{k}))=\lim_{M}\omega_{p_{M}}^{\otimes M}(p_{k}^{M})=\frac{e^{-\mu}\mu^{k}}{k!}

(7.45)

Therefore, we have the spectral decomposition of
\widetilde{\lambda}(\delta_{1})
:

\widetilde{\lambda}(\delta_{1})=\sum_{k\geq 0}k\widetilde{\lambda}(p_{k})

(7.46)

And the von Neumann subalgebra generated by
\widetilde{\lambda}(\delta_{1})
is the algebra of bounded sequences on
\mathbb{N}
, and the state
\omega
restricted to this subalgebra reduces to the Poisson distribution. The isomorphism is given by

\varphi:\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{N})\rightarrow\langle\widetilde{\lambda}(\delta_{1})\rangle:\sum_{k\geq 0}\alpha_{k}\delta_{k}\mapsto\sum_{k\geq 0}\alpha_{k}\widetilde{\lambda}(p_{k})

(7.47)

where
(\alpha_{k})_{k\geq 0}
is a bounded sequence of complex numbers. The limiting state
\omega
reduces to:

\omega\circ\varphi(\sum_{k\geq 0}\alpha_{k}\delta_{k})=\sum_{k\geq 0}\alpha_{k}\omega(\widetilde{\lambda}(p_{k}))=\sum_{k\geq 0}\alpha_{k}\frac{e^{-\lambda}\lambda^{k}}{k!}

This concludes the ultraproduct modeling of Bernoulli approximation.

7.2.3
Noncommutative Bernoulli approximation to Poissonization

The classical Bernoulli approximation is the simplest example of Poissonization. The resulting Poisson algebra is a commutative von Neumann algebra generated by a single Poisson quantized operator
\widetilde{\lambda}(\delta_{1})
, and the Poisson state is the Poisson distribution. Rephrasing this construction in a slightly more concise and abstract way, the input data in the Bernoulli approximation is the trivial von Neumann algebra
\mathbb{C}
and the input weight is the linear functional of multiplication by a positive constant
\mu>0
:

\omega_{\mu}:\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}:z\mapsto\mu z

(7.48)

In the ultraproduct construction (as opposed to the symmetric tensor algebra construction), the Poisson quantization map is given by a sequence of linear maps:

\widetilde{\lambda}_{M}:\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\otimes_{\text{sym}}^{M}\ell_{\infty}^{2}:\delta_{1}\mapsto\sum_{1\leq i\leq M}1\otimes\cdots\underbrace{\otimes\delta_{1}\otimes}_{\text{i-th position}}\cdots\otimes 1

(7.49)

where we have regarded
\mathbb{C}
as the von Neumann algebra of measurable functions on the one-point space
\{1\}
and
\delta_{1}
generates this trivial algebra. The full Poisson quantization map is defined to be:

\widetilde{\lambda}:\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{N},\otimes_{\text{sym}}^{M}\ell_{\infty}^{2})/I_{w}:\delta_{1}\mapsto[(\widetilde{\lambda}_{M}(\delta_{1}))]

(7.50)

where again the square bracket emphasizes that the operator exists in the quotient algebra.

The Poisson algebra is the subalgebra generated by
\widetilde{\lambda}(\delta_{1})
in the ultraproduct algebra and the Poisson state is the limiting state
\varphi_{\omega_{\mu}}:=\lim_{M}\omega_{p_{M}}^{\otimes M}
restricted to this subalgebra.

The generalization to more general von Neumann algebras is straightforward. Here we restrict ourselves to consider tracial von Neumann algebras as inputs
67
67

67

The reason is that the ultraproduct definition needs to be modified for non-tracial weights. This involves introducing additional (but standard) machineries.
. Instead of
(\mathbb{C},\omega_{\mu})
, the input data is
(\mathcal{N},\tau)
where
\tau
is a tracial finite weight (i.e.
\tau(1)<\infty
). The Poisson quantization map again is a sequence of linear maps:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\lambda}_{M}:\mathcal{N}\rightarrow\otimes_{\text{sym}}^{M}(\mathcal{N}\otimes\ell_{\infty}^{2}):\mathcal{O}\mapsto

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i\leq M}1\otimes\cdots\underbrace{\otimes(\mathcal{O}\otimes\delta_{1})\otimes}_{\text{i-th position}}\cdots\otimes 1

(7.51)

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq i\leq M}1\otimes\cdots\underbrace{\otimes\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{O}&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}\otimes}_{\text{i-th position}}\cdots\otimes 1

where
M\geq[\tau(1)]+1
, and the symmetric tensor product is taken over the tensor algebra
\mathcal{N}\otimes\ell_{\infty}^{2}\cong\mathcal{N}\oplus\mathcal{N}
. In terms of the matrix notation,
\mathcal{N}\otimes\ell_{\infty}^{2}
is the set of diagonal
2\times 2
matrices where each matrix entry is an operator in
\mathcal{N}
. The
M
-th state on the algebra
\mathcal{N}\otimes\ell_{\infty}^{2}
is given by the tensor product of the state
\tau_{N}:=\frac{\tau}{\tau(1)}\otimes\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\tau(1)}{N}&0\\
0&1-\frac{\tau(1)}{N}\end{bmatrix}
. We consider the ultraproduct

\prod^{w}\big(\otimes_{\text{sym}}^{M}(\mathcal{N}\otimes\ell_{\infty}^{2}),\tau_{M}^{\otimes M}\big)

(7.52)

where
w
is a non-principal ultrafilter. The Poisson quantization map is given by:

\widetilde{\lambda}:\mathcal{N}\rightarrow\prod^{w}\big(\otimes_{\text{sym}}^{M}(\mathcal{N}\otimes\ell_{\infty}^{2}),\tau_{M}^{\otimes M}\big):\mathcal{O}\mapsto[(\widetilde{\lambda}_{M}(\mathcal{O}))]

(7.53)

where again the square bracket emphasizes that the operator exists in the quotient. The Poisson algebra is the subalgebra generated by:

\mathbb{P}_{\tau}\mathcal{N}:=\langle\widetilde{\lambda}(\mathcal{O}):\mathcal{O}^{\dagger}=\mathcal{O}\in\mathcal{N}\rangle\subset\prod^{w}(\otimes_{\text{sym}}^{M}(\mathcal{N}\otimes\ell_{\infty}^{2}),\tau_{M}^{\otimes M})

(7.54)

The Poisson state
\varphi_{\tau}
is the restriction of the limiting state
\lim_{M\rightarrow w}\tau_{M}^{\otimes M}
to the Poisson algebra
68
68

68

Technically, the Poisson quantized operator
\widetilde{\lambda}(\mathcal{O})
is generally
not
bounded operators in the Poisson algebra but
affiliated operators
with the Poisson algebra. Affiliated operators commute with the commutant of the Poisson algebra. But they are not necessarily bounded. For example, in the case of Bernoulli approximation,
\widetilde{\lambda}(\delta_{1})
has spectrum
\mathbb{N}
and is not bounded. Nevertheless, by Stone’s theorem, essentially self-adjoint affiliated operators generate a one-parameter family of unitary operators that exist inside the von Neumann algebra. This is what it means for the Poisson quantized operators to generate the Poisson algebra.
.

By definition of
\widetilde{\lambda}_{M}
, it is not difficult to see that
\widetilde{\lambda}_{M}
preserves commutation relation:

\widetilde{\lambda}_{M}([\mathcal{O},\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}])=[\widetilde{\lambda}_{M}(\mathcal{O}),\widetilde{\lambda}_{M}(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}})]

(7.55)

Hence, the Poisson quantization map also preserves commutation relations.

In addition, we can readily calculate the expectation of the Poisson quantized operator under the Poisson state:

\varphi_{\tau}(\widetilde{\lambda}(\mathcal{O}))=\lim_{M}\tau_{M}^{\otimes M}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{M}(\mathcal{O}))=\lim_{M}M\tau_{M}(1)^{M-1}\tau_{M}(\mathcal{O}\otimes\delta_{1})=\lim_{M}M\frac{\tau(\mathcal{O})}{\tau(1)}\frac{\tau(1)}{M}=\tau(\mathcal{O})

(7.56)

where we have used the fact that
\tau_{M}(1)=(\frac{\tau(1)}{M}+(1-\frac{\tau(1)}{M}))\frac{\tau(1)}{\tau(1)}=1
.

We can also derive the Poisson moment formula. Notice that by the same argument of the proof of Equation
7.20
, we have:

\tau_{M}^{\otimes M}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{M}(\mathcal{O}_{1})\cdots\widetilde{\lambda}_{M}(\mathcal{O}_{n}))=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n},|\sigma|\leq M}|\sigma|!\binom{M}{|\sigma|}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\tau_{M}(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{j\in A}(\mathcal{O}_{j}\otimes\delta_{1}))

(7.57)

Because
\delta_{1}
is a projection, we have
\overrightarrow{\prod}_{j\in A}(\mathcal{O}_{j}\otimes\delta_{1})=(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{j\in A}\mathcal{O}_{j})\otimes\delta_{1}
. Hence we have
\tau_{M}(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{j\in A}(\mathcal{O}_{j}\otimes\delta_{1}))=\frac{\tau(1)}{M}\frac{\tau(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{j\in A}x_{j})}{\tau(1)}
. And hence, we have:

\tau_{M}^{\otimes M}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{M}(\mathcal{O}_{1})\cdots\widetilde{\lambda}_{M}(\mathcal{O}_{n}))=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n},|\sigma|\leq M}|\sigma|!\binom{M}{|\sigma|}\frac{1}{M^{|\sigma|}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\tau(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{j\in A}\mathcal{O}_{j})

(7.58)

where
|\sigma|
is the number of subsets in the partition
\sigma
. Observe that the standard limit exists for the leading combinatorial factor:

\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}|\sigma|!\binom{M}{|\sigma|}\frac{1}{M^{|\sigma|}}=\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\frac{M(M-1)\cdots(M-|\sigma|+1)}{M^{|\sigma|}}=1

(7.59)

Taking the limit, we have the Poisson moment formula:

\varphi_{\tau}(\widetilde{\lambda}(\mathcal{O}_{1})\cdots\widetilde{\lambda}(\mathcal{O}_{n}))=\lim_{M}\tau_{M}^{\otimes M}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{M}(\mathcal{O}_{1})\cdots\widetilde{\lambda}_{M}(\mathcal{O}_{n}))=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\tau(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{j\in A}\mathcal{O}_{j})

(7.60)

We end this section with two comments:

1.

As emphasized, an ultraproduct of von Neumann algebras is typically not hyperfinite. However, the Poisson algebra as a proper subalgebra of an ultraproduct is hyperfinite if the input von Neumann algebra is hyperfinite. This is not easy to see in the ultraproduct construction of Poissonization, but it is easy to see in the symmetric tensor product construction of Poissonization. The missing link is to show that both constructions result in the same Poisson algebra and the same Poisson state. This is achieved by a moment-growth estimation
69
69

69

For physicists, such moment-growth estimations have shown up in the study of quantum chaos and specifically in the study of Universal Operator Growth Hypothesis
PCASA
.
and a noncommutative Hamburger moment theorem
MAW
(see also Appendix
F.3
).

2.

We have chosen to present Bernoulli approximation using the algebraic formulation of random variables to emphasize the mathematical structure of this construction and to demonstrate that the noncommutative generalization is natural and straightforward. Now that we are familiar with the noncommutative Bernoulli approximation, we can restate the construction in the more traditional language of random matrices. We devote the final part of this section to this reformulation.

In the following, we restrict our attention to the case where the input von Neumann algebra is a matrix algebra
M_{k}(\mathbb{C})
and the input state is the normalized trace
\frac{\tr}{k}
. First we redefine the map
\widetilde{\lambda}_{M}
as:

\widetilde{\lambda}_{M}(\mathcal{O})=\sum_{1\leq i\leq M}1\otimes\cdots\underbrace{\otimes(\mathcal{O}X^{(i)}_{M})\otimes}_{\text{i-th position}}\cdots\otimes 1

(7.61)

where
\mathcal{O}\in M_{k}(\mathbb{C})
is a matrix and
X_{M}^{(i)}
is a classical Bernoulli random variable with success probability
\frac{1}{M}
. Further,
X_{M}^{(i)}
and
X_{M}^{(i^{\prime})}
are mutually independent. Since
\mathcal{O}
is a matrix,
\widetilde{\lambda}_{M}(\mathcal{O})
is a sum of
M
Bernoulli random matrices, where each summand
1\otimes\cdots\underbrace{\otimes(\mathcal{O}X^{(i)}_{M})\otimes}_{\text{i-th position}}\cdots\otimes 1
is non-zero with probability
\frac{\tau(1)}{M}
. Notice that because of the tensor product structure, each summand
1\otimes\cdots\underbrace{\otimes(\mathcal{O}X^{(i)}_{M})\otimes}_{\text{i-th position}}\cdots\otimes 1
has at least half of its entries equal to
0
. Therefore,
\widetilde{\lambda}_{M}(\mathcal{O})
is a sum of sparse random matrices.

The limiting state is the familiar state in matrix models - namely, the expectation value of the normalized trace:

\lim_{M}\mathbb{E}_{M}\frac{\tr}{k^{M}}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{M}(\mathcal{O}_{1})\cdots\widetilde{\lambda}_{M}(\mathcal{O}_{n}))

(7.62)

where
k^{M}
is the dimension of the random matrix
\widetilde{\lambda}_{M}(\mathcal{O})
. Here, the expectation value is taken over all independent Bernoulli random variables. Notice that the normalized trace can be decomposed into a tensor product of normalized traces on
k\times k
-matrices:

\frac{\tr}{k^{M}}=(\frac{\tr}{k})^{\otimes M}

(7.63)

where
\tr
is the unnormalized trace and we have used the fact that
M_{k^{M}}(\mathbb{C})=\otimes^{M}M_{k}(\mathbb{C})
. Using these observations, we can calculate the finite
N
moment formula. Notice that the combinatorics in the calculation is the same combinatorics as before (see also
7.20
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{M}\frac{\tr}{k^{M}}(\widetilde{\lambda}(\mathcal{O}_{1})\cdots\widetilde{\lambda}(\mathcal{O}_{n}))&=\sum_{\vec{i}:[n]\rightarrow[M]}\prod_{1\leq\alpha\leq M}\mathbb{E}\frac{\tr}{k}(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{j\in i^{-1}(\alpha)}(\mathcal{O}_{j}X_{M}^{(\alpha)}))\\
&=\sum_{\vec{i}:[n]\rightarrow[M]}\prod_{1\leq\alpha\leq M}\mathbb{E}\big((X_{M}^{(\alpha)})^{|i^{-1}(\alpha)|}\big)\frac{\tr}{k}(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{j\in i^{-1}(\alpha)}\mathcal{O}_{j})\end{split}

(7.64)

Because powers of a Bernoulli random variable with value in
\{0,1\}
is the same random variable, we have:

\mathbb{E}\big((X_{M}^{(\alpha)})^{i^{-1}(\alpha)}\big)=\mathbb{E}(X_{M}^{(\alpha)})=\frac{1}{M}

(7.65)

Hence, the finite
M
moment formula can be written as:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{M}\frac{\tr}{k^{M}}(\widetilde{\lambda}(x_{1})\cdots\widetilde{\lambda}(x_{n}))&=\sum_{\vec{i}:[n]\rightarrow[M]}\prod_{1\leq\alpha\leq M}\frac{1}{M}\frac{\tr}{k}(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{j\in i^{-1}(\alpha)}\mathcal{O}_{j})\\
&=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n},|\sigma|\leq M}|\sigma|!\binom{M}{|\sigma|}\frac{1}{M^{|\sigma|}}\prod_{S\in\sigma}\frac{\tr}{k}(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{j\in S}\mathcal{O}_{j})\end{split}

(7.66)

Therefore in the large
M
limit, we have the Poisson moment formula:

\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}_{M}\frac{\tr}{k^{M}}(\widetilde{\lambda}(\mathcal{O}_{1})\cdots\widetilde{\lambda}(\mathcal{O}_{n}))=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\prod_{S\in\sigma}\frac{\tr}{k}(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{j\in S}\mathcal{O}_{j})

(7.67)

In short,
Poissonization of matrix algebra is the large
M
limit of particular sparse Bernoulli random matrices
.

7.3
Properties of Poissonization

In this section, we mention three key properties of Poissonization:

1.

The GNS Hilbert space of the Poisson state is a symmetric Fock space. The Poisson algebra represented on this symmetric Fock space is in its standard form. In particular, the commutant of the Poisson algebra is another Poisson algebra.

2.

Poissonization lifts unital CP maps on the one-particle algebra of observables to unital CP maps on the Poisson algebra.

3.

Modular automorphism associated with the input weight is lifted to a modular automorphism of the Poisson state.

7.3.1
Standard representation of Poisson algebra

Theorem 2
.

The GNS Hilbert space of the Poisson algebra
\mathbb{P}_{\omega}\mathcal{N}
under the Poisson state
\varphi_{\omega}
is isomorphic to the symmetric Fock space
\mathcal{F}_{\text{sym}}(L_{2}(\mathcal{N},\omega))
where
L_{2}(\mathcal{N},\omega)
is the GNS Hilbert space of
N
under the (finite) weight
\omega
.

We construct a basis for the GNS Hilbert space explicitly using an inductive procedure
70
70

70

This inductive construction is similar to the classical moment-to-cumulant transformation.
. Throughout the construction, all operators
\mathcal{O}
are assumed to have zero singleton expectation value:
\omega(\mathcal{O})=0

71
71

71

When
\omega
is finite, this is not restrictive since we can always subtract the expectation value from the operator, i.e.
\mathcal{O}\mapsto\mathcal{O}-\omega(\mathcal{O})\mathbbm{1}
.
. Consider the following recursive formula:

\lambda_{\emptyset}(\mathcal{O}_{1},...,\mathcal{O}_{n}):=\lambda(\mathcal{O}_{1})\lambda_{\emptyset}(\mathcal{O}_{2},...,\mathcal{O}_{n})-\sum_{2\leq i\leq n}\lambda_{\emptyset}(\mathcal{O}_{2},...,\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{i},...,\mathcal{O}_{n})

(7.68)

For the base case
n=1
, we define:

\lambda_{\emptyset}(\mathcal{O}):=\lambda(\mathcal{O})

(7.69)

Using the definition of
\lambda(\mathcal{O})=\sum_{N\geq 0}\sum_{1\leq j\leq N}1\otimes...\underbrace{\otimes\mathcal{O}\otimes}_{\text{the j-th position}}...\otimes 1
, the following formula for
\lambda_{\emptyset}(\mathcal{O}_{1},...,\mathcal{O}_{n})
holds:

\lambda_{\emptyset}(\mathcal{O}_{1},...,\mathcal{O}_{n}):=\sum_{N\geq n}\lambda_{\emptyset,N}(\mathcal{O}_{1},...\mathcal{O}_{n})=\sum_{N\geq n}\mathcal{O}_{1}\otimes_{\text{sym}}...\otimes_{\text{sym}}\mathcal{O}_{n}\otimes_{\text{sym}}1^{\otimes_{\text{sym}}(N-n)}

(7.70)

where
\otimes_{\text{sym}}
is the symmetric tensor product and
\lambda_{\emptyset,N}(\mathcal{O}_{1},...,\mathcal{O}_{n})
denotes the
N
-th degree component of
\lambda_{\emptyset}(\mathcal{O}_{1},...,\mathcal{O}_{n})
. Notice that there is no contribution from degrees less than
n
. To be more concrete, we give some examples of
\lambda_{\emptyset}(\mathcal{O}_{1},...\mathcal{O}_{n})
when
n
is small:

1.

When
n=2
, the first non-zero term is:

\lambda_{\emptyset,2}(\mathcal{O}_{1},\mathcal{O}_{2})=\mathcal{O}_{1}\otimes_{\text{sym}}\mathcal{O}_{2}=\mathcal{O}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{2}+\mathcal{O}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{1}

(7.71)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lambda_{\emptyset,3}(\mathcal{O}_{1},\mathcal{O}_{2})=\mathcal{O}_{1}\otimes_{\text{sym}}\mathcal{O}_{2}\otimes_{\text{sym}}1&=\mathcal{O}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{2}\otimes 1+\mathcal{O}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{1}\otimes 1\\
&+\mathcal{O}_{1}\otimes 1\otimes\mathcal{O}_{2}+\mathcal{O}_{2}\otimes 1\otimes\mathcal{O}_{1}\\
&+1\otimes\mathcal{O}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{2}+1\otimes\mathcal{O}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{1}\end{split}

(7.72)

In general, we need to sum over all possible ways of assigning 2 operators to
N
slots where each operator is assigned a different slots. For example when
N=3
, there are
\binom{3}{2}\times 2!=6
distinct assignments:

(1,2)\mapsto(1,2),(2,1),(1,3),(3,1),(2,3),(3,2)

(7.73)

Denote the set of such set maps as
\mathcal{P}_{2,N}:=\{\vec{j}:[2]\rightarrow[N]\text{s.t.}j_{1}\neq j_{2}\}
, then we can write:

\lambda_{\emptyset,N}(x_{1},x_{2})=\sum_{\vec{j}\in\mathcal{P}_{2,N}}1\otimes...\underbrace{\otimes x_{1}\otimes}_{j_{1}=k\text{ ,k-th position}}...\underbrace{\otimes x_{2}\otimes}_{j_{2}=\ell\text{ ,$\ell$-th position}}...\otimes 1

(7.74)

2.

When
n=3
, the first non-zero term is:

\lambda_{\emptyset,3}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{3}}x_{\pi(1)}\otimes x_{\pi(2)}\otimes x_{\pi(3)}

(7.75)

where
\mathcal{S}_{3}
is the permutation group of
3
elements. For general terms, an analogous formula as
\lambda_{\emptyset,N}(x_{1},x_{2})
holds. Denote the set of all possible ways of assigning 3 operators to
N
slots as
\mathcal{P}_{3,N}
. Then we have:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lambda_{\emptyset,N}&(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\\
&=\sum_{\vec{j}\in\mathcal{P}_{3,N}}1\otimes...\underbrace{\otimes x_{1}\otimes}_{j_{1}=k\text{ ,k-th position}}...\underbrace{\otimes x_{2}\otimes}_{j_{2}=\ell\text{ ,$\ell$-th position}}...\underbrace{\otimes x_{3}\otimes}_{j_{3}=m\text{ ,m-th position}}...\otimes 1\end{split}

(7.76)

The general formula of
\lambda_{\emptyset}(\mathcal{O}_{1},...,\mathcal{O}_{n})
follows from induction and recursive formula (c.f. Equation
7.68
). We sketch the argument below. By inductive hypothesis,
\lambda_{\emptyset}(\mathcal{O}_{2},...,\mathcal{O}_{n})
only contains terms where each operator of
\mathcal{O}_{2},...,\mathcal{O}_{n}
is placed at different tensorial positions. No two operators are assigned the same position. The inductive formula (c.f. Equation
7.68
) involves a product
\lambda(\mathcal{O}_{1})\lambda_{\emptyset}(\mathcal{O}_{2},...,\mathcal{O}_{n})
. This product contains two types of terms:

1.

The newly added operator
\mathcal{O}_{1}
is assigned to a previously empty position
72
72

72

Empty positions are occupied by the identity.
.

2.

The newly added operator
\mathcal{O}_{1}
is assigned to a previously occupied position. Suppose that position is occupied with the operator
\mathcal{O}_{i}
(
2\leq i\leq n
), then the addition of
\mathcal{O}_{1}
modifies the occupant operator to
\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{i}
.

Then by general symmmetric argument, we can see that the second type of terms sum up to exactly
\sum_{2\leq i\leq n}\lambda_{\emptyset}(\mathcal{O}_{2},...,\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{i},...,\mathcal{O}_{n})
. This is canceled by the second term in the recursive formula. The remaining expression is precisely Equation
7.70
.

We can calculate the inner product
\bra{\varphi_\omega^{1/2}}\lambda_{\emptyset}(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{1},..,\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{m}),\lambda_{\emptyset}(\mathcal{O}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{O}_{n})\ket{\varphi_\omega^{1/2}}
. Without loss of generality, we assume
m\geq n
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bra{\varphi_\omega^{1/2}}&\lambda_{\emptyset}(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{1},..,\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{m}),\lambda_{\emptyset}(\mathcal{O}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{O}_{n})\ket{\varphi_\omega^{1/2}}\\
&=\sum_{N\geq m}\frac{e^{-\omega(1)}}{N!}\omega^{\otimes N}\bigg(\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{\dagger}_{1}\otimes_{\text{sym}}\cdots\otimes_{\text{sym}}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{\dagger}_{m}\otimes_{\text{sym}}1^{\otimes_{\text{sym}}(N-m)}\right)\\
&\cdot\left(\mathcal{O}_{1}\otimes_{\text{sym}}\cdots\otimes_{\text{sym}}\mathcal{O}_{n}\otimes_{\text{sym}}1^{\otimes_{\text{sym}}(N-n)}\right)\bigg)\\
&=\sum_{N\geq m}\frac{e^{-\omega(1)}}{N!}\delta_{nm}(n!\binom{N}{n})\omega(1)^{N-n}\sum_{\pi\in S_{n}}\prod_{1\leq i\leq n}\omega(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{\dagger}_{i}\mathcal{O}_{\pi(i)})\\
&=\delta_{nm}\sum_{\pi\in S_{n}}\prod_{1\leq i\leq n}\omega(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{\dagger}_{i}\mathcal{O}_{\pi(i)})\end{split}

(7.77)

where we have used the following two facts:

1.

Because the singleton expectation vanishes, the non-zero contributions to
\omega^{\otimes N}((\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{\dagger}_{1}\otimes_{\text{sym}}\cdots\otimes_{\text{sym}}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{\dagger}_{m}\otimes_{\text{sym}}1^{\otimes_{\text{sym}}(N-m)})(\mathcal{O}_{1}\otimes_{\text{sym}}\cdots\otimes_{\text{sym}}\mathcal{O}_{n}\otimes_{\text{sym}}1^{\otimes_{\text{sym}}(N-n)}))
come from pairing
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{\dagger}_{j}
with
\mathcal{O}_{i}
. Therefore, this expectation value is non-zero if and only if
n=m

2.

The factor
n!
in the third line of the derivation comes from the symmetric tensor product. For example
\omega^{\otimes 2}((\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{\dagger}_{1}\otimes_{\text{sym}}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{\dagger}_{2})(\mathcal{O}_{1}\otimes_{\text{sym}}\mathcal{O}_{2}))=\omega^{\otimes 2}((\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{\dagger}_{1}\otimes\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{\dagger}_{2}+\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{\dagger}_{2}\otimes\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{\dagger}_{1})(\mathcal{O}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{2}+\mathcal{O}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{1}))=2\omega(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{\dagger}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{1})\omega(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{\dagger}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{2})+2\omega(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{\dagger}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2})\omega(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{\dagger}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{1})
. And the binomial factor
\binom{N}{n}
comes from choosing
n
positions out of
N
to place the nontrivial operators.

This calculation shows that the vectors
\{\lambda_{\emptyset}(\mathcal{O}_{1},...,\mathcal{O}_{n})\ket{\varphi_\omega^{1/2}}\}
form a basis for the symmetric Fock space
\mathcal{F}_{\text{sym}}\left(L_{2}(\mathcal{N},\omega)\right)

73
73

73

Notice that this basis of
\lambda_{\emptyset}
-vectors is
not
a finite-dimensional modification of the familiar symmetric basis in the sense that the difference between
\lambda_{\emptyset}(\mathcal{O}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{O}_{n})\ket{\varphi_\omega^{1/2}}
and
\mathcal{O}_{1}\otimes_{\text{sym}}\cdots\otimes_{\text{sym}}\mathcal{O}_{n}\ket{\varphi_\omega^{1/2}}
is a infinite sum of mutually orthogonal vectors:

\displaystyle\lambda_{\emptyset}(\mathcal{O}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{O}_{n})\ket{\varphi_\omega^{1/2}}-\mathcal{O}_{1}\otimes_{\text{sym}}\cdots\otimes_{\text{sym}}\mathcal{O}_{n}\ket{\varphi_\omega^{1/2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{N\geq n+1}\mathcal{O}_{1}\otimes_{\text{sym}}\cdots\otimes_{\text{sym}}\mathcal{O}_{n}\otimes_{\text{sym}}1^{\otimes_{\text{sym}}(N-n)}\ket{\varphi_\omega^{1/2}}

(7.78)

From this perspective, the
\lambda_{\emptyset}
-basis cannot be obtained by a small (i.e., finite-rank or compact) perturbation of the usual symmetric basis.
.

7.3.2
Poissonization and unital CP maps

We move on to discuss in what sense Poissonization preserves unital CP maps. If the unital CP map further preserves the input weight (finite and faithful):

\omega_{M}\circ\Psi=\omega_{N}

(7.79)

Then there exists a unital CP map between the two corresponding Poisson algebras:

\Gamma(\Psi):=\sum_{N\geq 0}\Psi^{\otimes N}:\mathbb{P}_{\omega_{N}}\mathcal{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}_{\omega_{M}}\mathcal{M}

(7.80)

Concretely, this lifted unital CP map acts on integrated Poisson quantized operators in the obvious way:

\Gamma(\Psi)\left(\Gamma(e^{i\mathcal{O}})\right)=\Gamma\left(\Psi(e^{i\mathcal{O}})\right)

(7.81)

It also acts on the Poisson quantized operators in the obvious way:

\Gamma(\Psi)\left(\lambda(\mathcal{O})\right)=\lambda\left(\Psi(\mathcal{O})\right)

(7.82)

The lifted unital CP map preserves the Poisson state:

\varphi_{\omega_{M}}\circ\Gamma(\Psi)=\varphi_{\omega_{N}}

(7.83)

A special example of a unital CP map is a normal
*
-homomorphism. Thus if we have a normal
*
-homomorphism
\pi:\mathcal{N}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
that preserves the weight:
\omega_{M}\circ\pi=\omega_{N}
, then we have a lifted normal
*
-homomorphism:
\Gamma(\pi):\mathbb{P}_{\omega_{N}}\mathcal{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}_{\omega_{M}}\mathcal{M}
such that the Poisson state is preserved:
\varphi_{\omega_{M}}\circ\Gamma(\pi)=\varphi_{\omega_{N}}
.

7.3.3
Modular automorphism of Poisson state

Finally, we explain the relation between the modular automorphism of the Poisson state and the modular automorphism of the input weight. The Poisson quantization map intertwines these two modular automorphisms in the sense that:

\sigma_{t}^{\varphi_{\omega}}(\lambda(\mathcal{O}))=\lambda(\sigma_{t}^{\omega}(\mathcal{O}))

(7.84)

where
\sigma_{t}^{\varphi_{\omega}}
is the modular automorphism of the Poisson state and
\sigma_{t}^{\omega}
is the modular automorphism of the input weight.

In addition, the Poisson quantization map also intertwines the modular conjugations:

J_{\varphi_{\omega}}(\lambda(\mathcal{O}))=\lambda(J_{\omega}(\mathcal{O}))

(7.85)

where
J_{\varphi_{\omega}}
is the modular conjugation of the Poisson state and
J_{\omega}
is the modular conjugation of the input weight.

This intertwining property allows us to calculate the spectrum of the modular operator
\Delta_{\varphi_{\omega}}
from the spectrum of the modular operator
\Delta_{\omega}
. This is useful to determine the type of the Poisson algebra. We will briefly mention some results along this line in the next section.

7.3.4
Relative modular operator, Connes cocycle, and Poissonization

Other key objects in the modular theory are the relative modular operator and the Connes cocycle. In the context of Poissonization, because the construction relies on both the algebra and the weight, it is natural to ask how two Poisson algebras
\mathbb{P}_{\omega_{1}}\mathcal{N}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\omega_{1}}\mathcal{N}
are related. Here, the input algebra is the same but the input weights are different. If one weight is a bounded perturbation of the other (i.e.
0\leq\omega_{2}-\omega_{1}
is a bounded positive linear functional), we can answer this question using Poisson quantized Connes cocycles.

Specifically, recall that Connes cocycle
u_{t}:=(D\omega_{1}:D\omega_{2})_{t}\in U(\mathcal{N})
is a unitary operator such that the cocycle condition holds:

u_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\omega_{2}}(u_{s})=u_{t+s}

(7.86)

where
\sigma_{t}^{\omega_{2}}
is the modular automorphism of
\omega_{2}
. Poissonizing this condition gives us:

\Gamma(u_{t})\sigma_{t}^{\varphi_{\omega_{2}}}(\Gamma(u_{s}))=\Gamma(u_{t})\Gamma(\sigma^{\omega_{2}}_{t}(u_{s}))=\Gamma(u_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\omega_{2}}(u_{s}))=\Gamma(u_{t+s})

(7.87)

where
\sigma_{t}^{\varphi_{\omega_{2}}}
is the modular automorphism of
\varphi_{\omega_{2}}
. In addition, the Poisson quantized operator
\Gamma(u_{t})
is unitary. Therefore, by Connes-Matsuda theorem
T
, there exists a normal faithful semifinite weight
\widetilde{\varphi}
on the Poisson algebra
\mathbb{P}_{\omega_{2}}\mathcal{N}
such that the Connes cocycle between
\widetilde{\varphi}
and
\varphi_{\omega_{2}}
is given by
\Gamma(u_{t})
:

(D\widetilde{\varphi}:D\varphi_{\omega_{2}})_{t}=\Gamma(u_{t})

(7.88)

So far, we have not used the fact that
\omega_{1}
is a bounded perturbation from
\omega_{2}
. Now because
\omega_{2}-\omega_{1}\geq 0
, the analytically-continues Connes cocycle operator
u_{-i/2}
is well-defined and is a contraction:
||u_{-i/2}||\leq 1
. Hence, the Poisson quantized operator
\Gamma(u_{-i/2})
is also a well-defined contraction and it is an analytic continuation of
\Gamma(u_{t})
. It is well-known that
u_{-i/2}
is the noncommutative Radon-Nikodym derivative between
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
:

\omega_{1}(\cdot)=\omega_{2}(u_{-i/2}^{\dagger}\cdot u_{-i/2})

(7.89)

Similarly, we can use
\Gamma(u_{-i/2})
to define a perturbation of the Poisson state
\varphi_{\omega_{2}}
. This perturbed functional is
\widetilde{\varphi}
by construction. Naively, one would expect that this perturbed state would be the Poisson state
\varphi_{\omega_{1}}
. However, this expectation is wrong:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{\varphi}(\Gamma(\mathcal{O}))&=\varphi_{\omega_{2}}(\Gamma(u_{-i/2})^{\dagger}\Gamma(\mathcal{O})\Gamma(u_{-i/2}))=\exp(\omega_2(u^\dagger_{-i/2}\mathcal{O}u_{-i/2} - 1))\\
&=e^{\omega_{1}(1)-\omega_{2}(1)}\exp(\omega_1(\mathcal{O} - 1))\end{split}

(7.90)

where
\Gamma(\mathcal{O})\in\mathbb{P}_{\omega_{2}}\mathcal{N}
,
|\omega_{2}(\mathcal{O}-1)|<\infty
and
||\mathcal{O}||\leq 1
. Here we have used the assumption that
\omega_{1}
is a bounded perturbation of
\omega_{2}
so that
|\omega_{1}(1)-\omega_{2}(1)|<\infty
. Notice that the factor
\exp(\omega_1(\mathcal{O} - 1))
is exactly
\varphi_{\omega_{1}}(\Gamma(\mathcal{O}))
. Therefore the state
e^{-\omega_{1}(1)+\omega_{2}(1)}\widetilde{\varphi}
is the same as the Poisson state
\varphi_{\omega_{1}}
. And the Connes cocycle between
\varphi_{\omega_{1}}
and
\varphi_{\omega_{2}}
is given by:

(D\varphi_{\omega_{1}}:D\varphi_{\omega_{2}})_{t}=(D\varphi_{\omega_{1}}:D\widetilde{\varphi})_{t}(D\widetilde{\varphi}:D\varphi_{\omega_{2}})_{t}=e^{it(\omega_{1}(1)-\omega_{2}(1))}\Gamma(u_{t})

(7.91)

We can draw two useful conclusions from this calculation. First, the Poisson algebra
\mathbb{P}_{\omega_{1}}\mathcal{N}
is isomorphic to the Poisson algebra
\mathbb{P}_{\omega_{2}}\mathcal{N}
. This is a consequence of the noncommutative Hamburger moment theorem
MAW
and the fact that
e^{-\omega_{1}(1)+\omega_{2}(2)}\widetilde{\varphi}
is a well-defined state on
\mathbb{P}_{\omega_{2}}\mathcal{N}
with the same moment formula as
\varphi_{\omega_{1}}
.

Second, we can calculate the relative modular operator
\Delta_{\varphi_{\omega_{1}},\varphi_{\omega_{2}}}
from the Connes cocycle:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta_{\varphi_{\omega_{1}},\varphi_{\omega_{2}}}^{it}&=(D\varphi_{\omega_{1}}:D\varphi_{\omega_{2}})_{t}\Delta_{\varphi_{\omega_{2}}}^{-it}=e^{it(\omega_{1}(1)-\omega_{2}(1))}\Gamma(u_{t})\Gamma(\Delta_{\omega_{2}}^{-it})\\
&=e^{it(\omega_{1}(1)-\omega_{2}(1))}\Gamma((D\omega_{1}:D\omega_{2})_{t}\Delta_{\omega_{2}}^{-it})\\
&=e^{it(\omega_{1}(1)-\omega_{2}(1))}\Gamma(\Delta_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}}^{it})\end{split}

(7.92)

where we have used the identity:
\Delta_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}}^{it}=(D\omega_{1}:D\omega_{2})_{t}\Delta_{\omega_{2}}^{-it}
. Therefore, the generators of the relative modular flow are related by:

\log\Delta_{\varphi_{\omega_{1}},\varphi_{\omega_{2}}}=\lambda(\log\Delta_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}})+(\omega_{1}-\omega_{2})(1)

(7.93)

where
\lambda(\log\Delta_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}})
is the Poisson quantization of the generator
\log\Delta_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}}
.

This formula is useful in various quantum information calculations. As a simple example, we calculate the relative entropy:

\displaystyle\begin{split}D(\varphi_{\omega_{1}}|\varphi_{\omega_{2}})&=-\bra{\varphi_{\omega_2}^{1/2}}\log\Delta_{\varphi_{\omega_{1}},\varphi_{\omega_{2}}}\ket{\varphi_{\omega_2}^{1/2}}=-\bra{\varphi_{\omega_2}^{1/2}}\lambda(\log\Delta_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}})\ket{\varphi_{\omega_2}^{1/2}}+(\omega_{1}-\omega_{2})(1)\\
&=-\bra{\omega_2^{1/2}}\log\Delta_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}}\ket{\omega_2^{1/2}}+(\omega_{1}-\omega_{2})(1)\\
&=D(\omega_{1}|\omega_{2})+(\omega_{1}-\omega_{2})(1)\end{split}

(7.94)

where we have used the fact that Poissonization preserves the one-point function:

\bra{\varphi_{\omega_2}^{1/2}}\lambda(\log\Delta_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}})\ket{\varphi_{\omega_2}^{1/2}}=\varphi_{\omega_{2}}(\lambda(\log(\Delta_{\omega_1,\omega_2})))=\omega_{2}(\log\Delta_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}})=\bra{\omega_2^{1/2}}\log\Delta_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}}\ket{\omega_2^{1/2}}

(7.95)

We observe that after Poissonization, the relative entropy is preserved up to the additive constant
(\omega_{1}-\omega_{2})(1)
. Because both
\varphi_{\omega_{1}}
and
\varphi_{\omega_{2}}
are normalized states, we can further write:

D(\varphi_{\omega_{1}}|\varphi_{\omega_{2}})+(\varphi_{\omega_{1}}-\varphi_{\omega_{2}})(1)=D(\omega_{1}|\omega_{2})+(\omega_{1}-\omega_{2})(1)

(7.96)

The quantity on either side of this equation was first studied by Lindblad
Lin
as an extension of Araki’s original definition
Ara1
;
Ara2
of relative entropy. Therefore, we conclude that
Poissonization preserves Lindblad relative entropy
.

7.4
Factors from Poissonization

7.4.1
The center of the Poisson algebra of matrices

The Poisson algebra of matrix factor
\mathbb{P}_{\tr}M_{d}(\mathbb{C})
is the closure of the algebraic symmetric tensor algebra. It has a nontrivial center generated by the number operator.

We demonstrate this point using creation/annihilation operators
74
74

74

The argument below is not a rigorous proof. The purpose is to show how the formalism of one-sided number-preserving operators can be used to study structures of Poisson algebras.
. Recall that
\mathbb{P}_{\tr}M_{d}(\mathbb{C})
is generated by the
one-sided number-preserving operators
(c.f. Subsection
4.2
):

\lambda(\mathcal{O})=\sum_{ijk}\mathcal{O}_{ij}a^{\dagger}_{i\bar{k}}a_{j\bar{k}}

(7.97)

where
T=\sum_{ij}\mathcal{O}_{ij}\ket{i}\bra{j}\in M_{d}(\mathbb{C})
is a matrix. Here, we have summed over the conjugate indices. Analogously, we can also define the conjugate one-sided number-preserving operator (c.f. Equation
4.35
):

\lambda^{\prime}(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}):=\sum_{ijk}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{ij}a^{\dagger}_{k\bar{i}}a_{k\bar{j}}

(7.98)

The Poisson algebra generated by
\lambda^{\prime}(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}})
is the commutant of the Poisson algebra generated by
\lambda(\mathcal{O})
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}[\lambda(\mathcal{O}),&\lambda^{\prime}(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}})]=\sum_{ijklmn}\mathcal{O}_{ij}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{lm}[a^{\dagger}_{i\bar{k}}a_{j\bar{k}},a^{\dagger}_{n\bar{l}}a_{n\bar{m}}]=\sum_{ijklmn}\mathcal{O}_{ij}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{lm}(\delta_{jn}\delta_{kl}a^{\dagger}_{i\bar{k}}a_{n\bar{m}}-\delta_{in}\delta_{km}a^{\dagger}_{n\bar{l}}a_{j\bar{k}})\\
&=\sum_{ijkm}\mathcal{O}_{ij}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{km}a^{\dagger}_{i\bar{k}}a_{j\bar{m}}-\sum_{ijkl}\mathcal{O}_{ij}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{lk}a^{\dagger}_{i\bar{l}}a_{j\bar{k}}=\sum_{ijkl}\mathcal{O}_{ij}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{kl}a^{\dagger}_{i\bar{k}}a_{j\bar{l}}-\mathcal{O}_{ij}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{lk}a^{\dagger}_{i\bar{l}}a_{j\bar{k}}=0\end{split}

(7.99)

From this simple calculation, it is clear that the number operator
\lambda(\mathbbm{1})=\lambda^{\prime}(\mathbbm{1})=\sum_{ik}a^{\dagger}_{i\bar{k}}a_{i\bar{k}}
commutes with both
\lambda(\mathcal{O})
’s and
\lambda^{\prime}(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}})
’s. Hence the number operator is affiliated with the center of
\mathbb{P}_{\tr}M_{d}(\mathbb{C})
.

7.4.2
Poissonization of
\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{H})
with tracial weight

Interestingly, there are setups where the output of the Poissonization is a factor. We focus on a single case - namely, Poissonization of a type
I_{\infty}
factor
\mathbb{P}_{\tr}\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{H})
is a type
II_{1}
factor
75
75

75

If
\mathcal{H}
is separable, the Poisson algebra is the hyperfinite
II_{1}
factor.
. So far, we have restricted to the case where the input weight to Poissonization is finite. This is not the case for the canonical trace on a type
I_{\infty}
factor. Thus, we first discuss how to understand the Poisson algebra when the input weight is an infinite trace. Then we will demonstrate explicitly how the center of Poissonization of finite-dimensional matrix algebra disappears when the dimensionality of the input algebra becomes infinite.

The construction of the Poisson algebra
\mathbb{P}_{\tr}\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{H})
relies on the inductive limit construction
76
76

76

One may ask if the same inductive limit construction works for all cases where the input weight is not finite. Unfortunately, the answer is no. The technical condition for the inductive limit construction is that the weight
\omega
is
strictly semi-finite
. This means that there exists an increasing family of projections that are invariant under the modular automorphism of
\omega
. Equivalently, this means there exists a normal conditional expectation from the von Neumann algebra
N
to its centralizer
N_{\omega}
. Clearly, when the weight is tracial, it is strictly semi-finite if and only if the weight is semi-finite. For more general input weight, other constructions of Poissonization exists.
. The idea is to approximate
\mathbb{P}_{\tr}\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{H})
by a compatible sequence of Poisson algebras
\mathbb{P}_{\tr_{n}}M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
where
\tr_{n}
is the un-normalized trace on the matrix algebra. This sequence of Poisson algebras is mutually compatible in the sense that there exists a homomorphism
that preserves the Poisson state
between two Poisson algebras:

\pi_{n,k}:\mathbb{P}_{\tr_{k}}M_{k}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\mathbb{P}_{\tr_{n}}M_{n}(\mathbb{C}):\lambda(\mathcal{O})\mapsto\lambda(e_{k}\mathcal{O}e_{k})

(7.100)

where
n>k
and
e_{k}\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
is the projection that projects
\mathbb{C}^{n}
to the
k
-dimensional subspace
\mathbb{C}^{k}
so that
e_{k}\mathcal{O}e_{k}
is non-zero in the upper left hand block
77
77

77

The family of projections
\{e_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
is the modular-automorphism-invariant projections necessary for the inductive construction of Poissonization (see also previous footnote).
. The key observations are

1.

The map
\pi_{n,k}
preserves the expectation value under the Poisson state:

\varphi_{\tr_{n}}(\pi_{n,k}(\lambda(\mathcal{O})))=\varphi_{\tr_{n}}(\lambda(e_{k}\mathcal{O}e_{k}))=\tr_{n}(e_{k}\mathcal{O}e_{k})=\tr_{k}(\mathcal{O})=\varphi_{\tr_{k}}(\lambda(\mathcal{O}))

(7.101)

2.

Two maps
\pi_{n,k}
and
\pi_{m,n}
are compatible:

\pi_{m,k}=\pi_{m,n}\circ\pi_{n,k}

(7.102)

where
m>n>k
.

In terms of the integrated Poisson quantized operators, we have:

\pi_{n,k}(\Gamma(e^{i\mathcal{O}}))=\Gamma(e_{k}e^{i\mathcal{O}}e_{k}+e_{n}-e_{k})

(7.103)

where
\mathcal{O}^{\dagger}=\mathcal{O}\in M_{k}(\mathbb{C})
. By differentiation, we have:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}\pi_{n,k}(\Gamma(e^{it\mathcal{O}}))

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}\Gamma(e_{k}e^{it\mathcal{O}}e_{k}+e_{n}-e_{k})

(7.104)

\displaystyle=\lambda(\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}(e_{k}e^{it\mathcal{O}}e_{k}+e_{n}-e_{k}))=i\lambda(e_{k}\mathcal{O}e_{k})

This is compatible with
\pi_{n,k}
’s action on the Poisson quantized operators. And on the integrated Poisson quantized operators, the expectation values are also preserved:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\varphi_{\tr_{n}}(\pi_{n,k}(\Gamma(e^{i\mathcal{O}})))&=\exp(\tr_n(e_ke^{i\mathcal{O}}e_k + e_n - e_k - e_n))=\exp(\tr_n(e_k(e^{i\mathcal{O}} - e_k)e_k))\\
&=\exp(\tr_k(e^{i\mathcal{O}} - e_k))=\varphi_{\tr_{k}}(\Gamma(e^{i\mathcal{O}}))\end{split}

(7.105)

where we observed
e_{n}
is simply the identity operator in
M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
. Here we write
e_{n}
to emphasize that when
M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
is embedded in a larger matrix algebra, the identity becomes a non-trivial projection.

Therefore, by the inductive limit construction, the limiting
C^{*}
-algebra of the following
directed system
exists:

\mathbb{P}_{\tr_{n}}M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\xrightarrow{\pi_{n+1,n}}\mathbb{P}_{\tr_{n+1}}M_{n+1}(\mathbb{C})\xrightarrow{\pi_{n+2,n+1}}\cdots

(7.106)

In addition,
because
\pi_{n,k}
preserves the Poisson states
, there exists a well-defined limiting state on the inductive limit
C^{*}
-algebra. In particular, by performing the GNS construction and then taking the weak closure, the closure of the limiting
C^{*}
-algebra is the Poisson algebra
\mathbb{P}_{\tr}\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{H})
and the limiting state is the Poisson state. Concretely, this means that for each
n
, there exists a homomorphism:

\pi_{\infty,n}:\mathbb{P}_{\tr_{n}}M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\mathbb{P}_{\tr}\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{H}):\lambda(\mathcal{O})\mapsto\lambda(e_{n}\mathcal{O}e_{n})

(7.107)

such that
\pi_{\infty,n}\circ\pi_{n,k}=\pi_{\infty,k}
and
\varphi_{\tr}\circ\pi_{\infty,n}=\varphi_{\tr_{n}}
.

Using
\pi_{n,k}
maps, it is immediate how the center of
\mathbb{P}_{\tr_{k}}M_{k}(\mathbb{C})
disappears in
\mathbb{P}_{\tr_{n}}M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
. Recall that the center of
\mathbb{P}_{\tr_{k}}M_{k}(\mathbb{C})
is generated by the number operator
\lambda(e_{k})
. In
\mathbb{P}_{\tr_{n}}M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
,
\lambda(e_{k})
is no longer in the center because the switch operator does not commute with the projection
e_{k}
:

\mathcal{O}_{k+1,k}:=\ket{k+1}\bra{k}+\ket{k}\bra{k+1}

(7.108)

Because the Poisson quantization map preserves the commutation relation, we have:

[\lambda(e_{k}),\lambda(\mathcal{O}_{k+1,k})]\neq 0

(7.109)

And hence
\lambda(e_{k})
is no longer in the center.

Therefore, we have observed that the maps
\pi_{n,k}
preserve the Poisson state (i.e., preserve the singleton expectation value), but it comes at the price of not preserving the center. Because centers from each finite-dimensional matrix algebra are not mutually compatible, the limiting Poisson algebra does not have a center. We now make this idea formal.

Consider a sequence of unitaries
\{u_{2n}:=\mathbbm{1}_{n}\otimes\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}0&e_{n}\\
e_{n}&0\end{bmatrix}\in M_{2n}(\mathbb{C})\}
where
e_{n}
is the
n\times n
identity matrix. For any two matrices
\mathcal{O}\in M_{k}(\mathbb{C})
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\in M_{m}(\mathbb{C})
and for any
n\geq\max(k,m)
, we consider the sesquilinear form:

\displaystyle\langle\Gamma(\mathcal{O}),\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}})\rangle_{2n}:=\varphi_{\tr_{2n}}(\pi_{2n,k}(\Gamma(\mathcal{O})^{\dagger})\Gamma(u_{2n})^{\dagger}\pi_{2n,m}(\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}))\Gamma(u_{2n}))

(7.110)

We can calculate this sesquilinear form explicitly:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\varphi_{\tr_{2n}}&(\pi_{2n,k}(\Gamma(\mathcal{O})^{\dagger})\Gamma(u_{2n})^{\dagger}\pi_{2n,m}(\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}))\Gamma(u_{2n}))\\
&=\exp\tr_{2n}\bigg((\mathcal{O}^{\dagger}+e_{2n}-e_{k})u^{\dagger}_{2n}(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}+e_{2n}-e_{m})u_{2n}-e_{2n}\bigg)\\
&=\exp\tr_{2n}(\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{O}^{\dagger}+e_{n}-e_{k}&0\\
0&e_{n}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0&e_{n}\\
e_{n}&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}+e_{n}-e_{m}&0\\
0&e_{n}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0&e_{n}\\
e_{n}&0\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}e_{n}&0\\
0&e_{n}\end{bmatrix})\\
&=\exp\tr_{2n}(\begin{bmatrix}0&\mathcal{O}^{\dagger}+e_{n}-e_{k}\\
e_{n}&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0&\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}+e_{n}-e_{m}\\
e_{n}&0\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}e_{n}&0\\
0&e_{n}\end{bmatrix})\\
&=\exp\tr_{2n}(\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{O}^{\dagger}+e_{n}-e_{k}-e_{n}&0\\
0&\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}+e_{n}-e_{m}-e_{n}\end{bmatrix})\\
&=\exp\bigg(\tr_n(\mathcal{O}^\dagger- e_k) + \tr_n(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}} - e_m)\bigg)=\exp(\tr_k(\mathcal{O}^\dagger- e_k))\exp(\tr_m(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}} - e_m))\\
&=\varphi_{\tr}(\Gamma(\mathcal{O})^{\dagger})\varphi_{\tr}(\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}))\end{split}

(7.111)

where
e_{2n}
is the identity matrix in
M_{2n}(\mathbb{C})
. Because
x
is a
k\times k
matrix and
k<n
, the matrix
\mathcal{O}^{\dagger}+e_{2n}-e_{k}
has the block diagonal form
\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{O}^{\dagger}+e_{n}-e_{k}&0\\
0&e_{n}\end{bmatrix}
. We have written
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}+e_{2n}-e_{m}
in a similar block diagonal form.

Consider the sesquilinear form:

\langle\Gamma(\mathcal{O}),\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}})\rangle:=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\langle\Gamma(\mathcal{O}),\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}})\rangle_{2n}

(7.112)

where
x,y
are finite-dimensional matrices (i.e., the sesquilinear form is defined on the (ultra-)weakly dense subalgebra
\cup_{k\geq 1}\mathbb{P}_{\tr_{k}}M_{k}(\mathbb{C})
). From the calculation above, this sesquilinear form always factorizes:

\langle\Gamma(\mathcal{O}),\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}})\rangle=\varphi_{\tr}(\Gamma(\mathcal{O})^{\dagger})\varphi_{\tr}(\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}))

(7.113)

Now suppose
\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}})
is in the center, then we have another way to calculate this sesquilinear form:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle&\Gamma(\mathcal{O}),\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}})\rangle=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\varphi_{\tr_{2n}}(\pi_{2n,k}(\Gamma(\mathcal{O})^{\dagger})\Gamma(u_{2n})^{\dagger}\pi_{2n,m}(\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}))\Gamma(u_{2n}))\\
&=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\varphi_{\tr_{2n}}(\pi_{2n,k}(\Gamma(\mathcal{O})^{\dagger})\pi_{2n,m}(\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}))\Gamma(u_{2n})^{\dagger}\Gamma(u_{2n}))\\
&=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\varphi_{\tr_{2n}}(\pi_{2n,k}(\Gamma(\mathcal{O})^{\dagger})\pi_{2n,m}(\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}})))=\varphi_{\tr}(\Gamma(\mathcal{O})^{\dagger}\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}))\end{split}

(7.114)

In the second line of the equation, we have used the fact that
\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}})
is central. Therefore if
\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}})
is in the center, we have:

\varphi_{\tr}(\Gamma(\mathcal{O})^{\dagger}\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}))=\varphi_{\tr}(\Gamma(\mathcal{O})^{\dagger})\varphi_{\tr}(\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}))

(7.115)

where
\mathcal{O},\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}
are finite matrices. Because this equation holds for any finite matrix
\mathcal{O}
and because
\cup_{k\geq 1}\mathbb{P}_{\tr_{k}}M_{k}(\mathbb{C})
is (ultra-)weakly dense in
\mathbb{P}_{\tr}\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{H})
, we have
\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}})=\varphi_{\tr}(\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}))1
. This results in a contradiction, since the identity operator in the Poisson algebra
\mathbb{P}_{\tr}\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{H})
is not a Poisson quantized operator of a finite matrix. Therefore, no
\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}})
is contained in the center. Again by denseness of
\cup_{k\geq 1}\mathbb{P}_{\tr_{k}}M_{k}(\mathbb{C})
, we conclude that the center of
\mathbb{P}_{\tr}\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{H})
is trivial.

Finally,
\mathbb{P}_{\tr}\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{H})
is a type
II_{1}
factor follows simply from the fact that the Poisson state
\varphi_{\tr}
is a tracial state on an infinite-dimensional factor.

One may ask if
\lambda(1)
is well-defined in
\mathbb{P}_{\tr}\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{H})
. The answer is simply no. When the input weight is not finite, the domain of the Poisson quantization map is
no longer
the entire input algebra. We will not define the domain rigorously, because it involves introducing additional notions such as Tomita algebras. It suffices to say that any operator that is not finite under the input weight is
not
in the domain of the Poisson quantization map. Therefore, the identity operator is not in the domain of the Poisson quantization map and thus
\lambda(1)
simply does
not
exist.

The proof that
\mathbb{P}_{\tr}\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{H})
is a factor can be generalized in multiple directions. We collect several results here and briefly discuss their proofs.

Proposition 7.1
.

Let
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
be the hyperfinite type
II_{\infty}
factor and let
\tau_{\infty}
be the canonical tracial weight on
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
, then the Poisson algebra
\mathbb{P}_{\tau_{\infty}}\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
is the hyperfinite type
II_{1}
factor.

Proposition 7.2
.

For any von Neumann algebra
\mathcal{N}
and any normal faithful
strictly semifinite
T
weight
\omega
on
\mathcal{N}
, consider the tensor product
\mathcal{N}\otimes\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{H})
and the tensor weight
\omega\otimes\tr
. Then the Poisson algebra
\mathbb{P}_{\omega\otimes\tr}\mathcal{N}\otimes\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{H})
is a factor.

Sketch of Proof

These two statements can be proved by mimicking the inductive limit construction of
\mathbb{P}_{\tr}\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{H})
. Instead of a sequence of Poisson algebras
\mathbb{P}_{\tr_{n}}M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
’s, we consider the sequence
\mathbb{P}_{\omega_{r}\otimes\tr_{n}}\left(f_{r}\mathcal{N}f_{r}\right)\otimes M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
where we have used an increasing sequence of
\omega
-finite projections in the centralizer
\mathcal{N}_{\omega}
:
\{f_{r}\in\mathcal{N}_{\omega}:\omega(f_{r})<\infty\}
that strongly converges to
1
T
78
78

78

The existence of such projections can be taken as the definition of
\omega
being strictly semifinite.
T
. Note that this sequence is now labeled by both
n
and
r
. Analogous to the connecting homomorphism
\pi_{n,k}
in the case of
\mathbb{P}_{\tr}\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{H})
, we consider the modified connecting homomorphisms:

\pi^{\mathcal{N}}_{(n,s),(k,r)}:\mathbb{P}_{\omega_{r}\otimes\tr_{k}}\left(f_{r}\mathcal{N}f_{r}\right)\otimes M_{k}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\mathbb{P}_{\omega_{s}\otimes\tr_{n}}\left(f_{s}\mathcal{N}f_{s}\right)\otimes M_{n}(\mathbb{C})

(7.116)

The key calculation is the following sesquilinear form:

\langle\Gamma(\mathcal{O}),\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}})\rangle_{\mathcal{N}}:=\lim_{n,r\rightarrow\infty}\varphi_{\omega_{r}\otimes\tr_{n}}\left(\Gamma(\mathcal{O}^{\dagger})\Gamma(f_{r}\otimes u^{\dagger}_{2n})\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}})\Gamma(f_{r}\otimes u_{2n})\right)

(7.117)

By essentially the same calculation as in the case of
\mathbb{P}_{\tr}\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{H})
, we can show that the factorization holds for any
\mathcal{O},\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}
in a (ultra-)weakly dense subalgebra:

\langle\Gamma(\mathcal{O}),\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}})\rangle_{\mathcal{N}}=\varphi_{\omega\otimes\tr}\left(\Gamma(\mathcal{O}^{\dagger})\right)\varphi_{\omega\otimes\tr}\left(\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}})\right)

(7.118)

Then following the same argument as in the case of
\mathbb{P}_{\tr}\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{H})
, we can show that any element in the center of
\mathbb{P}_{\omega\otimes\tr}\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{H})
is proportional to identity.

The von Neumann algebra in the previous proposition can be commutative and can be of any type. As long as it is a valid von Neumann algebra, the conclusion of the previous proposition holds.

Proposition 7.3
.

Using the same notation as the previous proposition and assume that the weight
\omega
is strictly semifinite
T
, the type of the Poisson algebra
\mathbb{P}_{\omega\otimes\tr}\mathcal{N}\otimes\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{H})
is determined by the spectrum of the modular operator
\Delta_{\omega}
.:

1.

\text{Spec}(\Delta_{\omega})=\{1\}
, the Poisson algebra
\mathbb{P}_{\omega\otimes\tr}\mathcal{N}\otimes\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{H})
is type
II_{1}
;

2.

If the multiplicative subgroup generated by
\text{Spec}(\Delta_{\omega})-\{0\}
in the multiplicative group of positive real numbers
\mathbb{R}^{\times}_{+}
is isomorphic to
\langle\lambda^{n}:n\in\mathbb{Z}\rangle
, the Poisson algebra
\mathbb{P}_{\omega\otimes\tr}\mathcal{N}\otimes\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{H})
is type
III_{\lambda}
;

3.

If the multiplicative subgroup generated by
\text{Spec}(\Delta_{\omega})-\{0\}
is the entire group of positive real numbers, the Poisson algebra
\mathbb{P}_{\omega\otimes\tr}\mathcal{N}\otimes\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{H})
is type
III_{1}
.

The proof relies on the well-known fact that the Connes invariant of a von Neumann algebra is completely determined by the spectrum of a single modular operator if both the algebra and the centralizer algebra are factors
T
. This proposition can be understood as a simple generalization of the well-known classification of Araki-Woods
Araki-Woods
. Using this proposition, we can engineer numerous physically interesting examples of Poisson algebras (e.g., we can construct covariant nets of type
III_{1}
factors that satisfy the Haag-Kastler axioms
haag
). We will dedicate relevant discussions to a subsequent paper.

7.5
General statement of Poissonization

In this final subsection, we collect the general theorem of Poissonization. The discussions in this work have focused on specific examples of Poissonization. It fails to emphasize the functoriality of Poissonization. As mentioned before, Poissonization is extremely general. Just like the second quantization, which can be applied to any one-particle Hilbert space, Poissonization can be applied to any one-particle operator algebra in the following sense:

Theorem 3
.

For any von Neumann algebra
\mathcal{N}
and any normal faithful semifinite weight
\omega
, the Poisson algebra
\mathbb{P}_{\omega}\mathcal{N}
and the Poisson state
\varphi_{\omega}
are well-defined.

8
Discussion

It is well-known that tunneling effects in quantum mechanics are rare events with a probability rate that is exponentially small
\Gamma\sim e^{-O(S_{E}/\hbar)}
in the semi-classical regime. It follows from the Poisson limit theorem that, at late times,
T\sim 1/\Gamma
, the statistics of multi-tunneling events are universally described by a Poisson process
79
79

79

In tunnel junctions, this Poisson process is called shot noise
blanter2000shot
.
. In this work, first, we argued that since topology fluctuations in gravity are also exponentially suppressed, the contribution of baby universes to exponentially late-time physics of quantum gravity correlators can be universally described by a Poisson process.

In third quantization or universe field theory, it is natural to allow for the creation and annihilation of all universes, including asymptotic open universes with noncommutative algebras. This suggests that the late-time and coarse-grained statistics of such rare events are universally captured by a noncommutative generalization of the Poisson process. We presented such a framework called Poissonization. We worked out simple 2D models of quantum gravity as examples of Poissonization. We observed that the indistinguishability of the bulk dynamical degrees of freedom is tied to the randomness of simple operators in ETH, quantum chaos, and gravity.

Similarly, in a quantum chaotic system, by the ETH, the amplitude for a simple operator to change the macroscopic energy of the system is also exponentially small and random. We argued that by the Poisson limit theorem, the contribution of these rare processes to powers of the simple operators at late time is captured by Poissonization of Gaussian random operators.

Coleman had argued that, to avoid causality problems, the algebra of operators that create and annihilate baby universes
a^{\dagger}(J)/a(J)
has to be commutative. Later, Marolf and Maxfield used the Euclidean path-integral methods to reach the same conclusion
MM
. In simple models such as 2D Topological QFTs (TQFT) and pure JT gravity, this algebra is indeed commutative. This is in tension with the universe field theory paradigm, where the algebra of creation/annihilation operators is that of a quantum field theory, and hence noncommutative. The explicit construction of the universe field theory in simple String theory motivated models and JT gravity resulted in a noncommutative algebra
giddings1988axion
;
post2022universe
. See
anous2020density
;
casali2022baby
for attempts to reconcile the two pictures.
More generally, once we allow creation/annihilation operators for asymptotic open boundaries as well. It is no longer reasonable to expect a commutative algebra. Our operator algebraic approach to third quantization (Poissonization) provides a resolution to the puzzle above.

As we saw in this work, for the Poisson limit theorem, one needs to wait for times of order
e^{2S}
, which is significantly longer than the timescale of the onset of the plateau in the spectral form factor of quantum chaotic systems and Gaussian random matrices. We attribute this to the fact that our simplified model of the Poisson process does not incorporate the finite dimensionality of the total Hilbert space and the fact that we have matrix degrees of freedom.
