\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\sum_{\pi_{A}\in\mathcal{S}_{|A|}}\mu=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}B_{|\pi|}(\mu)

(E.23)

where
\mathcal{P}_{n}
is the set of partitions of
n
-elements,
\sigma
is a partition, and
\mathcal{S}_{|A|}
is the permutation group on
|A|
elements.

Proof.

For any partition
\sigma=\sqcup A\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
, we have a set map:

f_{\sigma}:\sqcup_{A}\mathcal{S}_{|A|}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{n}:(\pi_{A})_{A}\mapsto\prod_{A}\pi_{A}

(E.24)

Consider the combined set map:

f=\sqcup_{\sigma}f_{\sigma}:\sqcup_{\sigma}(\sqcup_{A}\mathcal{S}_{|A|})\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{n}

(E.25)

This map is many-to-one. For each fixed permutation
\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
, consider its cycle decomposition. Let
|\pi|
be the number of cycles in
\pi
. The cycle decomposition defines a set map:

\pi:[n]\rightarrow[|\pi|]

(E.26)

where
\pi(i)=\pi(j)
if and only if
i,j
are in the same cycle. Then any partition
\eta
of
[m]
uniquely induces a partition
\pi^{-1}(\eta)
of
[n]
via pullback of
\pi
. In addition, for each subset
A
in the induced partition
\pi^{-1}(\eta)
of
[n]
, the cycles of the permutation
\pi
uniquely determines a permutation
\pi_{A}\in\mathcal{S}_{|A|}
. Moreover, the product:

\prod_{A\in\pi^{-1}(\eta)}\pi_{A}=\pi

(E.27)

Therefore, the preimage under the map
f
is given by:

f^{-1}(\pi)=\{(\pi_{A})_{A\in\pi^{-1}(\eta)}:\eta\in\mathcal{P}_{|\pi|}\text{ , and }\pi_{A}\text{ is product of cycles in }\pi\}

(E.28)

We emphasize that once the partition
\eta\in\mathcal{P}_{|\pi|}
is fixed, the permutation in the preimage
f^{-1}(\pi)
is uniquely defined by the cycle decomposition of
\pi
.
Then we have:

\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\sum_{\pi_{A}\in\mathcal{S}_{|A|}}\mu=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\sum_{(\pi_{A})_{A}\in\sqcup_{A}\mathcal{S}_{|A|}}\mu^{|\sigma|}=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}\sum_{\eta\in\mathcal{P}_{|\pi|}}\mu^{|\eta|}=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}B_{|\pi|}(\mu)

(E.29)

In the last equation, we have used the following formula of Touchard polynomial:

B_{n}(\mu)=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\mu^{|\sigma|}\qed

(E.30)

For the next corollary, we consider the following class of multiplicative functions on the permutation group
S_{n}
:

Definition E.1
.

f:S_{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is a multiplicative function on
S_{n}
if for any two commuting permutations
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
,
f(\pi_{1}\pi_{2})=f(\pi_{1})f(\pi_{2})
.

A multiplicative function is defined by fixing its values on irreducible cycles. This is because any permutation can be uniquely decomposed into a product of irreducible cycles (i.e., decomposition using the orbits of the permutation). Notice that a multiplicative function is
not
a group homomorphism. For two generic permutations
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
that do not commute with each other, in general
f(\pi_{1}\pi_{2})\neq f(\pi_{1})f(\pi_{2})
. The multiplicative property holds only when the two permutations commute with each other. For this reason, we have coined the name multiplicative function to distinguish the notion from a group homomorphism.

Corollary E.3
.

Let
f:S_{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a multiplicative function on the permutation group
S_{n}
then we have:

\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\sum_{\pi_{A}\in S_{A}}\mu\prod_{\gamma_{A}\in S_{A}}f(\gamma_{A})=\sum_{\pi\in S_{n}}B_{|\pi|}(\mu)\prod_{\gamma\in\pi}f(\gamma)

(E.31)

where
\gamma\in\pi
are irreducible cycles of
\pi\in S_{n}
and
\gamma_{A}\in\pi_{A}
are irreducible cycles of
\pi_{A}\in S_{A}
.

Proof.

The proof is a simple modification of the proof of the Lemma. Using the same notation, we recalculate Equation
E.29
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\sum_{\pi_{A}\in S_{A}}\mu\prod_{\gamma_{A}\in\pi_{A}}f(\gamma_{A})&=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\sum_{(\pi_{A})_{A}\in\sqcup_{A}S_{A}}\mu^{|\sigma|}\prod_{\pi_{A}}f(\pi_{A})\\
&=\sum_{\pi\in S_{n}}\sum_{\eta\in\mathcal{P}_{|\pi|}}\mu^{|\eta|}f(\pi)\\
&=\sum_{\pi\in S_{n}}B_{|\pi|}(\mu)\prod_{\gamma\in\pi}f(\gamma)\qed\end{split}

(E.32)

where in the last equation we used the definition of a multiplicative function to decompose
f(\pi)
into a product over its cycles. Also note the
\gamma_{A}
’s and
\pi_{A}
’s are pairwise commuting because the subsets
A
are pairwise disjoint.

There are two natural types of multiplicative functions that we need in the calculation of the moment formula.

1.

Fix a set of matrices
\{\mathcal{O}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{O}_{p}\}
. For any irreducible cycle
\gamma\in S_{p}
, we define:

f_{\tr}(\gamma):=\tr(\overrightarrow{\prod_{k\in\gamma}}\mathcal{O}_k)

(E.33)

where the product of matrices is ordered. For a given cycle
\gamma=(i_{1},i_{2},\cdots,i_{k})
, there is cyclic ambiguity in representing the cycle in the sense that:

\gamma=(i_{2},i_{3},\cdots i_{k},i_{1})=\cdots=(i_{k},i_{1},\cdots,i_{k-1})

(E.34)

This corresponds to the cyclic invariance of the tracial weight:

\tr(\mathcal{O}_{i_1}\cdots\mathcal{O}_{i_k})=\tr(\mathcal{O}_{i_2}\cdots\mathcal{O}_{i_k}\mathcal{O}_{i_1})=\cdots=\tr(\mathcal{O}_{i_k}\mathcal{O}_{i_1}\cdots\mathcal{O}_{i_{k-1}})

(E.35)

Therefore, the function
f_{\tr}
is well-defined on cycles. Then for general permutations
\pi\in S_{n}
, the multiplicative generalization of
f
is given by:

f_{\tr}(\pi)=\prod_{\gamma\in\pi}f_{\tr}(\gamma)

(E.36)

By construction,
f_{\tr}
is multiplicative;

2.

Given a constant
d>0
, for any permutation
\pi\in S_{p}
the counting function
d^{\text{cycles}(\pi)}
where
\text{cycles}(\pi)
counts the number of irreducible cycles in
\pi
. This function is multiplicative. This multiplicative counting function arises as the trace of a particular operator, which we explain below.

For any given index set
A=\{\alpha_{1}<\alpha_{2}<\cdots<\alpha_{|A|}:\alpha_{k}\in\{1,2,3,\cdots,p\}\}
101
101

101

A\subset\{1,2,\cdots,p\}
, the permutation group
S_{|A|}
acts naturally
A
:

\pi_{A}(\alpha_{k}):=\alpha_{\pi(k)}

(E.37)

where
\pi\in S_{|A|}
and
\pi_{A}\in S_{A}
.
S_{A}
is the permutation group of the set
A
(not its labels). The map
\pi\mapsto\pi_{A}
induces an isomorphism
S_{|A|}\cong S_{A}
. We want to calculate:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{1\leq i_{k},j_{k}\leq d}\prod_{k\in A}\delta_{i_{k}j_{\pi_{A}(k)}}&=\sum_{1\leq i_{k},j_{k}\leq d}\tr(\ket{i_1\cdots i_{|A|}}\bra{i_{\pi_A(1)}\cdots i_{\pi_A(|A|)}})\end{split}

(E.38)

The summands are non-zero only if all labels in the same cycle of
\pi_{A}
have the same index value. More precisely:

\sum_{1\leq i_{k},j_{k}\leq d}\tr(\ket{i_1\cdots i_{|A|}}\bra{i_{\pi_A(1)}\cdot i_{\pi_A(|A|)}})=\sum_{1\leq i_{\gamma}\leq d}\prod_{\gamma\in\pi_{A}}(\prod_{k\in\gamma}\delta_{i_{k},i_{\gamma}})=d^{\text{cycles($\pi_{A}$)}}

(E.39)

where
\gamma\in\pi_{A}
is a cycle. For simplicity of notation, we introduce the notation:

P^{A}_{\pi}:=\sum_{1\leq i_{k},j_{k}\leq d}\ket{i_1\cdots i_{|A|}}\bra{i_{\pi_A(1)}\cdots i_{\pi_A(|A|)}}

(E.40)

where
\pi_{A}\in S_{A}
. Then
\tr(P^A_\pi)=d^{\text{cycles($\pi_{A}$)}}
.

Proof.

Proof of Proposition

Now we present the calculation of the moment formula:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\varphi_{\mu\mathbb{E}\tr}&(\lambda(\mathcal{O}^{(\psi)}_{1})\cdots\lambda(\mathcal{O}^{(\psi)}_{p}))=\frac{1}{d^{p}}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\mu\mathbb{E}\tr\bigg(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{k\in A}(\sum_{a_kb_k,i_kj_k}\mathcal{O}_k^{a_kb_k}\psi^{a_k}_{i_k}\bar{\psi}^{b_k}_{j_k}\ket{i_k}\bra{j_k})\bigg)\\
&=\frac{1}{d^{p}}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\sum_{a_{k}b_{k},i_{k}j_{k}}(\prod_{k\in A}\mathcal{O}_{k}^{a_{k}b_{k}})\mu\mathbb{E}(\prod_{k\in A}\psi^{a_{k}}_{i_{k}}\bar{\psi}^{b_{k}}_{j_{k}})\tr(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{k\in A}\ket{i_k}\bra{j_k})\\
&=\frac{1}{d^{p}}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\sum_{a_{k}b_{k},i_{k}j_{k}}(\prod_{k\in A}\mathcal{O}_{k}^{a_{k}b_{k}})\mu\bigg(\sum_{\pi_{A}\in\mathcal{S}_{A}}\prod_{k\in A}\delta_{a_{k}b_{\pi_{A}(k)}}\delta_{i_{k}j_{\pi_{A}(k)}}\bigg)\bigg(\prod_{k\in A}\delta_{i_{\pi_{A}^{0}(k)}j_{k}}\bigg)\\
&=\frac{1}{d^{p}}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\sum_{\pi_{A}\in S_{A}}\mu(\sum_{a_{k}b_{k}}\prod_{k\in A}\mathcal{O}_{k}^{a_{k}b_{k}}\delta_{a_{k}b_{\pi_{A}(k)}})(\sum_{i_{k}j_{k}}\prod_{k\in A}\delta_{i_{k}j_{\pi_{A}(k)}}\delta_{i_{\pi_{A}^{0}(k)}j_{k}})\\
&=\frac{1}{d^{p}}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\sum_{\pi_{A}\in\mathcal{S}_{A}}\mu\prod_{\gamma\in\pi_{A}}\tr(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{k\in\gamma}\mathcal{O}_k)(\sum_{j_{k}}\prod_{k\in A}\delta_{j_{\pi_{A}^{0}\pi_{A}(k)}j_{k}})\\
&=\frac{1}{d^{p}}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\sum_{\pi_{A}\in\mathcal{S}_{A}}\mu\prod_{\gamma\in\pi_{A}}\tr(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{k\in\gamma}\mathcal{O}_k)\tr(P^A_{\pi_A^0\pi_A})\end{split}

(E.41)

where the product
\prod_{\gamma\in\pi_{A}}
is over the irreducible cycles in permutation
\pi_{A}
and
\pi_{A}^{0}
acts on
A:=\{\alpha_{1}<\alpha_{2}<\cdots\alpha_{|A|}:1\leq\alpha_{k}\leq p\}
by sending
\alpha_{k}
to
\alpha_{k+1}
.

By the general discussion of Poissonization, the connected component of the Poisson moment formula corresponds to the partition
\sigma=\{1,2,\cdots,p\}
. Hence, the connected correlation function reduces to
102
102

102

The constant parameter
\mu
is omitted for simplicity.
:

\frac{1}{d^{p}}\sum_{\pi\in S_{p}}\prod_{\gamma\in\pi}\tr(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{k\in\gamma}\mathcal{O}_k)d^{\text{cycles}(\pi^{0}\pi)}\qed

(E.42)

Proof.

Proof of Corollary

When
d\gg 1
, the leading contribution to the connected correlation function comes from the maximum of the combinatorial factor
d^{\text{cycles}(\pi^{0}\pi)}
. It is easy to see that the maximum is unique and it is achieved by the permutation
(\pi^{0})^{-1}=(p\cdots 21)
which is the inverse of the permutation
\pi^{0}
. For this permutation, the counting of cycles yields
d^{\text{cycles}(\pi^{0}(\pi^{0})^{-1})}=d^{p}
. Hence, the leading term of the connected correlation function is determined by
103
103

103

Again, the constant parameter
\mu
is omitted for simplicity.
:

\frac{1}{d^{p}}\tr(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{k\in(p\cdots 21)}\mathcal{O}_k)d^{p}=\tr(\mathcal{O}_p\cdots\mathcal{O}_2\mathcal{O}_1)

(E.43)

For the general correlation function, the same argument as above shows that the leading contribution is given by:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{1}{d^{p}}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\mu\tr(\overleftarrow{\prod}_{k\in A}\mathcal{O}_k)d^{\text{cycles}(\pi_{A}^{0}(\pi_{A}^{0})^{-1})}&=\frac{1}{d^{p}}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\mu^{|\sigma|}d^{\sum_{A\in\sigma}|A|}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\tr(\overleftarrow{\prod}_{k\in A}\mathcal{O}_k)\\
&=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\mu^{|\sigma|}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\tr(\overleftarrow{\prod}_{k\in A}\mathcal{O}_k)\end{split}

(E.44)

where
(\pi_{A}^{0})^{-1}
is the inverse of
\pi_{A}^{0}
and
|\sigma|
is the cardinality of the partition
\sigma
. Because
(\pi_{A}^{0})^{-1}
has only one cycle and it reverses the natural order of the set
A
, the matrix product is order-reversing
\overleftarrow{\prod}_{k\in A}
.
∎

This large-
d
correlation function is the Poisson moment formula of the Poisson algebra
\mathbb{P}_{\tr}M^{\text{op}}_{k}(\mathbb{C})

104
104

104

Ignoring the constant
\mu
.
, where
M^{\text{op}}_{k}(\mathbb{C})
is the
opposite
von Neumann algebra of
M_{k}(\mathbb{C})
. In terms of the standard form of
M_{k}(\mathbb{C})
with respect to the canonical trace, the opposite algebra
M^{\text{op}}_{k}(\mathbb{C})
can be realized as the commutant of the left representation of
M_{k}(\mathbb{C})
on the Hilbert-Schmidt space
L^{2}_{k}(\mathbb{C})
. The opposite algebra is the representation of
M_{k}(\mathbb{C})
on
L^{2}_{k}(\mathbb{C})
by
right
multiplication.

Corollary E.4
.

Consider the Poisson algebra
\mathbb{P}_{\tr}M^{\text{op}}_{k}(\mathbb{C})
, the Poisson moment formula is given by:

\varphi_{\tr}(\lambda(\mathcal{O}_{1}^{\text{op}})\cdots\lambda(\mathcal{O}^{\text{op}}_{p}))=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\tr(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{j\in A}\mathcal{O}^\text{op}_k)=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\tr(\overleftarrow{\prod}_{j\in A}\mathcal{O}_k)

(E.45)

The proof is a straightforward calculation and is omitted.

E.3
Projection to diagonal simple operators

Poissonization of simple operators will be used to model simplified JT gravity with end-of-the-world branes. To study open/closed 2D TQFTs, we need an additional projection to the diagonal matrices and consider diagonal simple operators. Recall that we have considered a unital CP map that outputs simple operators:

\Psi_{d}:L_{k}^{1}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow L^{1}(\mathbb{C}^{k},L^{1}_{d}(\mathbb{C}))

(E.46)

We now compose this channel with the projection to diagonal matrices:

\mathcal{E}_{d}:=(id\otimes P_{\text{diag}})\circ\Psi_{d}:L_{k}^{1}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow L^{1}(\mathbb{C}^{k},L_{d}^{1}(\mathbb{C}))\rightarrow L^{1}(\mathbb{C}^{k},\ell_{d}^{1}(\mathbb{C}))

(E.47)

where
P_{\text{diag}}:L^{1}_{d}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\ell^{1}_{d}(\mathbb{C})
is the projection to diagonal matrices.

\mathcal{E}_{d}(\mathcal{O})=(id\otimes P_{\text{diag}})(\mathcal{O}^{(\psi)})=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i,ab}\mathcal{O}^{ab}\psi^{a}_{i}\bar{\psi}^{b}_{i}\ket{i}\bra{i}

(E.48)

We now calculate the correlation function of Poisson quantized operators
\lambda(\mathcal{E}_{d}(\mathcal{O}))
:

Proposition E.2
.

Consider the Poisson algebra
\mathbb{P}_{\mu\mathbb{E}\tr}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}^{k},\ell_{d}^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}))
, we have the following correlation function (i.e., Poisson moment formula) of diagonal simple operators:

\varphi_{\mu\mathbb{E}\tr}(\lambda(\mathcal{E}_{d}(\mathcal{O}_{1}))\cdots\lambda(\mathcal{E}_{d}(\mathcal{O}_{p})))=\frac{1}{d^{p}}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{p}}B_{|\pi|}(\mu d)\prod_{\gamma\in\pi}\tr(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{k\in\pi}\mathcal{O}_k)

(E.49)

Moreover, the connected correlation function of diagonal simple operators is given by:

\frac{\mu d}{d^{p}}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{p}}\prod_{\gamma\in\pi}\tr(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{k\in\gamma}\mathcal{O}_k)

(E.50)

Proof.

The proof is a simple modification of the calculation for random matrices.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\varphi_{\mu\mathbb{E}\tr}&(\lambda(\mathcal{E}_{d}(\mathcal{O}_{1}))\cdots\lambda(\mathcal{E}_{d}(\mathcal{O}_{p})))=\frac{1}{d^{p}}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\mu\mathbb{E}\tr(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{k\in A}(\sum_{i_k,a_kb_k}\mathcal{O}^{a_kb_k}\psi^{a_k}_{i_k}\bar{\psi}^{b_k}_{i_k}\ket{i_k}\bra{i_k}))\\
&=\frac{1}{d^{p}}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\sum_{a_{k}b_{k},i_{k}}(\prod_{k\in A}\mathcal{O}^{a_{k}b_{k}})\mu\mathbb{E}(\prod_{k\in A}\psi^{a_{k}}_{i_{k}}\bar{\psi}^{b_{k}}_{i_{k}})\tr(\prod_{k\in A}\ket{i_k}\bra{i_k})\\
&=\frac{1}{d^{p}}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}d\sum_{a_{k}b_{k}}(\prod_{k\in A}\mathcal{O}^{a_{k}b_{k}})\mu(\sum_{\pi_{A}\in\mathcal{S}_{A}}\prod_{k\in A}\delta_{a_{k}b_{\pi_{A}(k)}})\\
&=\frac{1}{d^{p}}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\sum_{\pi_{A}\in\mathcal{S}_{A}}(\mu d)\prod_{\gamma\in\pi_{A}}\tr(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{k\in\gamma}\mathcal{O}_k)\\
&=\frac{1}{d^{p}}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{p}}B_{|\pi|}(\mu d)\prod_{\gamma\in\pi}\tr(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{k\in\gamma}\mathcal{O}_k)\end{split}

(E.51)

where the last equation follows from the combinatorial lemma.

To extract the connected correlation function, we simply focus on the contribution of the trivial partition
\{1,2,\cdots,p\}
. The corresponding summand is given by:

\frac{\mu d}{d^{p}}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{p}}\prod_{\gamma\in\pi}\tr(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{k\in\gamma}\mathcal{O}_k)\qed

(E.52)

Recall that for the Poisson algebra of random simple operators, the large-
d
limit of the correlation function is the correlation function of the Poisson algebra of non-random matrices. In particular, the large-
d
connected correlation function is given by a single trace
\tr(\mathcal{O}_p\cdots\mathcal{O}_2\mathcal{O}_1)
. As a comparison, for the Poisson algebra of random diagonal matrices, the large-
d
limit of the correlation function is given by a product of traces.

Corollary E.5
.

The leading contribution to the large-
d
limit of the correlation function is given by:

\mu^{p}\prod_{1\leq k\leq p}\tr(\mathcal{O}_k)

(E.53)

Proof.

When
d\rightarrow\infty
, the correlation function is dominated by the singleton partition:
\sigma_{\text{singleton}}:=\{\{1\},\{2\},\cdots,\{p\}\}
. Because each component of this partition is a singleton set, the permutation group associated with each component is trivial. Therefore, from the calculation in the Proposition, we have the following limit:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lim_{d\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{d^{p}}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\sum_{\pi_{A}\in\mathcal{S}_{A}}(\mu d)\prod_{\gamma\in\pi_{A}}\tr(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{k\in\gamma}\mathcal{O}_k)=\mu^{p}\prod_{1\leq k\leq d}\tr(\mathcal{O}_k)\qed\end{split}

(E.54)

Notice that in the limit
d\rightarrow\infty
, the finite-
d
connected correlation function of diagonal simple operators vanishes. Only the totally disconnected contribution to the full correlation function survives. This is in complete contrast with the correlation function of general simple operators, where the connected contributions survive in the large-
d
limit.

Because the diagonal simple operators mutually commute, we can directly calculate the moment generating function. Physically, this generating function should be understood as a partition function. The correlation function can be derived from the partition function.

Proposition E.3
.

The moment generating function of diagonal simple operators under the Poisson state is given by:

\varphi_{\mu\mathbb{E}\tr}(e^{u\lambda(\mathcal{E}_{d}(\mathcal{O}))})=\exp(\frac{\mu d}{\det(\mathbbm{1} - \frac{u}{d}T)} - \mu d)

(E.55)

where
u<0
is a negative constant.

Proof.

The proof is a simple calculation of the Gaussian integral.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\varphi_{\mu\mathbb{E}\tr}(e^{u\lambda(\mathcal{E}_{d}(\mathcal{O}))})&=\exp(\mu\mathbb{E}\tr(e^{u\mathcal{E}_d(\mathcal{O})} - \mathbbm{1}))\\
&=\exp(\mu\mathbb{E}\tr(\sum_ie^{\frac{u}{d}\sum_{ab}\mathcal{O}^{ab}\psi^a_i\bar{\psi}^b_i}\ket{i}\bra{i} - \mathbbm{1}))\\
&=\prod_{i}\exp(\mu\mathbb{E}(e^{\frac{u}{d}\sum_{ab}\mathcal{O}^{ab}\psi^a_i\bar{\psi}^b_i} - 1))\end{split}

(E.56)

The exponent is a Gaussian integral:

\mathbb{E}(e^{\frac{u}{d}\sum_{ab}\mathcal{O}^{ab}\psi^{a}_{i}\bar{\psi}^{b}_{i}})=\int\big(\prod_{a}\frac{d\psi^{a}_{i}d\bar{\psi}^{a}_{i}}{\pi}e^{-|\psi^{a}_{i}|^{2}}\big)e^{\frac{u}{d}\sum_{ab}\mathcal{O}^{ab}\psi^{a}_{i}\bar{\psi}^{b}_{i}}=\frac{1}{\det(\mathbbm{1} - \frac{u}{d}T)}

(E.57)

where the determinant is taken over
k\times k
-matrices. Therefore, we have:

\varphi_{\mu\mathbb{E}\tr}(e^{u\lambda(\mathcal{E}_{d}(\mathcal{O}))})=\prod_{d}\exp(\frac{\mu}{\det(\mathbbm{1} - \frac{u}{d}T)} - \mu)=\exp(\frac{\mu d}{\det(\mathbbm{1} - \frac{u}{d}T)} - \mu d)\qed

(E.58)

From the perspective of 2D open/closed TQFT, it is not surprising that its correlation function can be reproduced by a commutative Poisson algebra. Recall that the crucial bordism in a 2D open/closed TQFT is the so-called open-to-closed bordism. Geometrically, this bordism folds and glues the two endpoints of an open boundary. In the canonical form of a 2D open/closed bordism
Lauda-Pfeiffer
, each open boundary connects with the bulk manifold through an open-to-closed bordism, and the bulk manifold only has closed boundaries.

In terms of a knowledgeable Frobenius algebra, this open-to-closed bordism is represented by a projection to a commutative subalgebra. Therefore, the correlation function of a 2D open/closed TQFT is calculated by first projecting the (possibly noncommutative) operators associated with open boundaries to a commutative subalgebra. From this perspective, it is not surprising that the 2D open/closed TQFT correlation function is modeled by the Poisson moment formula of a commutative Poisson algebra.

E.4
Projection to central simple operators

In addition to the projection to diagonal simple operators, we can also go one step further and project to the central simple operators. This will yield a slightly different partition function and correlation functions. The central projection is given by the normalized trace:

\tau_{d}:=(id\otimes\frac{\tr}{d})\circ\Psi_{d}:L^{1}_{k}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow L^{1}(\mathbb{C}^{k},L^{1}_{d}(\mathbb{C}))\rightarrow L^{1}(\mathbb{C}^{k},\mathbb{C}\mathbbm{1})

(E.59)

where the normalized trace
\frac{\tr}{d}
maps
L^{1}_{d}(\mathbb{C})
to its center which is isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}
.

\overline{\mathcal{O}^{(\psi)}}:=\tau_{d}(\mathcal{O})=(id\otimes\frac{\tr}{d})(\mathcal{O}^{(\psi)})=\frac{1}{d^{2}}(\sum_{i,ab}\mathcal{O}^{ab}\psi^{a}_{i}\bar{\psi}^{b}_{i})\mathbbm{1}

(E.60)

Notice that the channel
\tau_{d}
preserves following two states:

\mathbb{E}\otimes\tr(\overline{\mathcal{O}^{(\psi)}})=\frac{1}{d^{2}}\sum_{i,ab}\mathcal{O}^{ab}\mathbb{E}(\psi^{a}_{i}\bar{\psi}^{b}_{i})\tr(\mathbbm{1})=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i}\sum_{ab}\mathcal{O}^{ab}\delta_{ab}=\tr(\mathcal{O})

(E.61)

We directly calculate the moment generating function.

Proposition E.4
.

Consider the Poisson algebra
\mathbb{P}_{\mu\mathbb{E}\tr}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}^{k},\mathbb{C}\mathbbm{1})
, we have the moment generating function:

\varphi_{\mu\mathbb{E}\tr}(e^{u\lambda(\overline{\mathcal{O}^{(\psi)}})})=\exp(\frac{\mu d}{\det(1 - \frac{u}{d^2}T)^d} - \mu d)

(E.62)

where
u<0
is a real constant.

Proof.

This is essentially the same calculation as the diagonal simple operators.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\varphi_{\mu\mathbb{E}\tr}(e^{u\lambda(\overline{\mathcal{O}^{(\psi)}})})&=\exp(\mu\mathbb{E}\tr(e^{u\overline{\mathcal{O}^{(\psi)}}} - \mathbbm{1}))=\exp(\mu\mathbb{E}(e^{\frac{u}{d^2}\sum_{i,ab}\mathcal{O}^{ab}\psi^a_i\bar{\psi}^b_i} - 1)\tr(\mathbbm{1}))\\
&=\exp(\mu d\mathbb{E}(e^{\frac{u}{d^2}\sum_{i,ab}\mathcal{O}^{ab}\psi^a_i\bar{\psi}^b_i} - 1))\end{split}

(E.63)

The exponent is a Gaussian integral:

\mathbb{E}(e^{\frac{u}{d^{2}}\sum_{i,a,b}\mathcal{O}^{ab}\psi^{a}_{i}\overline{\psi}^{b}_{i}})=\int\bigg(\prod_{i,a}\frac{d\psi^{a}_{i}d\bar{\psi}^{a}_{i}}{\pi}e^{-|\psi^{a}_{i}|^{2}}\bigg)e^{\frac{u}{d^{2}}\sum_{i,a,b}\mathcal{O}^{ab}\psi^{a}_{i}\bar{\psi}^{b}_{i}}

(E.64)

Note that this integral converges when
u<0
. This Gaussian integral is a product of
d
identical Gaussian integrals:

\mathbb{E}(e^{\frac{u}{d^{2}}\sum_{i,a,b}\mathcal{O}^{ab}\psi^{a}_{i}\bar{\psi}^{b}_{i}})=\prod_{1\leq i\leq d}\int\bigg(\prod_{a}\frac{d\psi^{a}_{i}d\bar{\psi}^{a}_{i}}{\pi}e^{-|\psi^{a}_{i}|^{2}}\bigg)e^{\frac{u}{d^{2}}\sum_{ab}\mathcal{O}^{ab}\psi^{a}_{i}\bar{\psi}^{b}_{i}}

(E.65)

And a simple calculation gives:

\int\bigg(\prod_{a}\frac{d\psi^{a}_{i}d\bar{\psi}^{a}_{i}}{\pi}e^{-|\psi^{a}_{i}|^{2}}\bigg)e^{\frac{u}{d^{2}}\sum_{ab}\mathcal{O}^{ab}\psi^{a}_{i}\bar{\psi}^{b}_{i}}=\frac{1}{\det(1 - \frac{u}{d^2}T)}

(E.66)

where the determinant is taken over
k\times k
-matrices. Therefore, we have:

\varphi_{\mu\mathbb{E}\tr}(e^{u\lambda(\overline{\mathcal{O}^{(\psi)}})})=\exp(\frac{\mu d}{\det(1 - \frac{u}{d^2}T)^d} - \mu d)\qed

(E.67)

Compared with the partition function for diagonal simple operators, the only difference is in the determinant factor.

We can derive the correlation function of central simple operators from this partition function. Alternatively, we can calculate the correlation function directly:

Proposition E.5
.

The correlation function of central simple operators is given by:

\varphi_{\mu\mathbb{E}\tr}(\lambda(\overline{\mathcal{O}^{(\psi)}_{1}})\cdots\lambda(\overline{\mathcal{O}_{p}^{(\psi)}})=\frac{1}{d^{2p}}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{p}}B_{|\pi|}(\mu d)\prod_{\gamma\in\pi}d\tr(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{k\in\gamma}\mathcal{O}_k)

(E.68)

Proof.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\varphi_{\mu\mathbb{E}\tr}&(\lambda(\overline{\mathcal{O}^{(\psi)}_{1}})\cdots\lambda(\overline{\mathcal{O}^{(\psi)}_{p}}))=\frac{1}{d^{2p}}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\mu\mathbb{E}\tr(\prod_{k\in A}(\sum_{i_k,a_kb_k}\mathcal{O}^{a_kb_k}_k\psi^{a_k}_{i_k}\bar{\psi}^{b_k}_{i_k})\mathbbm{1})\\
&=\frac{1}{d^{2p}}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\sum_{i_{k},a_{k}b_{k}}(\prod_{k\in A}\mathcal{O}^{a_{k}b_{k}}_{k})\mu\mathbb{E}(\prod_{k\in A}\psi^{a_{k}}_{i_{k}}\bar{\psi}^{b_{k}}_{i_{k}})\tr(\mathbbm{1})\\
&=\frac{1}{d^{2p}}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\sum_{i_{k},a_{k}b_{k}}(\prod_{k\in A}\mathcal{O}^{a_{k}b_{k}}_{k})\mu(\sum_{\pi_{A}\in\mathcal{S}_{A}}\prod_{k\in A}\delta_{a_{k}b_{\pi_{A}(k)}}\delta_{i_{k}i_{\pi_{A}(k)}})\tr(\mathbbm{1})\\
&=\frac{1}{d^{2p}}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\sum_{\pi_{A}\in\mathcal{S}_{A}}\mu\prod_{\gamma\in\pi_{A}}\tr(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{k\in\gamma}\mathcal{O}_k)(\sum_{i_{k}}\prod_{k\in A}\delta_{i_{k}i_{\pi_{A}(k)}})\tr(\mathbbm{1})\\
&=\frac{1}{d^{2p}}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\sum_{\pi_{A}\in\mathcal{S}_{A}}\mu\big(\prod_{\gamma\in\pi_{A}}\tr(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{k\in\gamma}\mathcal{O}_k)\big)d^{1+\text{cycles}(\pi_{A})}\\
&=\frac{1}{d^{2p}}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\sum_{\pi_{A}\in\mathcal{S}_{A}}(\mu d)\big(\prod_{\gamma\in\pi_{A}}d\tr(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{k\in\gamma}\mathcal{O}_k)\big)\\
&=\frac{1}{d^{2p}}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{p}}B_{|\pi|}(\mu d)\prod_{\gamma\in\pi}d\tr(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{k\in\gamma}\mathcal{O}_k)\end{split}

(E.69)

where the last equation follows from the combinatorial lemma.
∎

Corollary E.6
.

In the large-
d
limit, the correlation function of central simple operators reduces to:

\mu^{p}\prod_{1\leq k\leq p}\tr(\mathcal{O}_k)

(E.70)

Proof.

For each partition
\sigma
and each set of permutations
(\pi_{A})_{A\in\sigma}
, the
d
-dependent factor is given by
d^{|\sigma|+\sum_{A\in\sigma}\text{cycles}(\pi_{A})}
. Because
|\sigma|\leq p
and
\sum_{A\in\sigma}\text{cycles}(\pi_{A})\leq\sum_{A\in\sigma}|A|=p
. Both inequalities achieve a maximum when the partition
\sigma
is the singleton partition and the set of partitions is trivial. In this case, we have:

\lim_{d\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{d^{2p}}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{p}}B_{|\pi|}(\mu d)\prod_{\gamma\in\pi}d\tr(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{k\in\gamma}\mathcal{O}_k)=\mu^{p}\prod_{1\leq k\leq p}\tr(\mathcal{O}_k)\qed

(E.71)

Notice that this is the same large-
d
limit as the correlation function of the diagonal simple operators.

E.5
Simplified JT gravity with End-of-the-World branes

In this section, we apply Poissonization to study simplified JT gravity with end-of-the-world branes
Penington:2019kki
; the example discussed in Section
6
. In particular, we show that the correlation function of particular (reduced) density matrices calculated in
Penington:2019kki
is the Poisson moment formula of random matrices. This demonstration is largely an exercise of matching notations. We will not discuss the physical meaning of the density matrices considered in
Penington:2019kki
since this is well explained in the original paper and in the main body of the text.

We state the observation as the following corollary to Proposition
E.1
.

Corollary E.7
.

Consider a particular diagonal
d\times d
-matrix:

\mathcal{O}(\beta):=\sum_{i}2^{1-2\mu}|\Gamma(\mu-\frac{1}{2}+i\sqrt{2E_{i}})|^{2}e^{-\beta E_{i}}\ket{i}\bra{i}

(E.72)

where
\mu>0
is a constant parameter (tension of the brane)
105
105

105

This is not to be confused with the
\mu
introduced earlier for the normalization of the weight.
and the set
\{E_{i}\}
are the eigen-energies and
\ket{i}
is the corresponding energy eigen-state
106
106

106

Here for simplicity of notation, we assume there is no degeneracy. Each energy
E_{i}
corresponds to a distinct state
\ket{i}
. Our discussion can accommodate degeneracy with only changes in notations.
. Then, the Poisson moment formula for the Poisson quantized random operators
\lambda(\mathcal{O}(\beta)_{(\psi)})
is given by (c.f. Equation
E.19
):

\varphi_{\mathbb{E}\tr}(\lambda(\mathcal{O}(\beta_{1})_{(\psi)})\cdots\lambda(\mathcal{O}(\beta_{p})_{\psi}))=\frac{1}{k^{p}}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\sum_{\pi_{A}\in S_{A}}k^{\text{cycles}(\pi_{A}^{0}\pi_{A})}\prod_{\gamma\in\pi_{A}}\tr(\prod_{j\in\gamma}\mathcal{O}(\beta_j))

(E.73)

where recall
\pi^{0}_{A}
is the cyclic permutation as explained in the proof of Proposition F.1.

This moment formula contains both connected and disconnected contributions. Since connected correlation function corresponds to the trivial partition
\{1,2,\cdots,p\}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}
, the connected correlation function is given by:

\mathbb{E}\tr(\mathcal{O}(\beta_1)_{(\psi)}\cdots\mathcal{O}(\beta_p)_{(\psi)})=\frac{1}{k^{p}}\sum_{\pi\in S_{p}}\prod_{\gamma\in\pi}\tr(\prod_{j\in\gamma}\mathcal{O}(\beta_k))k^{\text{cycles}(\pi^{0}\pi)}

(E.74)

where again
\pi_{0}
is the cyclic permutation as explained in the proof of Proposition F.1.

We now match notation with the original text (see also Appendix D
Penington:2019kki
). Recall the key (reduced) density operator
107
107

107

The original paper assumes a pure state on the composite system of a black hole and its Hawking radiation and considers its reduction to the radiation subsystem.
considered in
Penington:2019kki
is given by (using the original notation):

\rho(\beta):=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{1\leq a,b\leq k}\bra{\psi_b(\beta)}\ket{\psi_a(\beta)}\ket{a}\bra{b}=\tr_{B}\bigg((\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}\sum_{a}\ket{\psi_a(\beta)}_{B}\ket{a}_{R})(\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}\sum_{j}\bra{\psi_b(\beta)}_{B}\bra{b}_{R})\bigg)

(E.75)

where the partial trace is over the gravitational component
B

108
108

108

Following the original notation in
Penington:2019kki
,
B
stands for black hole.
. In terms of the original notation of
Penington:2019kki
, the pure state
\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}\sum_{a}\ket{\psi_a(\beta)}_{B}\ket{a}_{R}
is denoted as
\ket{\Psi}
(see also Section 2
Penington:2019kki
).

Using the definition of
\ket{\psi_a(\beta)}
, we have (see also Equations D.2 and D.3 in
Penington:2019kki
):

\rho(\beta)=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{1\leq a,b\leq k}\sum_{1\leq i\leq d}2^{1-2\mu}|\Gamma(\mu-\frac{1}{2}+i\sqrt{2E_{i}})|^{2}e^{-\beta E_{i}}\psi^{a}_{i}\bar{\psi}^{b}_{i}\ket{a}\bra{b}

(E.76)

where
\psi_{i}^{a}
’s are standard complex normal random variables
109
109

109

In terms of the original notation,
\psi_{i}^{a}
is denoted as
C_{i,a}
.
. The authors are interested in calculating the
connected
correlation function
110
110

110

In the language of JT gravity with EOW branes, this connected correlation function calculates the p-point function of p open boundaries with lengths
\{\beta_{m}\}_{1\leq m\leq p}
. Each open boundary is bounded by EOW branes. For simplicity, we have not included possible contributions from closed boundaries.
:

\mathbb{E}\tr(\rho(\beta_1)\cdots\rho(\beta_p))=\frac{1}{k^{p}}\sum_{ab}\tr(\overrightarrow{\prod_{1\leq m\leq p}}\ket{a_m}\bra{b_m})\mathbb{E}(\prod_{1\leq m\leq p}\bra{\psi_{b_m}(\beta_m)}\ket{\psi_{a_m}(\beta_m)})

(E.77)

The Equations D.2 and D.3 in
Penington:2019kki
calculates the random expectation part of this correlation function, namely
\mathbb{E}(\prod_{1\leq m\leq p}\bra{\psi_{b_m}(\beta_m)}\ket{\psi_{a_m}(\beta_m)})
. Plug in the result derived in
Penington:2019kki
to the equation above, we can express the connected correlation function as:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}\tr(\rho(\beta_1)\cdots\rho(\beta_p))=\frac{1}{k^{p}}\sum_{a_{m}b_{m}}\tr(\overrightarrow{\prod_{1\leq m\leq p}}\ket{a_m}\bra{b_m})\\
&\times\sum_{\pi\in S_{p}}\bigg[(\prod_{m}\delta_{a_{m}b_{\pi(m)}})\prod_{\gamma\in\pi}\tr(\prod_{l\in\gamma}\bigg[\sum_i 2^{1-2\mu}e^{-\beta_kE_i}|\Gamma(\mu- \frac{1}{2} + i\sqrt{2E_i})|^2\ket{i}\bra{i}\bigg])\bigg]\\
&=\frac{1}{k^{p}}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{p}}\bigg[\sum_{a_{m}b_{m}}\prod_{m}\delta_{a_{\pi^{0}(m)}b_{m}}\prod_{m}\delta_{a_{\pi(m)}b_{m}}\bigg]\\
&\times\bigg[\prod_{\gamma\in\pi}\tr(\prod_{l\in\gamma}\bigg[\sum_i 2^{1-2\mu}e^{-\beta_kE_i}|\Gamma(\mu- \frac{1}{2} + i\sqrt{2E_i})|^2\ket{i}\bra{i}\bigg])\bigg]\\
&=\frac{1}{k^{p}}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{p}}\prod_{\gamma\in\pi}\tr(\prod_{l\in\gamma}\bigg[\sum_i 2^{1-2\mu}e^{-\beta_kE_i}|\Gamma(\mu- \frac{1}{2} + i\sqrt{2E_i})|^2\ket{i}\bra{i}\bigg])k^{\text{cycles}(\pi^{0}\pi)}\end{split}

(E.78)

In the first equation, we simply plug in the results derived in
Penington:2019kki
. In the second equation, we used the fact that
\tr(\overrightarrow{\prod}_m\ket{a_m}\bra{b_m} = \delta_{a_{\pi_0(m)}b_m})
where
\pi_{0}
is the cyclic permutation
(12\cdots p)\in\mathcal{S}_{p}
.

In our framework, the density operator
\rho(\beta)
is modeled by a Poisson quantized operator:
\lambda(\mathcal{O}(\beta)_{(\psi)})
111
111

111

In fact, Equation D.10 of
Penington:2019kki
precisely defines the CPTP map
\mathcal{O}(\beta)\mapsto\mathcal{O}(\beta)_{(\psi)}
. This is the process of ”dressing” open boundary operators with end-point Gaussian random variables. The new feature added by Poissonization is to consider the Poisson quantized operator
\lambda(\mathcal{O}(\beta)_{(\psi)})
instead of
\mathcal{O}(\beta)_{(\psi)}
. Observe that we have defined:

\mathcal{O}(\beta_{k})=\sum_{i}2^{1-2\mu}e^{-\beta_{k}E_{i}}|\Gamma(\mu-\frac{1}{2}+i\sqrt{2E_{i}})|^{2}\ket{i}\bra{i}

(E.79)

Therefore, in our notation, the connected correlation function derived in
Penington:2019kki
can be expressed as:

\mathbb{E}\tr(\mathcal{O}(\beta_1)_{(\psi)}\cdots\mathcal{O}(\beta_p)_{(\psi)})=\frac{1}{k^{p}}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{p}}\prod_{\gamma\in\pi}\tr(\prod_{l\in\gamma}\mathcal{O}(\beta_l))k^{\text{cycles}(\pi^{0}\pi)}

(E.80)

This is exactly the same expression derived using Poissonization (c.f. Equation
E.20
). Since the general correlation function is a summation over products of connected correlation functions, the Poisson moment formula correctly reproduces the correlation functions of simplified JT gravity with EOW branes.

In the main text of
Penington:2019kki
, the authors considered the planar limit of JT gravity with EOW branes (c.f. Section 2.5 of
Penington:2019kki
). The planar limit is achieved by taking both
k
and
d\sim e^{S_{0}}
to infinity. In Poissonization, these two limits have distinct effects. When
k\rightarrow\infty
, the only surviving term in the connected correlation function corresponds to the cyclic permutation
(\pi^{0})^{-1}
. The connected correlation function becomes:

\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\tr(\mathcal{O}(\beta_1)_{(\psi)}\cdots\mathcal{O}(\beta_p)_{(\psi)})=\tr\left(\prod_{1\leq k\leq p}\mathcal{O}(\beta_{k})\right)=\sum_{i}\prod_{1\leq k\leq p}\left(2^{1-2\mu}e^{-\beta_{k}E_{i}}|\Gamma(\mu-\frac{1}{2}+i\sqrt{2E_{i}})|^{2}\right)

(E.81)

We can rewrite this equation in terms of spectral density. Following
Penington:2019kki
, we introduce a new variable
s>0
such that
\frac{s^{2}}{2}=E
. Then in terms of a density function
\rho_{\text{spec}}(s)

112
112

112

In the original text
Penington:2019kki
, the spectral density is simply denoted as
\rho(s)
. Here we added the subscript to emphasize that
\rho_{\text{spec}}(s)
is the spectral density while
\rho(\beta)
is the density matrix.
, we have:

\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\tr(\mathcal{O}(\beta)_{(\psi)}^p)=\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\rho_{\text{spec}}(s)y_{\beta}(s)^{p}

(E.82)

where
y_{\beta}(s):=2^{1-2\mu}e^{-\beta s^{2}/2}|\Gamma(\mu-\frac{1}{2}+is)|^{2}
(c.f. Equation 2.32
Penington:2019kki
). Here
\rho_{\text{spec}}(s)=\sum_{i}\delta(s-\sqrt{2E_{i}})
.

Now taking the limit
d\sim e^{S_{0}}\rightarrow\infty
, all genus creation is suppressed. The spectral density reduces to the leading term:

\rho_{0}(s)=\frac{s}{2\pi^{2}}\sinh(2\pi s)

(E.83)

In this case, the connected correlation function becomes:

\lim_{k,d\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\tr(\mathcal{O}(\beta)_{(\psi)}^p)=\lim_{k,d\rightarrow\infty}\tr\left(\mathcal{O}(\beta)^{p}\right)=\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\rho_{0}(s)y_{\beta}(s)^{p}=Z_{p}

(E.84)

It is easy to see that Equation
E.84
is precisely the formula of the planar connected correlation function derived in
Penington:2019kki
(c.f. Equation 2.32). The notation
Z_{p}
follows from the original text
Penington:2019kki
and it denotes the connected correlation function with
p
asymptotic boundaries and disk bulk topology.

E.6
Open/Closed 2D TQFTs

In this section, we apply Poissonization to study open/closed 2D TQFT as defined by
@Moore-Segal
;
banerjee2022comments
;
gardiner20212D
. In particular, we show that the correlation function of open/closed 2D TQFT is a Poisson moment formula.

To compare our calculation with the results of
banerjee2022comments
, recall the n-point correlation function (i.e., the bordism mapping n open boundaries to
\mathbb{C}
) derived in
banerjee2022comments
is given by (see also Equation 4.13 of
banerjee2022comments
):

\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{open}}(n,0):\mathcal{O}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{O}_{n}\mapsto\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}B_{r(\sigma)}(\lambda)e^{\lambda}\prod_{c\in\text{cycl}(\sigma)}\mu^{-1}\tr(\mathcal{O}_c)

(E.85)

where
r(\sigma)=|\sigma|
is the number of cycles in
\sigma
,
\text{cycl}(\sigma)
is the set of irreducible cycles of
\sigma
,
\mathcal{O}_{c}=\overrightarrow{\prod}_{k\in c}\mathcal{O}_{k}
is the ordered product of matrices with labels in the cycle
c
. Translating to our notation, this n-point correlation function can be written as:

\overline{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{open}}(n,0)(\mathcal{O}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{O}_{n})=\sum_{\pi\in S_{n}}B_{|\pi|}(\mu)e^{\mu}\prod_{\gamma\in\pi}\alpha^{-1}\tr(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{k\in\gamma}\mathcal{O}_k)

(E.86)

When
\frac{1}{\alpha}
is an integer, this correlation function is a Poisson moment formula:

\frac{e^{\mu}}{\mu^{2p}}\varphi_{\mu\alpha\mathbb{E}\tr}(\lambda(\overline{\mathcal{O}^{(\psi)}_{1}})\cdots\lambda(\overline{\mathcal{O}^{(\psi)}_{n}}))=\overline{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{open}}(n,0)(\mathcal{O}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{O}_{n})

(E.87)

To check this, we simply plug in the Poisson moment formula for central simple operators and define
d=\frac{1}{\mu}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{e^{\mu}}{\alpha^{2p}}\varphi_{\mu\alpha\mathbb{E}\tr}(\lambda(\overline{\mathcal{O}^{(\psi)}_{1}})\cdots\lambda(\overline{\mathcal{O}^{(\psi)}_{n}}))&=\frac{e^{\mu}}{\alpha^{2p}}\frac{1}{d^{2p}}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{p}}B_{|\pi|}(\mu\alpha d)\prod_{\gamma\in\pi}d\tr(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{k\in\gamma}\mathcal{O}_k)\\
&=e^{\mu}\sum_{\pi}B_{|\pi|}(\mu)\prod_{\gamma}\alpha^{-1}\tr(\overrightarrow{\prod}_k \mathcal{O}_k)\end{split}

(E.88)

This reproduces the n-point correlation function of an open/closed 2D TQFT.

So far, we have considered the case where
\frac{1}{\alpha}
is a positive integer. To consider the general case, we need to replace the standard normal random variable
\psi^{a}_{i}
with complex free field
\psi^{a}(x)
with two-point function
113
113

113

Rigorously, Dirac delta function cannot be used to construct Gaussian free fields. One has to use other kernel functions to approximate Dirac delta function. We have omitted this limiting procedure here and focus on presenting the main calculation.
:

\mathbb{E}(\psi^{a}(x)\bar{\psi}^{b}(y))=\delta_{ab}\delta(x,y)

(E.89)

With the Gaussian free fields, we consider a modified central simple operator:

\widetilde{\overline{\mathcal{O}^{(\psi)}}}:=\sum_{ab}\int_{0}^{1/\alpha}dx\mathcal{O}^{ab}\psi^{a}(x)\bar{\psi^{b}}(x)

(E.90)

Note that in this modified central simple operator, we have taken
d=1
. Then we can calculate the correlation function of the Poisson quantized modified central simple operators:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\varphi_{\mu\mathbb{E}}&(\lambda(\widetilde{\overline{\mathcal{O}^{(\psi)}_{1}}})\cdots\lambda(\widetilde{\overline{\mathcal{O}^{(\psi)}}_{p}}))=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\mu\mathbb{E}(\prod_{k\in A}(\sum_{a_{k}b_{k}}\int_{0}^{1/\alpha}dx_{k}\mathcal{O}^{a_{k}b_{k}}_{k}\psi^{a_{k}}(x_{k})\bar{\psi}^{b_{k}}(x_{k})))\\
&=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\sum_{\{a_{k}b_{k}\}}(\prod_{k}\int_{0}^{1/\alpha}dx_{k})(\prod_{k\in A}\mathcal{O}^{a_{k}b_{k}}_{k})\mu\mathbb{E}(\prod_{k\in A}\psi^{a_{k}}(x_{k})\bar{\psi}^{b_{k}}(x_{k}))\\
&=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\sum_{\{a_{k}b_{k}\}}(\prod_{k}\int_{0}^{1/\alpha}dx_{k})(\prod_{k}\mathcal{O}^{a_{k}b_{k}}_{k})\mu(\sum_{\pi_{A}\in\mathcal{S}_{A}}\prod_{k\in A}\delta_{a_{k}b_{\pi_{A}(k)}}\delta(x_{k},x_{\pi_{A}(k)}))\\
&=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\sum_{\pi_{A}\in\mathcal{S}_{A}}\mu(\prod_{\gamma\in\pi_{A}}\tr(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{k\in A}\mathcal{O}_k))(\int_{0}^{1/\alpha}\cdots\int_{0}^{1/\alpha}dx_{1}dx_{2}\cdots dx_{p}\prod_{k}\delta(x_{k},x_{\pi_{A}(k)}))\\
&=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\sum_{\pi_{A}\in\mathcal{S}_{A}}\mu(\prod_{\gamma\in\pi_{A}}\tr(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{k\in A}\mathcal{O}_k))\alpha^{-\text{cycles}(\pi_{A})}=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\mu(\prod_{\gamma\in\pi_{A}}\alpha^{-1}\tr(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{k\in\gamma}\mathcal{O}_k))\\
&=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{p}}B_{|\pi|}(\mu)\prod_{\gamma}\alpha^{-1}\tr(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{k\in\gamma}\mathcal{O}_k)\end{split}

(E.91)

where the last equation follows from the combinatorial lemma. Therefore, the correlation function of open/closed 2D TQFT is a Poisson moment function:

e^{\mu}\varphi_{\mu\mathbb{E}}(\lambda(\widetilde{\overline{\mathcal{O}_{1}^{(\psi)}}})\cdots\lambda(\widetilde{\overline{\mathcal{O}_{n}^{(\psi)}}}))=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}e^{\mu}B_{|\pi|}(\mu)\prod_{\gamma}\alpha^{-1}\tr(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{k\in\gamma}\mathcal{O}_k)

(E.92)

E.7
Marolf-Maxfield’s topological gravity with EOW branes

In this section, we apply Poissonization to study the Marolf-Maxfield model of topological gravity with end-of-the-world brane
MM
. In particular, we show that the correlation function of this toy model is the moment formula of Poisson quantized central simple operators.

Recall that in the Marolf-Maxfield model, the moment generating function is given by (see also Equation 3.34
MM
):

\langle\exp(u\widehat{Z} + \sum_{1\leq i,j \leq k}t_{ij}\widehat{(\psi_j, \psi_i)})\rangle=\exp(\lambda\frac{e^u}{\det(1 - t)})

(E.93)

Here we have copied the original notations.

We use Poissonization of central simple operators with
d=1
to reproduce this moment generating function. Recall that when
d=1
, we have (see also Appendix
E.4
):

\varphi_{\mu\mathbb{E}}(e^{\lambda(\overline{\mathcal{O}^{(\psi)}})})=\exp(\frac{\mu}{\det(1 - \mathcal{O})}-\mu)

(E.94)

In this case, the operator
\overline{\mathcal{O}^{(\psi)}}
is given by:
\sum_{ab}\mathcal{O}^{ab}\psi^{ab}
. Thus, the moment generating function of the Marolf-Maxfield model is given by:

e^{\mu}\varphi_{\mu\mathbb{E}}(e^{u\lambda(1)+\lambda(\overline{\mathcal{O}^{(\psi)}})})=e^{\mu}\exp(\mu\mathbb{E}(e^ue^{\overline{\mathcal{O}^{(\psi)}}} - 1))=\exp(\mu\frac{e^u}{\det(1 - \mathcal{O})})

(E.95)

where
u
is a real parameter and
\lambda(1)
is the Poisson quantization of identity operator. In the first equation, we have used the fact that
[\lambda(1),\lambda(\overline{\mathcal{O}^{(\psi)}})]=\lambda([1,\overline{\mathcal{O}^{(\psi)}}])=0
and hence
e^{u\lambda(1)+\lambda(\overline{\mathcal{O}^{(\psi)}})}=e^{u\lambda(1)}e^{\lambda(\overline{\mathcal{O}^{\psi}})}
.

Expanding the moment generating function in terms of the eigenvectors of
\lambda(1)
(the number operator), the moment generating function can be written as:

e^{\mu}\sum_{n\geq 0}e^{un}\frac{e^{-\mu}\mu^{n}}{n!}\left(\frac{1}{\det(1 - \mathcal{O})}\right)^{n}=e^{\mu}\sum_{n\geq 0}e^{un}p_{n}(\mu)\left\langle\exp\left(\sum_{a,b}\mathcal{O}^{ab}\psi^{ab}\right)\right\rangle_{\lambda(1)=n}

(E.96)

This is exactly Equation 3.37 in
MM
.

Appendix F
Probability theory and operator algebra appendices

F.1
Algebraic formulation of classical probability theory

In this appendix, we review how to reformulate classical probability theory in an algebraic language. This is the basis for the theory of commutative von Neumann algebras. The key idea is to study the properties of the underlying probability space via the algebra of (measurable) functions on this space. Throughout, we use a Bernoulli random variable as a concrete example.

Mathematically, any random variable
X
is a measurable function from the space of samples
\Omega
to a measurable space (typically
\mathbb{R}
for real-valued random variables or discrete spaces such as
\{0,1\}
in the case of the Bernoulli random variable). The space of samples comes equipped with a probability measure
\mu
, and the random variable
X

pushes forward
the probability measure to the target measurable space. This push-forward probability measure on the target space
X_{*}\mu
is the
law of
X
.

In general, to study the properties of the law of a random variable, it is unimportant what the underlying sample space is. This is because the sample space is not unique. However, any random variable uniquely specifies a target space and a probability distribution on the target space. For example, a real-valued standard normal random variable has
\mathbb{R}
as its target space and the Gaussian distribution as its law. However, the underlying sample space can be any set of experimental outcomes that follows the normal distribution. To study the law of a random variable, it suffices to study the probability distribution and its effect on measurable functions on the target space.

In the case of a Bernoulli random variable, the target space is a discrete two-point set
\{0,1\}
(with discrete topology). On this discrete space, a generic measurable function is given by two complex numbers
\alpha_{i}
(
i=0,1
):

\alpha_{0}\delta_{0}+\alpha_{1}\delta_{1}

(F.1)

Suppose the Bernoulli random variable takes value 1 (i.e., the positive event occurs) with probability
p\in[0,1]
. Then we can define a state on the space of (essentially bounded) measurable functions on the two-point space
\ell_{\infty}^{2}
:

\omega_{p}(\alpha_{0}\delta_{0}+\alpha_{1}\delta_{1})=\alpha_{0}(1-p)+\alpha_{1}p

(F.2)

\ell_{\infty}^{2}
is a commutative von Neumann algebra and
\omega_{p}
is a (normal faithful) state on this von Neumann algebra. Any property of the Bernoulli distribution can be translated into a property of the state. For example, any moment of the Bernoulli distribution is given by:

\mathbb{E}(X^{k})=\omega_{p}((0\times\delta_{0}+1\times\delta_{1})^{k})=\omega_{p}(\delta_{1}^{k})=\omega_{p}(\delta_{1})=p

(F.3)

And the moment generating function of the Bernoulli distribution is given by:

\mathbb{E}(e^{tX})=\omega_{p}(e^{t\times 0}\delta_{0}+e^{t\times 1}\delta_{1})=(1-p)+e^{t}p

(F.4)

Given a set of i.i.d. Bernoull random variables
\{X_{1},\cdots,X_{N}\}
, the sum
X=\sum_{1\leq i\leq N}X_{i}
follows the multinomial distribution:

\text{Prob}(X=k)=\binom{N}{k}p^{k}(1-p)^{N-k}

(F.5)

We can characterize the sum and the multinomial distribution using a von Neumann algebra and a state as well. First, the probabilistic notion of independence can be formulated as a tensor product of states. For example, if
X
is a real-valued random variable, we can characterize
X
and its law by
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
d\mu_{X}
, where
d\mu_{X}
is the probability measure of
X
. Further if
X
and
Y
are two independent random variables, then the moment generating function of
X+Y
is a function in
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})=L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\otimes L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and the probability measure on this tensor product is simply the tensor product state
d\mu_{X}\otimes d\mu_{Y}
:

\mathbb{E}(e^{t(X+Y)})=\mathbb{E}(e^{tX})\mathbb{E}(e^{tY})=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{tx}d\mu_{X}(x)\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{ty}d\mu_{Y}(y)

(F.6)

If the two random variables are identically distributed, the two states (or probability measures) are the same:
d\mu_{X}=d\mu_{Y}
. Instead of the tensor product, we can further restrict to the symmetric tensor product
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\otimes_{\text{sym}}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. Identical distribution means that the state
d\mu_{X}\otimes d\mu_{Y}
is invariant under permutation, and therefore gives the tensor product state is well-defined on the symmetric tensor product. The symmetric tensor product contains more information than the random variable
X+Y
. We demonstrate this point using
N
i.i.d. Bernoulli random variables.

First, we consider the symmetric tensor algebra:

\otimes_{\text{sym}}^{N}\ell_{\infty}^{2}=\ell_{\infty}^{2}\otimes_{\text{sym}}\cdots\otimes_{\text{sym}}\ell_{\infty}^{2}

(F.7)

Along with the permutation invariant state:

\omega_{p}^{N}=\omega_{p}\otimes\cdots\otimes\omega_{p}

(F.8)

We consider the linear map
114
114

114

This is simply an
N
-th degree Poisson quantization map.
:

\lambda_{N}:\ell_{\infty}^{2}\rightarrow\otimes_{\text{sym}}^{N}\ell_{\infty}^{2}:\alpha_{0}\delta_{0}+\alpha_{1}\delta_{1}\mapsto\sum_{1\leq i\leq N}1\otimes\cdots\underbrace{\otimes(\alpha_{0}\delta_{0}+\alpha_{1}\delta_{1})\otimes}_{\text{i-th position}}\cdots\otimes 1

(F.9)

where
1
denotes the unit function
\delta_{0}+\delta_{1}
in
\ell_{\infty}^{2}
. It is clear that
\lambda_{N}
preserves adjoint:

\lambda_{N}(\overline{\alpha_{0}}\delta_{0}+\overline{\alpha_{1}}\delta_{1})=\overline{\lambda_{N}(\alpha_{0}\delta_{0}+\alpha_{1}\delta_{1})}

(F.10)

Hence, a self-adjoint function (i.e., a real-valued function) is mapped to a self-adjoint function. In particular,
\lambda_{N}(\delta_{1})
is self-adjoint and one can consider its spectral decomposition.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lambda_{N}(\delta_{1})&=\sum_{1\leq i\leq N}1\otimes\cdots\underbrace{\otimes\delta_{1}\otimes}_{\text{i-th position}}\cdots\otimes 1=\sum_{1\leq i\leq N}(\delta_{0}+\delta_{1})\otimes\cdots\underbrace{\otimes\delta_{1}\otimes}_{\text{i-th position}}\cdots\otimes(\delta_{0}+\delta_{1})\\
&=N\delta_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\delta_{1}+(N-1)\sum_{1\leq i\leq N}\delta_{1}\otimes\cdots\underbrace{\otimes\delta_{0}\otimes}_{\text{i-th position}}\cdots\otimes\delta_{1}+\cdots\\
&+\sum_{1\leq i\leq N}\delta_{0}\otimes\cdots\underbrace{\otimes\delta_{1}\otimes}_{\text{i-th position}}\cdots\otimes\delta_{0}+0\times\delta_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes\delta_{0}\end{split}

(F.11)

The
k
-th term in the final summation is:

(N-k)\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq N}\delta_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\delta_{1}\underbrace{\otimes\delta_{0}\otimes}_{\text{$i_{1}$-th position}}\delta_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\delta_{1}\underbrace{\otimes\delta_{0}\otimes}_{\text{$i_{k}$-th position}}\delta_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\delta_{1}

(F.12)

It is the symmetrization of tensor products of
N-k
terms of
\delta_{1}
and
k
terms of
\delta_{0}
. This symmetrization contains
\binom{N}{k}
terms in the summation. For simplicity, we denote the symmetrized tensor product as
\big((\otimes_{\text{sym}}^{(N-k)}\delta_{1})\otimes_{\text{sym}}(\otimes_{\text{sym}}^{k}\delta_{0})\big)
. Note that because
\delta_{0}
and
\delta_{1}
are mutually orthogonal projections in
\ell_{\infty}^{2}
,
The following operator is also a projection:

e_{k}^{N}:=\big((\otimes_{\text{sym}}^{(N-k)}\delta_{1})\otimes(\otimes_{\text{sym}}^{k}\delta_{0})\big)

(F.13)

These projections are mutually orthogonal:

e^{N}_{k}e^{N}_{k^{\prime}}=\delta_{kk^{\prime}}e^{N}_{k}

(F.14)

Therefore, the spectrum of
\lambda_{N}(\delta_{1})
is the set
\{N,N-1,\cdots,1,0\}
and for each
k
, the corresponding spectral projection is given by
e_{k}^{N}
.

The von Neumann algebra generated by these spectral projections is:

\ell_{\infty}^{N+1}=\mathbb{C}e^{N}_{0}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathbb{C}e^{N}_{N}

(F.15)

This is the subalgebra that contains all the information of the sum
\sum_{1\leq i\leq N}X_{i}
. The multinomial distribution is reflected in the expectation value of the spectral projections:

\text{Prob}(\sum_{1\leq i\leq N}X_{i}=k)=\omega_{p}^{\otimes N}(e^{N}_{k})=\omega_{p}^{\otimes N}\bigg(\big((\otimes_{\text{sym}}^{(N-k)}\delta_{1})\otimes(\otimes_{\text{sym}}^{k}\delta_{0})\big)\bigg)=\binom{N}{k}p^{k}(1-p)^{N-k}

(F.16)

The expectation value of
X
can be calculated by the expectation value of
\lambda_{N}(\delta_{1})
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\sum_{1\leq i\leq N}X_{i})=\sum_{0\leq k\leq N}k\text{Prob}(X=k)=\sum_{0\leq k\leq N}k\omega_{p}^{\otimes N}(e_{k}^{N})

\displaystyle=\omega_{p}^{\otimes N}(\sum_{0\leq k\leq N}ke^{N}_{k})=\omega_{p}^{\otimes N}(\lambda_{N}(\delta_{1}))

(F.17)

Similarly the moment generating function of
X
can be calculated by the expectation value of
e^{t\lambda_{N}(\delta_{1})}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(e^{tX})=\sum_{0\leq k\leq N}e^{tk}\text{Prob}(X^{N}=k)=\sum_{0\leq k\leq N}e^{tk}\omega_{p}^{\otimes N}(e_{k}^{N})

\displaystyle=\omega_{p}^{\otimes N}(\sum_{0\leq k\leq N}e^{tk}e_{k}^{N})=\omega_{p}^{\otimes N}(e^{t\lambda_{N}(\delta_{1})})

(F.18)

where we have used the spectral decomposition of
\lambda_{N}(\delta_{1})
to decompose the exponential function
e^{t\lambda_{N}(\delta_{1})}
.

F.2
Brief introduction to ultrafilter and ultraproduct

In this subsection, we introduce the notions of ultrafilter and ultraproduct to physicists. We will
not
provide the rigorous mathematical definitions. Rather, we will focus on how to use these tools to study asymptotic properties of sequences of operators/operator algebras.

For our purpose, an ultrafilter can be understood as a consistent set of rules to define limits for
all
bounded sequences of complex numbers. It provides a generalization of the notion of convergence in standard analysis. This is particularly useful when we study
asymptotic
properties of sequences of operators.

As a demonstration, we go back to the original proof by Murray and von Neumann
MvN
that the hyperfinite type
II_{1}
factor is not isomorphic to the free group factor (i.e., the large
N
limit of Gaussian random matrices). The key technical tool in their proof is the notion of a nontrivial central sequence in the hyperfinite
II_{1}
factor
\mathcal{R}
. In terms of infinite tensor product,
\mathcal{R}\cong\otimes_{n\geq 0}(M_{2}(\mathbb{C}),\frac{1}{2}\tr_{2})
. A nontrivial central sequence in this case is a sequence of operators
(x_{n})_{n\geq 0}
such that:

1.

The sequence is uniformly bounded in norm:
\sup_{n}||x_{n}||<\infty
;

2.

For any operator
y\in\mathcal{R}
, the sequence asymptotically commute with
y
:

[x_{n},y]\rightarrow 0\text{ strongly}

(F.19)

3.

The following nontriviality conditions hold:

\forall n\geq 0\text{ , }\tau(x_{n})=0

(F.20)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\tau(x_{n}^{\dagger}x_{n})\neq 0

(F.21)

where
\tau
is the canonical trace on
\mathcal{R}
.

Murray and von Neumann showed that there exist nontrivial central sequences in the hyperfinite type
II_{1}
factor, while the free group factors do not have nontrivial central sequences. This proof highlights the importance of asymptotic properties in operator algebras.

It turns out that there is an alternative, more concise and exact formulation of these conditions. Since we want to study sequences of operators in
\mathcal{R}
, it is natural to consider the von Neumann algebra of sequences:
\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{N},\mathcal{R}):=\{(x_{n})_{n\geq 0}:x_{n}\in\mathcal{R}\text{ , }\sup_{n\geq 0}||x_{n}||<\infty\}
. This algebra is the algebra of all sequences satisfying the first condition. Within
\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{N},\mathcal{R})
, there exists a special kind of sequences - namely, the diagonal sequences:
\{(x_{n})_{n\geq 0}:\forall n\text{ , }x_{n}=x\in\mathcal{R}\}
. This is a subalgebra in
\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{N},\mathcal{R})
isomorphic to
\mathcal{R}
. Tentatively, an asymptotically central sequence can now be reformulated as an element in the relative commutant
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\cap\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{N},\mathcal{R})
.

One caveat is that there exist trivial examples of asymptotically central sequences. And we would like to get rid of these examples. For example, the following sequence is trivially asymptotically central:

(\widetilde{x}_{n}:=\frac{1}{n}\mathbbm{1}^{\otimes n}\otimes\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}\otimes\mathbbm{1}\otimes\cdots)_{n\geq 0}

(F.22)

This is because for all
y\in\mathcal{R}
, we have:

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}|\tau(\widetilde{x}_{n}y)|\leq\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\tau(\widetilde{x}_{n}^{\dagger}\widetilde{x}_{n})^{1/2}\tau(y^{\dagger}y)^{1/2}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\tau(y^{\dagger}y)^{1/2}}{n}=0

(F.23)

And similarly
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\tau(y\widetilde{x}_{n})=0
. From this and the fact that any vector
\ket{\xi}
in the GNS representation can be written as
\ket{\xi}=z\ket{\tau^{1/2}}
for some
z\in\mathcal{R}
115
115

115

Here
\ket{\tau^{1/2}}
is the vector implementing the canonical trace
\tau
in the GNS Hilbert space.
, we have:

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\bra{\eta}[\widetilde{x}_{n},y]\ket{\xi}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\tau(w^{\dagger}[\widetilde{x}_{n},y]z)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\tau(\widetilde{x}_{n}yzw^{\dagger})-\tau(yzw^{\dagger}\widetilde{x}_{n})=0

(F.24)

where
\ket{\eta}=w\ket{\tau^{1/2}}
and
\ket{\xi}=z\ket{\tau^{1/2}}
. Thus
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}[\widetilde{x}_{n},y]\ket{\xi}=0
for all
y\in\mathcal{R}
and for all
\ket{\xi}
.

The nontriviality condition gets rid of these trivial sequences. In terms of the algebra of bounded sequences
\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{N},\mathcal{R})
, we are tempted to get rid of these trivial sequences by quotienting out
\{(x_{n})_{n\geq 0}:\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\tau(x^{\dagger}_{n}x_{n})=0\}
. This is the correct idea but the key technical issue is that the standard limit
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\tau(x^{\dagger}_{n}x_{n})
is not always defined. In principle, the sequence of numbers
(\tau(x^{\dagger}_{n}x_{n}))_{n\geq 0}
is only a bounded sequence - it needs not to be convergent in the standard sense.
This is where the notion of ultrafilter comes to the rescue
. As mentioned above, for us an ultrafilter is simply a consistent set of rules that give all bounded sequences a well-defined limit. In terms of notation, we denote this well-defined limit as:
\lim_{w}\tau(x^{\dagger}_{n}x_{n})
where
w
is an ultrafilter
116
116

116

To be precise,
w
is a nonprincipal ultrafilter:
w\in\beta\mathbb{N}\backslash\mathbb{N}
where
\beta\mathbb{N}
is the Stone-Cech compactification of the natural numebrs.
. Using this limit, the following set is well-defined:
I_{w}:=\{(x_{n})_{n\geq 0}:\lim_{w}\tau(x^{\dagger}_{n}x_{n})=0\}
. It turns out that
I_{w}
is a closed
*
-ideal and we can form the quotient von Neumann algebra:

\mathcal{R}^{w}:=\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{N},\mathcal{R})/I_{w}

(F.25)

This is the ultraproduct algebra of
\mathcal{R}
.
Now the asymptotic central sequences are simply elements in the relative commutant:
\mathcal{R}^{w}\cap\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
. The fact that the hyperfinite type
II_{1}
algebra has many nontrivial central sequences can be reformulated as a precise statement:
\mathcal{R}^{w}\cap\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\neq\mathbb{C}
. The tools of ultrafilter and ultraproduct turn an asymptotic property (e.g., asymptotic centrality) into a property of the ultraproduct algebra
\mathcal{R}^{w}
. In this sense, one can regard the ultraproduct algebra as a way to encode asymptotic information.

In the main text, we used a slightly more general version of the ultraproduct construction. For a sequence
(x_{n})_{n\geq 0}
in
\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{N},\mathcal{R})
, each operator
x_{n}
takes value in the same algebra
\mathcal{R}
. Alternatively, we can allow for operators taking values in different algebras. For example, given a sequence of matrix algebras
\{M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\}
, we can form the von Neumann algebra of bounded sequences
\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{N},M_{n}(\mathbb{C})):=\{(x_{n})_{n\geq 0}:x_{n}\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\}
. Using this, we can form the ultraproduct algebra
\prod^{w}M_{n}(\mathbb{C}):=\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{N},M_{n}(\mathbb{C}))/I_{w}
where
I_{w}:=\{(x_{n})_{n\geq 0}\in\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{N},M_{n}(\mathbb{C})):\lim_{w}\tr_{n}(x_{n}^{\dagger}x_{n})=0\}
is a closed
*
-ideal. Here
\tr_{n}
is the canonical normalized trace on
M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
. This ultraproduct of matrix algebras is useful in capturing asymptotic properties of sequences of matrices. This makes the ultraproduct construction useful in studying large
N
properties of matrix models. For example, the free group factor (i.e., the large
N
limit of Gaussian random matrices) can be constructed from this ultraproduct construction.

However, we emphasize that the ultraproduct algebra is typically
not
hyperfinite and it can have drastically different properties than the original sequence of algebras. In addition, the ultraproduct algebra depends on the ultrafilter
w
used in the construction. Often, to show that certain constructions or properties are independent of the ultrafilter, additional arguments would be needed. For our application to Poissonization, we will show that the Poisson algebra is independent of the ultrafilter used in the noncommutative Bernoulli approximation by using a noncommutative Hamburger moment theorem. This moment theorem will be introduced in the next subsection.

F.2.1
Interpolation between Poisson and Gaussian

In this subsection, we demonstrate an interpolation between Poisson algebra and Gaussian algebra. We include it here to emphasize the utility and versatility of the ultraproduct construction. In the previous subsection, we saw that the noncommutative Bernoulli approximation can be rigorously implemented using the ultraproduct construction. Recall that the classical Bernoulli approximation is the Poisson limit theorem (see also Appendix
B
). Similarly, we can use the ultraproduct construction to implement the noncommutative central limit theorem
Speicher1992
(see also Appendix
B
). The resulting algebra is a Gaussian algebra. This brings about an interesting question: whether an interpolation exists between the Poisson algebra and the Gaussian algebra.

Here we demonstrate a one-parameter deformation of Poissonization that interpolates between the Poisson algebra and the Gaussian algebra. First, we make a simple observation. The classical Bernoulli random variable with success probability
\frac{1}{N}
can be modeled by a simple characteristic function
\mathbb{1}_{[0,\frac{1}{N}]}(t)
which is 1 in the interval
[0,\frac{1}{N}]
and 0 elsewhere. Taken the expectation value is equivalent to integration
\int_{0}^{1}dt\mathbbm{1}_{0,\frac{1}{N}}(t)=\frac{1}{N}
. It is easy to see that all higher moments of the Bernoulli random variable can be calculated using
\int_{0}^{1}dt\mathbbm{1}_{[0,\frac{1}{N}]}^{k}(t)
where
k\geq 1
is the order of the moment. Therefore, we can modify the noncommutative Bernoulli approximation as follows. We define a modified
N
-th order Poisson quantization map (see also Equation
7.51
):

\widetilde{\lambda}_{N}:\mathcal{N}\rightarrow\otimes_{\text{sym}}^{N}L^{\infty}([0,1],\mathcal{N}):x\mapsto\sum_{1\leq i\leq N}1\otimes\cdots\underbrace{\otimes(x\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{[0,\frac{1}{N}]})\otimes}_{\text{i-th position}}\cdots\otimes 1

(F.26)

where
L^{\infty}([0,1],\mathcal{N})=L^{\infty}([0,1])\overline{\otimes}\mathcal{N}
is the algebra of bounded operator-valued functions on
[0,1]
with value in
\mathcal{N}
. The weight on
L^{\infty}([0,1],\mathcal{N})
is given by the tensor product between Lebesgue integral and
\tau
on
\mathcal{N}
. And the modified Poisson quantization map is given by:

\widetilde{\lambda}:\mathcal{N}\rightarrow\prod^{w}\left(\otimes_{\text{sym}}^{N}L^{\infty}([0,1],\mathcal{N})\right):x\mapsto[\widetilde{\lambda}_{N}(x)]

(F.27)

The Poisson algebra
\mathbb{P}_{\tau}\mathcal{N}
is simply the algebra generated by
\langle\widetilde{\lambda}(x):x^{\dagger}=x\in\mathcal{N}\rangle
. The same calculation as in the previous section shows that the Poisson moment formula holds in the limit:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\varphi_{\tau}(\widetilde{\lambda}(x_{1})\cdots\widetilde{\lambda}(x_{n}))&=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n},|\sigma|\leq N}|\sigma|!\binom{N}{|\sigma|}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\int_{0}^{1}dt\mathbbm{1}_{[0,\frac{1}{N}]}(t)\tau(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{j\in A}x_{j})\\
&=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n},|\sigma|\leq N}|\sigma|!\binom{N}{|\sigma|}\frac{1}{N^{|\sigma|}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\tau(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{j\in A}x_{j})\\
&=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\tau(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{j\in A}x_{j})\end{split}

(F.28)

The Gaussian algebra is characterized by the fact that its moment formula decomposes into a sum of products of two-point functions
117
117

117

This holds for elements whose one-point function vanishes.
. When the input data is
(\mathcal{N},\tau)
, the resulting Gaussian functional admits a moment formula of the form:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\phi_{\text{Gaussian}}&\left(\lambda_{\text{Gaussian}}\left(x_{1}-\tau\left(x_{1}\right)\right)\cdots\lambda_{\text{Gaussian}}\left(x_{2n}-\tau\left((x_{2n}\right)\right)\right)\\
&=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{2n,\text{pair}}}\prod_{(ij)\in\sigma}\tau\left(\left(x_{i}-\tau\left(x_{i}\right)\right)\left(x_{j}-\tau\left(x_{j}\right)\right)\right)\end{split}

(F.29)

where
\mathcal{P}_{2n,\text{pair}}
is the set of pair partitions and
(ij)\in\sigma
is a pair partition in
\sigma
. The
N
-th order Gaussian quantization map
\lambda_{\text{Gaussian}}
is define by:

x-\tau(x)\mapsto\sum_{1\leq i\leq N}\frac{1\otimes\cdots\underbrace{\otimes(x-\tau(x))\otimes}_{\text{i-th position}}\cdots\otimes 1}{\sqrt{N}}

(F.30)

This is nothing but the familiar formula from the central limit theorem. Then the Gaussian quantization map
\lambda_{\text{Gaussian}}
is simply the (quotient of) sequence of such operators. The Gaussian functional is then the limiting functional:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phi_{\text{Gaussian}}(\lambda_{\text{Gaussian}}(x_{1})\cdots\lambda_{\text{Gaussian}}(x_{n}))\\
&=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\tau^{\otimes N}\left(\left(\sum_{1\leq i\leq N}\frac{1\otimes\cdots\underbrace{\otimes(x_{1}-\tau(x_{1}))\otimes}_{\text{i-th position}}\cdots\otimes 1}{\sqrt{N}}\right)\cdots\left(\sum_{1\leq i\leq N}\frac{1\otimes\cdots\underbrace{\otimes(x_{n}-\tau(x_{n}))\otimes}_{\text{i-th position}}\cdots\otimes 1}{\sqrt{N}}\right)\right)\\
&=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}|\sigma|!\binom{N}{|\sigma|}\frac{1}{N^{\sum_{A\in\sigma}|A|/2}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\tau(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{j\in A}(x_{j}-\tau(x_{j})))\\
&=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\frac{N(N-1)\cdots(N-|\sigma|+1)}{N^{n/2}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\tau(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{j\in A}(x_{j}-\tau(x_{j})))\end{split}

(F.31)

To take the limit, because the one-point functions vanish, if a partition
\sigma
contains a singleton, the product
\prod_{A\in\sigma}\tau(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{j\in A}(x_{j}-\tau(x_{j})))
vanishes. Hence, all nonzero contributions come from the partition
\sigma
without singleton subsets. The maximal cardinality of
\sigma
is thus
|\sigma|\leq[n/2]
. If
n
is odd, the limit is zero because
[n/2]<n/2
. If
n
is even, the limit projects to the pair partitions because these are the partitions with the largest cardinality
n/2
. Therefore, the limit reduces to the Gaussian form.

Now, an interpolation between the Poisson algebra and the Gaussian algebra is simple to implement. We introduce a one-parameter deformation of the quantization map:

\lambda^{(\alpha)}_{N}(x):=\sum_{1\leq i\leq N}1\otimes\cdots\underbrace{\otimes\left(x\otimes\left(\alpha\mathbbm{1}_{[0,\frac{1}{N}]}+(1-\alpha)\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\right)\right)\otimes}_{\text{i-th position}}\cdots\otimes 1

(F.32)

where
\alpha\in[0,1]
. Notice that
\alpha\mathbbm{1}_{[0,\frac{1}{N}]}+(1-\alpha)\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}
is a function in
L^{\infty}([0,1])
. When
\alpha=1
, this reduces to the Poisson quantization
\widetilde{\lambda}_{N}
. When
\alpha=0
, this reduces to the Gaussian quantization. For each
\alpha
, we can define the corresponding quantization map
\lambda^{(\alpha)}(x):=[\lambda^{(\alpha)}_{N}(x)]
and consider the von Neumann algebra generated by
\lambda^{(\alpha)}(x)
’s in the ultraproduct algebra:
\prod^{w}\otimes_{\text{sym}}^{N}L^{\infty}([0,1],\mathcal{N})
. When
\alpha=1
, this algebra is the Poisson algebra. When
\alpha=0
, this algebra is the Gaussian algebra. In between, we have a family of von Neumann algebras interpolating between these two extremes. One can calculate the moment formula in the case of a general
\alpha
. This parameter essentially controls the relative weights of the pair partitions among all partitions. When
\alpha=1
(in the case of Poisson algebra), all partitions have the same weight. When
\alpha=0
(in the case of Gaussian algebra), all partitions other than the pair partitions have weight
0
.

There are numerous other deformations of Poissonization we can explore. We will not discuss these in depth here but reserve them for future work.

F.3
Noncommutative Hamburger moment theorem

Throughout this work, we have presented several constructions of Poissonization. Yet we have not shown these constructions are equivalent. Here we quote the result that allows us to prove the equivalence of different constructions of Poissonization. This result is based on a noncommutative generalization of the classical Hamburger’s moment theorem
MAW
. This generalization is done in the framework of noncommutative probability theory
quantStoch
. One of the key ingredients of noncommutative probability theory is the correlation function. In a sense, noncommutative probability theory aims to construct and study the properties of operator algebras using combinatorial structures in correlation functions. To demonstrate the use of noncommutative probability theory, we will revisit the well-known second quantization and reformulate its construction using tools from noncommutative probability theory.

Hamburger moment problem and its reformulation in noncommutative probability theory:

This classical moment problem considers the reverse-engineering question whether a sequence of real numbers can
uniquely
specify a probability measure on
\mathbb{R}
. Suppose two such probability measures have already been found
d\nu_{1}(x)
and
d\nu_{2}(x)
, these two measures are the same if the
moment growth condition
is satisfies:

|\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}x^{n}d\nu_{1}(x)|=|\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}x^{n}d\nu_{2}(x)|=|\mu_{n}|\leq Ce^{n}n^{n}

(F.33)

where
C>0
is an absolute constant.

To introduce the noncommutative generalization, we first rethink the essential input to the classical Hamburger’s moment problem. The problem statement specifies a sequence of real numbers
\{\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\cdots\}
. One suspects these real numbers might be moments of some probability measure
d\nu(x)
. To make this suspicion mathematically precise, we need two ingredients. First, we consider the abstract algebra generated by two symbols
1
and
x
. In fact, this algebra is nothing but the familiar algebra of polynomials:
\mathbb{C}[x]
. Note that there exists an adjoint operation on polynomials:

(\sum_{0\leq n\leq N}\alpha_{n}x^{n})^{\dagger}=\sum_{0\leq n\leq N}\overline{\alpha_{n}}x^{n}

(F.34)

This means that
\mathbb{C}[x]
is a unital
*
-associative algebra
118
118

118

Unital means there exists a multiplicative unit in the algebra. Associativity means that the multiplication is associative.
. In addition, the sequence of real numbers
\{\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\cdots\}
defines a linear functional:

\phi:\mathbb{C}[x]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}:\sum_{0\leq n\leq N}\alpha_{n}x^{n}\mapsto\sum_{0\leq n\leq N}\alpha_{n}\mu_{n}

(F.35)

where
\mu_{0}
is defined to be 1. Hence, this linear functional is normalized:

\phi(1)=\mu_{0}=1

(F.36)

The existence part of the Hamburger moment theorem states that there exists a probability measure on
\mathbb{R}
whose moments are given by
\{\mu_{0},\mu_{1},\cdots\}
if and only if the Hankel operator is positive definite:

\begin{bmatrix}\mu_{0}&\mu_{1}&\mu_{2}&\cdots&\mu_{N}&\cdots\\
\mu_{1}&\mu_{2}&\mu_{3}&\cdots&\mu_{N+1}&\cdots\\
\mu_{2}&\mu_{3}&\mu_{4}&\cdots&\mu_{N+2}&\cdots\\
\cdots\\
\mu_{N}&\mu_{N+1}&\mu_{N+2}&\cdots\\
\cdots\end{bmatrix}\geq 0

(F.37)

Equivalently, for any finite sequence of complex parameters
(\alpha_{n})_{0\leq n\leq N}
, we have:

\begin{bmatrix}\overline{\alpha_{0}}&\overline{\alpha_{1}}&\cdots&\overline{\alpha_{N}}&0&\cdots\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\mu_{0}&\mu_{1}&\mu_{2}&\cdots&\mu_{N}&\cdots\\
\mu_{1}&\mu_{2}&\mu_{3}&\cdots&\mu_{N+1}&\cdots\\
\mu_{2}&\mu_{3}&\mu_{4}&\cdots&\mu_{N+2}&\cdots\\
\cdots\\
\mu_{N}&\mu_{N+1}&\mu_{N+2}&\cdots\\
\cdots\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{0}\\
\alpha_{1}\\
\alpha_{2}\\
\cdots\\
\alpha_{N}\\
0\\
\cdots\end{bmatrix}\geq 0

(F.38)

In other words:

\sum_{0\leq n,m\leq N}\overline{\alpha_{n}}\alpha_{m}\mu_{n+m}\geq 0

(F.39)

In terms of the linear functional
\phi
, we have:

\phi((\sum_{0\leq n\leq N}\alpha_{n}x^{n})^{\dagger}(\sum_{0\leq n\leq N}\alpha_{n}x^{n}))=\sum_{0\leq n,m\leq N}\overline{\alpha_{n}}\alpha_{m}\phi(x^{n+m})=\sum_{0\leq n,m\leq N}\overline{\alpha_{n}}\alpha_{m}\mu_{n+m}\geq 0

(F.40)

In addition, since the measure is a probability measure, we have a normalization condition:

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\nu(x)=1

(F.41)

where
d\nu(x)
is the probability measure whose moments are
\{\mu_{0},\mu_{1},\cdots\}
. This is exactly the normalization of the functional
\phi
. Therefore, the existence of a probability measure on
\mathbb{R}
whose moments are
\{\mu_{0},\mu_{1},\cdots\}
is equivalent to the linear functional
\phi
being positive and unital:

\phi(f(x)^{\dagger}f(x))\geq 0\text{ for any }f(x)\in\mathbb{C}[x]

(F.42)

\phi(1)=1

(F.43)

Given such a linear functional, the GNS construction gives a representation of the polynomial algebra on the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R},d\nu(x))
:

\pi:\mathbb{C}[x]\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R},d\nu(x))):f(x)\mapsto M_{f(x)}

(F.44)

where
\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R},d\nu(x)))
is the space of linear operators densely defined on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R},d\nu(x))
, and
M_{p(x)}
is the multiplication operator:

(M_{f(x)}g)(x)=f(x)g(x)

(F.45)

Notice that this is indeed the GNS construction because the inner product is given by the positive functional
\phi
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}||\pi(f(x))1||^{2}&=||M_{f(x)}1||^{2}=||f(x)||^{2}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|f(x)|^{2}D\nu(x)=\sum_{0\leq n\leq N}\overline{\alpha_{n}}\alpha_{m}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}x^{n+m}d\nu(x)\\
&=\sum_{0\leq n,m\leq N}\overline{\alpha_{n}}\alpha_{m}\mu_{n+m}=\phi(f(x)^{\dagger}f(x))<\infty\end{split}

(F.46)

where
f(x)=\sum_{0\leq n\leq N}\alpha_{n}x^{n}
. In particular, all polynomials are elements of the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R},d\nu(x))
. The representation
\pi
is unital:

\pi(1)=id

(F.47)

And it preserves the adjoint:

\pi(f(x)^{\dagger})=M_{f(x)}^{\dagger}

(F.48)

Moreover, although the multiplication operator
M_{f(x)}
may be unbounded in general, all polynomials are in its domain, because all moments of the probability distribution are finite:

||M_{f(x)}g(x)||^{2}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|f(x)g(x)|^{2}D\nu(x)=\phi(|f(x)g(x)|^{2})=\sum_{0\leq n,m\leq N}\overline{\beta_{n}}\beta_{m}\mu_{n+m}<\infty

(F.49)

where
f(x)g(x)=\sum_{0\leq n\leq N}\beta_{n}x^{n}
.

The pair
(\mathbb{C}[x],\phi)
is a basic example of a noncommutative probability space. The representation
\pi
is a basic example of the GNS representation of a noncommutative probability space. More generally, a noncommutative probability space is a pair of data:

1.

A unital
*
-associative algebra
\mathcal{A}

2.

And a normalized positive linear functional
\phi:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
such that
\phi(x^{\dagger}x)\geq 0
and
\phi(1)=1

Previously we construct the GNS representation of
(\mathbb{C}[x],\phi)
on the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R},d\nu)
. However, the choice of Hilbert space is not unique. Different choices result in different models of the same noncommutative probability space
(\mathbb{C}[x],\phi)
. For example, one may find another probability measure
d\nu^{\prime}(x)
on
\mathbb{R}
whose moments are
\{\mu_{0},\mu_{1},\cdots\}
. Then the GNS representation of
(\mathbb{C}[x],\phi)
can act on the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R},d\nu^{\prime}(x))
. Within each GNS representation, the noncommutative probability space generates a von Neumann algebra. This is the algebra generated by (the spectral projections of) the basis elements:

\overline{\pi_{\nu}(\mathbb{C}[x])}:=\langle\pi_{\nu}(x),\pi_{\nu}(1)\rangle\subset\mathbb{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R},d\nu))

(F.50)

where
\{1,x\}
are the generators that generate the polynomial algebra and
\pi_{\nu}
is the GNS representation of
(\mathbb{C}[x],\phi)
. The probability measure
\nu
induces a state on this von Neumann algebra:

\phi_{\nu}\bigg(\pi_{\nu}\big(f(x)\big)\bigg):=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\bigg(\pi_{\nu}\big(f(x)\big)1\bigg)d\nu(x)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}M_{f(x)}1d\nu(x)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(x)d\nu(x)

(F.51)

The uniqueness of the probability measure can now be translated into the uniqueness of the von Neumann algebra generated by the noncommutative probability space
(\mathbb{C}[x],\phi)
. The probability measure is unique if and only if there exists an isomorphism of von Neumann algebras:

\psi:\overline{\pi_{\nu}(\mathbb{C}[x])}\rightarrow\overline{\pi_{\nu^{\prime}}(\mathbb{C}[x])}

(F.52)

such that the map preserves the state:
\phi_{\nu^{\prime}}\circ\psi=\phi_{\nu}
. And by the Hamburger moment theorem, the measure is unique if and only if the moment growth condition is satisfied:

\phi(x^{n})=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}x^{n}d\nu(x)\leq Ce^{n}n^{n}

(F.53)

The noncommutative probability space
(\mathbb{C}[x],\phi)
is generated by a single non-trivial element
x
. In general, a noncommutative probability space
(\mathcal{A},\phi)
can be generated by a set
S:=\{s_{i}\in\mathcal{A}\}_{i\in I}
. And a noncommutative moment is defined to be expressions of the form:

\phi(s_{1}s_{2}\cdots s_{n})

(F.54)

We are finally ready to state the noncommutative generalization of the Hamburger moment theorem
MAW
:

Theorem 5
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},\phi)
be a noncommutative probability space. Let
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
be two representations of
(\mathcal{A},\phi)
. Then there exists a state-preserving von Neumann algebra isomorphism:

\psi:\overline{\pi_{1}(\mathcal{A})}\rightarrow\overline{\pi_{2}(\mathcal{A})}

(F.55)

if and only if there exists a length function on a generating set
S
of
\mathcal{A}
:

\ell:S\rightarrow(0,\infty)

(F.56)

such that the noncommutative moment growth condition is satisfied:

|\phi(s_{1}\cdots s_{n})|\leq Cn^{n}\prod_{1\leq i\leq n}\ell(s_{i})

In the case of
(\mathbb{C}[x],\phi)
, we can simply take the length function to be the constant function with value being the natural exponent:

\ell(x):=e

(F.57)

Equivalence of different constructions of Poissonization:

We can now immediately apply this theorem to show the equivalence of various constructions of Poissonization. The noncommutative probability space is the
free
algebra generated by the
abstract
symbols:

\mathcal{A}_{\text{Poiss}}:=\langle 1,\lambda(\mathcal{O}):x\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{Tomita}}\rangle

(F.58)

where
\mathcal{N}_{\text{Tomita}}
is the Tomita subalgebra of
\mathcal{N}
under the weight
\omega
. When
\omega(1)<\infty
,
\mathcal{N}_{\text{Tomita}}
can be taken as the entire algebra
\mathcal{N}
.

\mathcal{A}_{\text{Poiss}}
is a unital
*
-associative algebra where elements are words:

\lambda(\mathcal{O}_{1})\lambda(\mathcal{O}_{2})\cdots\lambda(\mathcal{O}_{n})

(F.59)

The adjoint operation is given by:

\lambda(\mathcal{O})^{\dagger}:=\lambda(\mathcal{O}^{\dagger})

(F.60)

The state
\phi_{\text{Poiss}}
is defined explicitly by the formula:

\phi_{\text{Poiss}}(\lambda(\mathcal{O}_{1})\cdots\lambda(\mathcal{O}_{n}))=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\omega(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{i\in A}x_{i})

(F.61)

where
\mathcal{P}_{n}
is the set of partitions on
\{1,..,n\}
and
A\in\sigma
is an element in the partition
\sigma
.

On the von Neumann algebra
\mathcal{N}
(or the Tomita algebra
