\mathcal{N}_{\text{Tomita}}
in case
\omega
is infinite), we define the semi-norm:

|||x|||:=\max\{||x||,\omega(x^{\dagger}x)^{1/2},\omega(xx^{\dagger})^{1/2}\}

(F.62)

And the length function is a rescaling of the semi-norm:

\ell(x):=e|||x|||

(F.63)

Then the
Poisson moment formula
satisfies the growth condition:

\displaystyle\begin{split}|\phi_{\text{Poiss}}(\lambda(\mathcal{O}_{1})\cdots\lambda(\mathcal{O}_{n}))|&\leq\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}|\omega(\overrightarrow{\prod}_{i\in A}x_{i})|\leq\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\prod_{A\in\sigma}\prod_{i\in A}|||x_{i}|||\\
&\leq|\mathcal{P}_{n}|\prod_{1\leq i\leq n}|||x_{i}|||\leq Ce^{n}n^{n}\prod_{1\leq i\leq n}|||x_{i}|||=Cn^{n}\prod_{1\leq i\leq n}\ell(x_{i})\end{split}

(F.64)

where we have used the Cauchy-Schwartz estimate:

\displaystyle\begin{split}|\omega(xy_{1}\cdots y_{n}z)|&\leq\omega(xx^{\dagger})^{1/2}\omega(z^{\dagger}y^{\dagger}yz)^{1/2}\leq\omega(xx^{\dagger})^{1/2}||y_{1}..y_{n}||\omega(z^{\dagger}z)^{1/2}\\
&\leq\omega(xx^{\dagger})^{1/2}\prod_{1\leq i\leq n}||y_{i}||\omega(z^{\dagger}z)^{1/2}\leq|||x|||(\prod_{1\leq i\leq n}|||y_{i}|||)|||z|||\end{split}

(F.65)

In addition, we have used the fact that the cardinality of the set of partitions
\mathcal{P}_{n}
is given by the classical Bell number
B_{n}
, and the Bell number is bounded above by:

B_{n}\leq Ce^{n}n^{n}

(F.66)

where
C>0
is an absolute constant.

Therefore, from the noncommutative Hamburger moment theorem, all formulations of Poissonization are equivalent because all formulations take the same input and produce the same Poisson moment formula, which satisfies the growth condition.

The key idea of this proof is that the Poisson algebra can be fully characterized by the combinatorial structure of the Poisson moment formula.
The abstract noncommutative probability space
(\mathcal{A}_{\text{Poiss}},\phi_{\text{Poiss}})
serves to formalize the combinatorial structure and to add in the necessary analytical control over the moment formula. To determine an analytical object (in this case, the Poisson algebra) from combinatorial information is one of the key ideas in noncommutative probability theory. Another example of noncommutative probability theory is free probability, where in physics language, many of the properties are fixed by the structures of large
N
correlation functions. Yet another example is the second quantization. As a demonstration, we elaborate on the noncommutative probability perspective on the second quantization below.

F.3.1
Application of noncommutative probability: Second quantization

Here we restate the second quantization using the framework of noncommutative probability theory. For simplicity, we consider the so-called ”full” second quantization, where the construction does not rely on a polarization (or, equivalently, a Lagrangian subspace). The input data of the second quantization is a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. The imaginary part of the inner product gives a natural symplectic structure on the Hilbert space. Each unit vector
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
is associated with a pair of symbols
a_{\xi},a_{\xi}^{\dagger}
. And we consider the
free
algebra generated by the family of symbols:

\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\text{Gauss}}=\langle 1,a_{\xi},a_{\xi}^{\dagger}:\xi\in\mathcal{H}\rangle

More concretely, elements of this algebra are words concatenated from the basic symbols
a_{\xi}
and
a_{\xi}^{\dagger}
. The free algebra admits an obvious anti-linear adjoint operation:

a_{\xi}\mapsto a_{\xi}^{\dagger}

Consider the ideal generated by the canonical commutation relation:

\mathcal{I}_{\text{Gauss}}:=\langle a_{\xi}a_{\eta}^{\dagger}-a_{\eta}^{\dagger}a_{\xi}-\bra{\xi}\ket{\eta}1\text{ , }a_{\xi}a_{\eta}-a_{\eta}a_{\xi}\text{ , }a_{\xi}^{\dagger}a_{\eta}^{\dagger}-a_{\eta}^{\dagger}a_{\xi}^{\dagger}:\xi,\eta\in\mathcal{H}\rangle

Note that
a_{\xi}a_{\eta}^{\dagger}-a_{\eta}^{\dagger}a_{\xi}-\bra{\xi}\ket{\eta}1
is a valid word in the free algebra
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\text{Gauss}}
. And the ideal
\mathcal{I}_{\text{Gauss}}
is invariant under the adjoint action. Therefore, we can consider quotient algebra:

\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\text{Gauss}}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\text{Gauss}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\text{Gauss}}/\widetilde{I}_{\text{Gauss}}

It is clear that
\mathcal{A}_{\text{Gauss}}
is a unital
*
-associative algebra. By the canonical commutation relations (which is precisely the relations enforced by quotienting the ideal
\widetilde{I}_{\text{Gauss}}
), the canonical form of a word in
\mathcal{A}_{\text{Gauss}}
is:

\prod_{1\leq i\leq m}a_{\xi_{i}}\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}a_{\eta_{j}}^{\dagger}

When
m=0
or
n=0
, the corresponding product is defined to be equal to the unit symbol
1
. We define a linear functional
\phi_{\text{Gauss}}
on this algebra:

\phi_{\text{Gauss}}(\prod_{1\leq i\leq m}a_{\xi_{i}}\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}a_{\eta_{j}}^{\dagger})=\delta_{n,m}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\prod_{1\leq i\leq n}\bra{\xi_i}\ket{\eta_{\sigma(i)}}

This is nothing but the Wick contraction. By construction, the linear functional is normalized, and by a standard calculation (the same calculation used to prove the inner product on the symmetric Fock space is positive), we can see that
\phi_{\text{Gauss}}
is also positive. Therefore
(\mathcal{A}_{\text{Gauss}},\phi_{\text{Gauss}})
is a noncommutative probability space.

The GNS construction provides a representation of
\mathcal{A}_{\text{Gauss}}
on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\text{abstract}}
. On the other hand, we have already known that
\mathcal{A}_{\text{Gauss}}
can be represented on the symmetric Fock space
\mathcal{F}_{\text{sym}}(\mathcal{H})
where
a_{\xi}
is the (unbounded) annhilation operator and
a_{\xi}^{\dagger}
is the creation operator. A priori, it is unclear that the von Neumann algebra generated by the creation/annihilation operators is isomorphic to the (ultra-)weak closure of
\mathcal{A}_{\text{Gauss}}
acting on
\mathcal{H}_{\text{abstract}}
. However, there exists a natural length function on the generating set of
\mathcal{A}_{\text{Gauss}}
- namely the norm on
\mathcal{H}
:

\ell(a_{\xi})=\ell(a_{\xi}^{\dagger}):=||\xi||

On the unit symbol, we simply define
\ell(1)=1
. Then the Gaussian moment formula satisfies the growth condition:

|\phi_{\text{Gauss}}(\prod_{1\leq i\leq m}a_{\xi_{i}}\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}a_{\eta_{j}}^{\dagger})|\leq\delta_{n,m}|S_{n}|\prod_{1\leq i\leq m}||\xi_{i}||\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}||\eta_{j}||\leq n!\prod_{1\leq i\leq m}\ell(a_{\xi_{i}})\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\ell(a_{\eta_{j}}^{\dagger})

Since
n!<n^{n}
, the moment growth condition is satisfied. By the noncommutative Hamburger moment’s theorem, the abstract construction of the second quantization is equivalent to the usual symmetric Fock space model.

F.4
Set partitions and distributions

Throughout this work, the key combinatorial object is the set of partitions
\mathcal{P}_{n}
on the finite set
[n]=\{1,2,\cdots,n\}
. In this subsection, we collect several useful facts about
\mathcal{P}_{n}
.

First a
partition

\sigma
of
[n]
is a grouping of the elements of
[n]
into non-empty disjoint subsets:

\sigma:=\{A_{1},A_{2},\cdots,A_{m}:A_{i}\subset[n]\text{ , }A_{i}\cap A_{j}=\emptyset\}

(F.67)

The cardinality of
\sigma
is at least
1
and at most
n
. For the unique partition with only
1
element, we call it the trivial partition:

\sigma_{\text{trivial}}=\{\{1,2,\cdots,n\}\}

(F.68)

For the unique partition with
n
elements, we call it the singleton partition:

\sigma_{\text{singleton}}=\{\{1\},\cdots,\{n\}\}

(F.69)

The number of partitions of
[n]
with cardinality
m
is
the Stirling number of second kind

S(n,m)
:

S(n,m)=\frac{1}{m!}\sum_{p_{1}+\cdots+p_{m}=n}\frac{n!}{p_{1}!\cdots p_{m}!}

(F.70)

The total number of partitions of
[n]
is
the Bell number

B_{n}
:

B_{n}=\sum_{1\leq m\leq n}S(n,m)

(F.71)

The generating function of the Stirling number of the second kind is
the Touchard polynomial

T_{n}(x)
:

T_{n}(x)=\sum_{1\leq m\leq n}S(n,m)x^{m}

(F.72)

From the definitions, the Bell number is simply the Touchard polynomial when
x=1
:

B_{n}=T_{n}(1)

(F.73)

The moments of a Poisson distribution with intensity
\mu
are given by the Touchard polynomials:

\mathbb{E}(X^{n})=\sum_{k\geq 0}k^{n}\frac{e^{-\mu}\mu^{k}}{k!}=T_{n}(\mu)

(F.74)

where
X
is a Poisson random variable with intensity
\mu
. The additivity of Poisson intensities can be written in terms of a recursion relation between Touchard polynomials:

T_{n}(\mu_{1}+\mu_{2})=\sum_{0\leq k\leq n}\binom{n}{k}T_{k}(\mu_{1})T_{n-k}(\mu_{2})

(F.75)

where by convention
T_{0}(\mu)=1
. And the moment generating function of a Poisson distribution can be written as:

\exp(\mu(e^{\eta} - 1))=\mathbb{E}(e^{\eta X})=\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{\eta^{n}}{n!}T_{n}(\mu)=\sum_{n\geq 0}\sum_{1\leq m\leq n}\frac{\eta^{n}}{n!}S(n,m)\mu^{m}

(F.76)

where
X
is a Poisson random variable with intensity
\mu
.

The Stirling numbers and the Touchard polynomials have shown up in various formulae involving normal ordering of number operators
mansour2016commutation
. For example, we have:

\widehat{N}^{n}=\sum_{1\leq m\leq n}S(n,m):\widehat{N}^{m}:

(F.77)

where the number operators on the right hand side
:\widehat{N}^{m}:
are normally ordered. We can regard the right-hand side as a normal-ordered operator:

:T_{n}(\widehat{N}):=\sum_{1\leq m\leq n}S(n,m):\widehat{N}^{m}:

(F.78)

The exponential of the number operator can be written as:

\exp(\eta\widehat{N})=\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{\eta^{n}}{n!}\widehat{N}^{n}=\sum_{n\geq 0}\sum_{1\leq m\leq n}\frac{\eta^{n}}{n!}S(n,m):\widehat{N}^{m}:=:\exp((e^{\eta} - 1)\widehat{N}):

(F.79)

Appendix G
Universe field theory of JT gravity

JT gravity is a simple 2D theory of gravity with the action

\displaystyle I_{JT}=S_{0}\chi-\left(\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{M}}\sqrt{g}\phi(R+2)+\int_{\partial\mathcal{M}}\phi K\right)

(G.1)

where
\chi=2-2g-n
is the Euler characteristic of a Riemann surface of genus
g
with
n
disks removed.

Consider the probe limit of a single asymptotic open boundary (parent universe) emitting and absorbing closed baby universes. We can choose boundary conditions that correspond to fixing the geodesic length of the closed universe to be
b
. To each time of length
T
that does not scale with entropy, associate the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{open}\otimes\mathcal{H}
with
\psi(b)
a wave-function on a single baby universe Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+},bdb)
. A single absorption/emission event is an evolution in this Hilbert space.
Since the emissions are identical, to describe multiple emissions we consider the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{open}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{sym}\left(\mathcal{H}\right)
where
\mathcal{H}
is the Fock space of closed universes:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{sym}\left(\mathcal{H}\right)\equiv\overline{\oplus_{n\geq 0}\otimes_{\text{sym}}^{n}\mathcal{H}}=\overline{\mathbb{C}\ket{\Omega}\oplus\mathcal{H}\oplus\text{sym}(\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H})\otimes\cdots}

(G.2)

In the absence of matter fields, the Hilbert space of the open boundary
\mathcal{H}_{open}
furnishes a representation of the commutative algebra of functions of Hamiltonian
f(H)=\int dE\rho_{0}(E)f(E)
where
\rho_{0}(E)=\frac{e^{S}}{2\pi^{2}}\sinh(2\pi\sqrt{2E})
is the eigenvalue density. Another convenient basis is
l
-basis, where
l
is the renormalized bulk geodesic length between the left and right boundaries in
\mathcal{H}_{open}
. They are related to the bulk energy eigenbasis using

\displaystyle\psi_{E}(l)=\innerproduct{l}{E}=\frac{u}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dx\>e^{-u\cosh x}e^{-2isx}

\displaystyle s=\sqrt{2E},\qquad u=4e^{-l/2}\ .

(G.3)

The resolution of identity in the
l
-basis takes the form

\displaystyle e^{-S}\int e^{l}dl\innerproduct{E'}{l}\innerproduct{l}{E}=\frac{1}{\rho_{0}(E)}\delta(E-E^{\prime})

(G.4)

\displaystyle e^{-S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{l}dl\>\ket{l}\bra{l}=1\ .

(G.5)

In the absence of topology fluctuations, the disk partition function captures the transition amplitude between thermofield doubles of different temperatures:

\displaystyle\innerproduct{TFD_\beta}{TFD_{\beta'}}=\int dE\rho_{0}(E)e^{-(\beta+\beta^{\prime})E/2}\ .

(G.6)

We can compute the disk amplitude for a transition to
\ket{l}_{open}
after time
t
:

\displaystyle\bra{l}e^{-iHT/2}\ket{TFD_\beta}=\int_{0}^{\infty}dE\rho_{0}(E)e^{-(\beta/2+iT)E}\psi_{E}(l)\ .

(G.7)

The amplitude for an event that corresponds to the absorption of a single baby universe is

\ket{l,b}\equiv\ket{l}_{open}\otimes\ket{b}
with
\ket{b}\in\mathcal{H}
:

\displaystyle\bra{l,b}e^{-iHT/2}\ket{TFD_\beta}=\int_{0}^{\infty}dE\rho_{0}(E)e^{-(\beta/2+iT)E}\psi_{E}(l)\left(\frac{\cos(b \sqrt{2E})}{\pi\rho_{0}(E)\sqrt{2E}}\right)

(G.8)

In the probe limit of a single open boundary, we can represent the emission of a baby universe with the action of the operator
\mathcal{O}(b)
below, which is diagonal in the energy eigenbasis of the parent universe
119
119

119

Note that the vector
\ket{E,b}=\mathcal{O}(b)\ket{E}
is not properly normalized. Its norm is given by the two-point function of
\mathcal{O}(b)
, which diverges in the limit
E\to 0
. However, the operator
\mathcal{O}(b)
is still densely defined in the Hilbert space of the open boundary
penington2023algebras
.
; see Figure
10
(a):

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(b)=\int dE\rho_{0}(E)\left(\frac{\cos(b \sqrt{2E})}{\pi\rho_{0}(E)\sqrt{2E}}\right)\ket{E}\bra{E}

(G.9)

The amplitude for the emission of a baby universe of length
b
in a thermofield double state is

\displaystyle Z_{trumpet}(\beta+iT,b)

\displaystyle\equiv\bra{TFD_{\beta/2}}e^{-iH_{bulk}T/2}\ket{TFD_{\beta/2},b}

(G.10)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}dE\>\frac{\cos(b\sqrt{2E})}{\pi\sqrt{2E}}e^{-(\beta+iT)E}\ .

Since
\rho_{0}(E)\sim e^{S}
, the amplitude for the emission of a baby universe of circumference
b
is exponentially suppressed. Therefore, we can ignore the creation of multiple baby universes in times of order
T=O(1)
. The operators
\mathcal{O}(b)
generate a commutative algebra of observables on the parent Hilbert space.

Figure 10:
(a) The operator
\mathcal{O}(b)
in JT gravity that creates an baby universe with length
b
. (b) The operator that creates an asymptotic boundary
Z(E_{1},E^{\prime}_{1})
can be decomposed as a second-order effect with emission and absorption of a baby universe
saad2019late
.

Going beyond the probe limit in JT, one can compute the amplitude for insertion of a closed asymptotic boundary as the operator
Z(E_{1},E_{1}^{\prime})
in the one-boundary Hilbert space; see Figure
10
(b):

\displaystyle\bra{E'}Z(E^{\prime}_{1},E_{1})\ket{E}

\displaystyle=\int bdb\innerproduct{E',b}{E}\innerproduct{E_1',b}{E_1}

(G.11)

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta(E-E^{\prime})\delta(E_{1}-E_{1}^{\prime})}{\rho(E)\rho(E_{1})}\frac{(E+E_{1})}{4\pi^{2}\sqrt{EE_{1}}(E-E_{1})^{2}}

In the limit
E\to E_{1}
, we find the level repulsion term
1/(E-E_{1})^{2}
often attributed to the ramp. More generally, acting on a single boundary with the operator
Z(E^{\prime}_{1},E_{1})\cdots Z(E^{\prime}_{p},E_{p})
gives

\displaystyle\bra{E'}Z(E^{\prime}_{1},E_{1})\cdots Z(E^{\prime}_{p},E_{p})\ket{E}=\frac{\delta(E-E^{\prime})}{(2\pi)^{p}\rho(E)^{p}\sqrt{E}}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\frac{\delta(E_{i}-E^{\prime}_{i})(E+E_{i})}{\rho(E_{i})\sqrt{E_{i}}(E-E_{i})^{2}}

(G.12)

It is convenient to define
z=\sqrt{E}
, and label
Z(E^{\prime}_{i},E_{i})
by a single variable
E_{i}
. Then, the factor in (
G.11
) can be written as

\displaystyle\frac{(E+E_{1})}{(E-E_{1})^{2}}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1}{(z-z_{1})^{2}}+\frac{1}{(z+z_{1})^{2}}\right)\ .

(G.13)

This makes it clear that (
G.12
) corresponds to the correlators of a free
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-twisted chiral bosons
\phi_{+}(z_{1})
and
\phi_{-}(z_{2})

post2022universe
. The universe field theory of JT (Kodaira-Spencer theory) also includes a cubic interaction term. The correlators of the interacting theory can be repackaged as the correlators of the free theory with an extra local operator insertion on the twisted vacuum

For more details, see Appendix C of a
post2022universe
